introduced to the dogs and learning how to handle them.
In Portee, the second breeder, Whitecloud, breeds small
lap dogs for wealthy Dynasts. The dogs are bred for their
long, silky white hair, their light bird-like cries and their
unusual eyes —- which come in every bright color of the
rainbow and sometimes change color during times of high
emotion, which is to say constantly.

The dominion as a whole has become a very popular
tourist area. Most farming communities have at least one
charming bed and breakfast where visitors can fawn over
the quaint architecture and home-cooked food. Towns
ship handmade goods in from the artisans in the cities and
sell them at ludicrous prices. Several of the houses have
homes high in the foothills that stay cool even well into
the month of Resplendent Fire.


### Pangu Prefecture


Pangu is a small prefecture in the northeast of the
Blessed Isle, across the river from the Scarlet Prefecture
and the Imperial City. The prefecture is almost entirely
comprised of farming communities, and much of the food
produced there goes to feed the various legions of the
empire on their travels — only the Threshold city of the
Lap surpasses Pangu Prefecture in feeding soldiers. Time
Rain, the prefect, has had a deal going with the Empress
(through Mnemon Bata, who takes care of the day-to-day
details of the Scarlet Prefecture) for the past 15 years
whereby he keeps his prices low, and she decrees that the
legions have to buy from him. He expects to lose that deal
the moment someone new takes the Scarlet Throne and is
desperate to keep that from happening. He’s also eager to
find new contracts, particularly now that a good handful of
legions have been destroyed or disbanded. He’s just starting to realize that his reliance on his deal with the Empress
has left him vulnerable, and he has no contacts in other
prefectures to whom he can sell his farmers’ produce.

There is a small cult based out of the town of Blind
Love whose members secretly worship the Empress as a
deity. They pray for her return and do what little they can
to find out what happened to her, including sending their
children to find work as servants in the Imperial Palace and
its environs.-Each Calibration, they hold somber night-

someone deformed, blind or mentally handicapped — to
appease whatever demon or other god holds their goddess

House Cynis is a favorite topic of conversation in the
area. If there aren’t any children around, talk will often
turn to speculation about what Cynis does with its slaves.
Some of the locals believe that the house's tastes run to the
violent, and they will point to various areas as theoretical
dumping grounds for used-up bodies. Almost all of the
accounts of people seeing bodies buried are third-hand at
best. There are also stories of local disappearances, which
are always quietly attributed to House Cynis setting its
jaded eye on someone it couldn’t have.

Most locals refuse to hire their children out to the
house as servants, so Cynis usually imports servants from
other prefectures. Given its fondness for slaves, however,
Cynis uses fewer servants than most houses.


### The Scarlet Prefecture


The Scarlet Prefecture includes the Imperial City
and the land from the city to the sea. It is a small
prefecture, as such things go, but then, it’s also under the
direct control of the Empress (the Regent, currently).
The Empress appointed someone to deal with the day-today affairs of the prefecture and give her reports once a
month. The Regent has happily kept up this practice but
without the monthly reports.

The de facto prefect is currently Mnemon Bata, one
of Mnemon’s human sons. He is headstrong and doesn't
tend to side with his mother in political matters, which
is why the Empress chose him. His family is now trying to
curry favor with him, which he milks for as much worth
as he can without technically stepping outside the bounds
of his job. His favored tactic is to find things that he needs
to have done anyway and then to subtly maneuver
someone in his family into “convincing” him that he’d be
doing them a great favor by doing it. He's made a game of
accumulating as many favors as possible without doing
anything he wouldn’t have done anyway; it’s likely to get
him in trouble eventually.

The head of the Imperial Force in the Scarlet Prefecture is Nellens Birane. He was hand-chosen by the Empress
from among the human members of his house. He could
not abuse his position in. major ways without her notice
(through Mnemon Bata): But it has given Nellens some
small measure of control: ¢ power among the Dragon-

Blooded, which has a help

<!--PAGE 45-->
There is also a surfeit of con men running scams, but
they rarely stay for long. As long as they only con
patricians and peasants, they're likely to get a warning
the first time or two they get caught, but after thar, the
penalties get harsher. Once they've been caught once or
twice they tend to move on until they can be sure their
faces have been forgotten.

Much of the region’s food and cloth are imported,
although there are several farming communities between
the Imperial City and the sea. The peasants in these areas
tend to be unusually literate, and many have spent time in
the city. There is less superstition in these parts, particularly since bored Dragon-Blooded sometimes roam the
area looking for spirits that have stepped out of their places
or river dragons that need to be put down. Pretty much
everyone follows the Order of the Immaculate Dragons,
but there are few fanatics. Since the Dragon-Blooded are
seen so frequently, it’s harder to view them as distant,
powerful and mysterious entities.


### Ventus Prefecture


Ventus Prefecture spans the rugged fields and forests
of the northern shores of the Blessed Isle. The farming
communities are small and close-knit and sell any extra

produce to Chanos for the Ragara legions. They raise hardy
crops resistant to the moderate cold spells the area receives
and are particularly well known for their sweet-tart apples.

_. Many. of the foothills are forested, and several
houses have hunting lodges in the area, including Ragara,
V'neef, Cynis and Mnemon. There is a fair population
of bears, a few-hybroc in the higher regions and some of
the smaller great cats.

The area is also a source of various substances used by
sorcerers, alchemists, healers, pharmacists and chemists.
Healing herbs of all kinds are found in the forests and on
the heights of the hills; many of the locals are named after
the herbs that they raise or collect. Sulfur, mercury, coal
and other substances are mined or otherwise collected and
shipped out. Ventus has laws against processing these
substances into usable form within the bounds of the
prefecture, as the by-products would destroy many of the
healing herbs that are so valuable.

Ventus has only one major city, Bright Obelisk,
named for the gleaming silvery monument erected in the
middle of the city by its founders several centuries ago.
Even this city is comparatively small; apart from Dynasts
visiting their hunting lodges and the occasional caravan,
few people travel through Ventus. The prefect of Ventus,
Sage Lake, spends most of her time drinking herself into
a stupor. She leaves most of the details of government in
the hands of the governor of Bright Obelisk, Holly, a
quiet but capable woman descended from both human
and Mountain Folk stock.

The streets of Bright Obelisk are of packed dirt and are
kept surprisingly clean. They also wander confusingly in
odd directions, and finding your way through the city if you
haven't lived there for several years can be difficult at best.
Some people have lived in the city all their lives and still
only know their way around certain neighborhoods.

The populace of Ventus is comparatively quiet and
well mannered. Most are happy with their lot and eventempered. Some speculate that this is due to a noticeable
infusion of Mountain Folk blood throughout the region.
Few travel unless they have specific reason to do so, but

‘there is no stigma attached to traveling.

### The Jhousand Scales


Officially, there is nothing in the Realm known as
the Thousand Scales. The bureaucracy that drives the
engine of empire is officially known as the Imperial
Service, a massive web of ministries, departments, councils and advisory committees that serves to perpetuate all
of the functions of empire while carefully obscuring those
functions from the imperial citizenry.

The Imperial Service itself is broken into three
branches: the legions, the Magistracy and the All-Seeing
Eye, as well as innumerable bureaucratic ministries that
are officially set underneath the magistrates. Each performs a vital function in perpetuating the Realm, and each
branch is jealous and fearful of the other two. Until
recently, all three worked in relative harmony, as the hand
of the Empress balanced their rivalries, but her absence has
propelled the magistrates — or more accurately, their
putative subordinates, the ministers — into a position of
greater authority at the expense of the other two.


### The Magistrates


The true magistrates of the Realm are few and far
between. Appointed by the Empress herself, they are
plenipotentiaries without portfolio. Their task is to ensure the continued smooth operation of the Imperial
Bureaucracy in all its guises, and they walk to and fro in
the land and up and down it performing their tasks as they
see fit. The magistrates are troubleshooters, authorized to
deal with situations ranging from rampaging beasts to
murder investigations. With the authorization of the
Empress behind him, a magistrate could confiscate any
property, execute any sentence and, in general, exercise
his will and powers as he saw fit. Limited to the Dragon-
Blooded and often drawn from the criminal ranks,
magistrates longserved as the visible manifestation of the
Empress’. will. (as opposed to the All-Seeing Eye, the
invisible manifestation of same). Withdut the Empress i in
placé, the magistrates technically now report tonoone at
all and are a law unto themselves.


### The Magistracy of Hares


In reality, the magistrates have lost their
all-powerful protector, and many magistrates
find themselves harried or hunted by those
whom they have judged against in the past. The
current state of affairs has been dubbed “the
Magistracy of Hares” by one wit, for never
before have so many of the Empress’ favorite
toys been forced to seek shelter, all at once.

perhaps a warrior or: By

Ministers, on the other hand, are appointed officials
who serve in the actual Imperial Bureaucracy. They are the
cogs that turn the wheels of empire, and in the popular
mind, they are confused with the roving magistrates.
Drawn from the ranks of mortals as well as Dragon-
Blooded, the ministers have their powers severely proscribed
by law. However, as the number of tasks associated with
the maintenance of the Realm is huge, the ministers’
number is legion.

There is a natural hatred for the unfettered magistrates among the ministers and a natural contempt for the
deskbound clerks of the ministries among the magistrates.
However, in recent years, the ministers have gained the
upper hand. Using the laws and regulations of the Realm
as a noose, they have slowly drawn their powers tight
around the once-proud magistrates and seem intent on
either harnessing them or grinding them into dust.

Duties AND Honors

The job of a magistrate is to defend the Realm.
Whether she does so by organizing hunts to eliminate
beasts plaguing imperial lands or by sitting in judgment
on a murderer makes no difference. Each task the
magistrate performs is for the good of the Realm, either
enforcing its laws, protecting its citizens or punishing its
recidivist inhabitants.

Of no fixed abode, magistrates wander the land
waiting for trouble to find them. News of a magistrate’s
appearance in an area travels like wildfire, and if there is
anything in the region that demands the magistrate’s
attention, runners will be sent to find her posthaste.
Interestingly, the Empress has forbid her magistrates
from acquiring wealth, so that they might be the poorest
inhabitants of the Realm and thus its humblest. However, since all citizens are duty-bound (and wise enough)
to accede to any requests a magistrate makes, the functional definition of poverty that magistrates enjoy isa bit
different from that afflicting the average peasant. However, the imperial strictures on the position do make

- Magistrates hard to bribe; when they can have anything

they want for the askin but cannot carry any wealth with

Officially, they nee

between fast

e by the Capen

<!--PAGE 47-->
been spared thus — and well aware of the price they will
pay if they lapse into former habits — the magistrates
pledge their considerable skills to the service of the
Realm. Almost uniform among them is a deep personal
loyalty to the Empress herself, which is almost unheard of
in the Imperial Service.

Less prominent, however, are the servants known as
archons, who are to the magistrates what the magistrates
are to the Empress. Like their superiors, they are often
recruited from the ranks of the most dangerous criminals
and promising young revolutionaries, and the vast majority would have faced the executioner’s axe had they not
been offered the chance to serve the Realm instead. They
are bound to obey the orders of the magistrates they serve
and can bring a vast array of skills to bear on any problem
that might vex their masters. Finding evidence, locating
witnesses (and convincing them to testify), spying and, if
necessary, eliminating those whose actions the magistrate judges to be detrimental to the Realm — these are
just a few of the duties laid at an archon’s feet. The
number of archons attached to a given magistrate varies;
inexperienced magistrates usually have perhaps three
archons at most, while those who have been at the trade
for years can have as many as a dozen. This is not to say
that a senior magistrate is surrounded by a traveling
horde of archons. Rather, a magistrate in position to
support a number of archons will have them working solo

or in small teams, moving about the countryside on a
variety of vital assignments.

Each archon’s life is bound up with the life of the
magistrate he serves, for when the magistrate’s life
ends, so does that of the archon. While this penalty is
rarely invoked, due to the’simple fact that most magistrates are Dragon-Blooded and most archons aren’r,
itis still a powerful incentive for archons to see to their
masters’ well-being.


### Common Tasks Performed by Magistrates


Magistrates will be sent for to perform a wide variety
of tasks. The most important include:

® Sitting in Judgment — Magistrates often hear
suits or are petitioned to serve as judges in cases of
murder and other heinous crimes, No Dynast wants to
take a case before an actual judge because all the judges
are loyal to various Great Houses. Magistrates also
frequently serve as an amicus curiae, when they feel the
local judge has the situation well in hand but want to
legitimize the proceedings.

© Hunting the Beast — Many of the oldest and bestloved children’s stories of the Realm revolve around this
theme: Beast terrorizes village, magistrate hears of the
village’s distress, magistrate arrives and dispatches the
beast. In these dark times, the magistrate is just as likely to
gather a posse as to attempt to beard the creature in itsden,

<!--PAGE 48-->
alone, but the end result is generally the same. In the past,
beasts were generally regarded as just that — animals or
monsters who'd decided to prey on the Empress’ subjects.
However, of late, the definition has expanded to include
lawless men, infestations of undead, ambitious Fair Folk
and, most recently, incautious Anathema.

® Rooting Out Corruption — When suspicion grows
that a satrap, prefect or minister is unduly corrupt, it is the
magistrates who are called in to investigate the matter.
Woe betide any whom they find guilty.

*Punishing Bandits — Oftentimes, it is easier and
faster to get a magistrate on the scene ina region terrorized
by bandits. Once the magistrate arrives, however, it is the
bandits who become the hunted. In most cases, they are
eradicated within the week.

*Dealing with Unquiet Spirits — If a spirit or,
worse yet, a ghost is refusing to behave, generally, a
magistrate is called for. The expectation is that the
magistrate will demonstrate to the spirit or ghost the folly
of its ways and resolve the problem forever. If necessary,
the magistrate will destroy the offending entity, though
this is generally held as a last resort. In these situations,
members of the Immaculate Order are also often called
in, and there is a roughly friendly working alliance
between the monks and the magistrates. At such times,
the Immaculates politely bow to the civil authority of the
magistrates, who then politely requests the monks’ assistance. In this manner, the maximum amount of expertise
and force can be brought to bear on the situation while
ail of the proprieties are observed.


### Above the Law


No law of the land, save those applying to treason,
applies to magistrates. They have carte blanche to do as
they see fit in defense of the Realm, whether that be
confiscating land, shattering a crystal vase or ordering an
execution. In theory, their power has no limits except the
Empress’ direct opposition to one of their decrees. In
reality, they are strongly encouraged to exercise restraint.
When possible, they will work through others to enforce
their judgment — for example, hiring mercenaries to rida
region of a marauding pack of beasts or giving a cook an
addictive powder to mix into the wine of an overly ambitious landowner. Here is where the archons earn their pay
-—and their stays of execution.

Moré:rarely does a magistrate hand down overly harsh

some element of the case is so far beyond the bounds of the
laws of the Realm that only raw power can resolve it.


### Recruitment


Before her disappearance, the Empress selected and
initiated each magistrate personally. Now, there is no way
to restock the dwindling ranks of the magistrates, as noone
has the authority to appoint new ones. This factor, more
than any other, has speeded the ascent to prominence of
the ministers. Beneath imperial notice most of the time,
they have swarmed like ants, and their numbers are telling.


### Training


There is no formal training for magistrates. They are
merely given their badges of office, instructed in their
duties and given a suggestion that they should learn the
laws of the Realm to the best of their ability. Left unspoken
is the knowledge that the older magistrates often shadow
the younger ones, to assess their performance and provide
assistance, as well as to test them.

Magistrates also have no set routes. Most will make
something of a home in a single town, often a provincial
capital, but a fair number live on the road. Occasionally,
their paths cross by design, occasionally, by accident.
Once every five years, a magistrate is required to report to
the Imperial City to detail her deeds and judgments. At
this time, the records kept by her associated scribe (if she
has one) are tallied and pored over, and the magistrate is
commended or rebuked. Only twice has a magistrate been
stripped of rank for poor performance in the field, and such
a horrific event has not occurred in 170 years.

With the disappearance of the Scarlet Empress, magistrates have become notably reluctant to answer the five
year summons for fear of being ambushed or stripped of
their power by the Regent. Instead, they continue to
function in the far reaches of the Realm as best they can,
praying for the Empress’ return and calling on favors from
those who have valued their judgments in the past.


### The Ministers


There are well over 100 different bureaus, departments, ministries and. offic s, within the Thousand Scales,
each rigidly defined asto what it oversees. They range from
the minuscule (The Of ce:

the mundane (The Rigt
of Weights and Meas.

<!--PAGE 49-->

### Ministries of the Thousand Scales


It’s all well and good to say that the Thousand Scales have many powerful ministries in
them, but what are these ministries? Certainly,
there are many insignificant ministries, but what
are the really important ones, the ones that
everyone knows?

These are the Realm’s most important ministries:

The Humble and Honest Assessors of the
Imperial Tax (Tax Collection): An extremely
important and (formerly) heavily policed ministry, the Humble and Honest Assessors both
assessed the imperial tax and collected it. Estates
were assessed accurately, while regions of peasants were slapped with a flat tax that the tax
assessor had to make. The pressure for corruption
at all levels was countered by the constant scrutiny of the All-Seeing Eye. With the Eye blinded
by the disappearance of the Empress, the local tax
collectors have gotten very generous to the Great
Houses and very oppressive to the average citizen. Keep in mind that this isn’t just assessment
of the value of the lease on a give tract, but also
taxes on imports, exports, licenses for taxable
items such as grain-mills, use taxes on roads and
bridges and the like.

Cathak Curuk rules the Honest Assessors.
Though he is a man of great business acumen, the
entire structure of the Realm has leagued against
him. He survives by the support of Bal Keraz and
his own willingness to bow to inevitability. Curuk’s
bureau has shrunken in the last five years and
shows no sign of ceasing to dwindle.

The Imperial Treasury (The Treasury): The
Assessors have their power of tax assessment
blocked by the fact that they have no authority to
spend a single penny of the money they collect. It
must all be remitted to the Treasury, which authorizes the Assessors’ expenditures. These tax
revenues, along with the tribute of the subject
princes, formed the bulk of the Empress’ income.
Much of this was immediately doled back out in
the state subsidy to the Dynastic houses or to pay
for the upkeep of the Realm, but there is little
doubt that the Empress was the most fabulously
wealthy being in Creation. Those who tended
her fortune were her most loyal and trusted servants, and they have several almost-impregnable
treasure Manses guarded by demons and the
Realm’s most elite troops. The Realm’s income
may have dried up, but the current fortune won’t
be gone any time soon.

In addition to being the world’s wealthiest
woman, the Empress was also the world’s largest
mercantile bank, and she bonded and financed
much of the world’s trade. Since her disappearance, the Treasury has been avoiding risky
commercial and development loans, and many
merchants have been forced to deal with the
Guild at usurious terms. Her favorable terms on
loans to Dynastic adventures are part of what kept
the Realm dynamic over many centuries.

The Treasury is ruled with an iron fist by Bal
Keraz, the semi-hereditary holder of the post.
One-hundred and cighty years old and as ruthless
as his legendary father Kesif, Keraz is as powerful as
the leader of most Great Houses. Holding the
purse for most of the ministries, he commands
loyalty throughout the Thousand Scales.

The Wise and Knowledgeable Advisors of
Foreign Tributaries (Foreign Office): The Wise...:
and Knowledgeable Advisors are the satraps and
their various advisors and staff. Each satrap is a
Dynast from one of the Great Houses who oversees the collection and remittance of a kingdom’s
tribute to the Empress. In theory, it’s a trivial task.
In reality, it’s a challenging combination of power
politics, military leadership and managerial skills.
Overseeing a tributary state successfully is difficult, keeping it free cnough that it can grow and
defend itself and yet still weak enough it doesn’t
dare test the limits of imperial control. Though
the actual satrap is a political appointee, most of
his staff are career bureaucrats. The Foreign Office
works closely with the legions and has been disastrously on the rocks since the army’s gutting. The
satraps are attempting to retain control of the
tributaries, but without a credible army, this is
proving difficult. The lands of those satraps who
already had private armies (a not-uncommon situation) have, in some cases, become virtually
independent principalities.

The Knowledgeable Advisors are run by Ledaal
Arnis. Despite the name, she has little to do with
the Great House. Since her grandmother took the
post almost 400 years ago, Arnis’ line has managed
itself separately from the Great House of the
Empress’ fourth child. Arnis is currently in a
terrible situation — without the Empress, her
advice on the satrapies is going unheeded, her
bureaucracy is being subverted, and more than
half the Realm’s income has dried up. The Deliberative refuses to do anything, and the satraps
have ceased to respond to her demands. All she
can do it watch her bureau die.

<!--PAGE 50-->
Honorable and Humble Caretakers of the
Common Folk (Home Office): Just as the satrap
oversees and accepts tribute from a satrapy, so does
the prefect oversee the well-being and tax collection from the Empress subjects on the Blessed Isle.
The prefects were always more closely monitored
than the satraps, and the prefectural taxes formed
the bedrock of the Empress’ budget. Now, the
prefectural establishments are tools of the Great
Houses, as likely as not feuding — sometimes to
the point of bloodshed — with the Humble and
Honest Assessors, who are as likely as not in the
pocket of a different house. Currently, the houses
and their cronies receive about 80 percent of
domestic tax receipts.

The chief of the Caretakers, Sesus Sereda, was
assassinated several years ago by parties unknown,
and the Deliberative has not yet seen fit to appoint
a successor, despite several attempts by the Regent
to name one. This has actually been a benefit to
the ministry, as Cathak Curuk and Bal Keraz have
been able to influence it to some degree and keep
the empire from withering totally.

The Infallible Conveyors of Official Messages and Heartfelt Expressions (Imperial Post):
The Imperial Post is one of the empire’s most
enduring bureaucracies. Without communications,
the legions, the satrapies, the far-flung military
and the trading empire —all would be impossible.

with each imperial decree that modifies the terms of
imperial service, careful corrections are made. Only the
most highly qualified and elegant scribes of the Thousand Scales are permitted to inscribe even the smallest
change-in the Registry. The penalty for a single misplaced brushstroke is blinding by hot irons, though this
has only been invoked once in the past l, 000 years.

### Interpreting the Registry

None wish to be haunted centuries later by a dubious (or

### The Process of Interpretation


When a dispute arises betweerf two or more ministries, arbitration can be called for from the Keeper of the
Registry — if all parties agree. This generally happens
only when the rival ministries are well matched; otherwise, the stronger generally overwhelms the weaker. An
interpreter is dispatched and, upon arriving, hears the
arguments from all concerned parties. She then deliberates fora period of not longer than a fortnight and not less
than three days, calling additional witnesses into private
discussion and otherwise assessing the evidence and the
writings of the Registry. The pronouncement, when it is
made, is final and binding, and a runner is dispatched to
the Imperial City with news of the decision. The interpreter then continues on her way; by tradition, she must
leave the city by nightfall on the day her decision is
announced, to prevent her from being rewarded by the
winning side. Furthermore, no arbiter may render judgment twice in the same city in a given year, lest the
groundwork for a favorable decision at a later date be laid
during an earlier, less important visit.

That being said, attempts to bribe interpreters of
the Registry are manifold and clever. Often, a minister
thinking of calling for an interpretation will dispatch
runners to ply all of the arbiters in the area with lavish
food and rich gifts, to kindly dispose them toward his
point of view long before their services are required.
During residence in a city, arbiters are often gifted
with food, gems, jade, concubines, silks and other
bribes — all provided not by the interested parties, but
by friends, allies and those seeking to curry favor with
the arbiters. Sometimes, a litigant is in a position to do
something for a relative or favorite of the arbitrator
and Jets this fact be subtly known. The period of

### Advancement


Advancement among the ministers is a question of
merit, timing, politicking and luck. Each year, every
member of the Imperial Service takes the Nine Rigorous
Examinations for her rank, and those scoring the highest
are selected for advancement — if any open positions can
be found. If there are no openings in a particularly
exceptional civil servant’s branch-of the government,
she simply bides her time until an opening does develop
— at least, in theory.

In reality, the process of promotion in the Imperial
Service is much more complicated than that, an elegant
dance of favors, alliances and murder. While a bureaucrat’s
marks on the Nine Rigorous Examinations are part of the
process by which advancement is doled out, they are only
a part — and not always the most important one.

"THE GENTLE ART OF ASSASSINATION

A time-honored technique for rising in the ranks is
the genteel and artistic removal of one’s superiors through
foul play. While murder of one’s superiors and rivals is. station in.

officially frowned upon, it is as much an aspect of life
among the Thousand Scales as breathing. There are only
so many high-level posts, and most of them are held by
Dtagon-Blooded. The rate at which the pyramid of power
narrows for mortal bureaucrats is alarmingly steep. Thus,
competition for choice offices is fierce, and the number of
voluntary retireménts and deaths from natural causes each
year is staggeringly small. Therefore, assassination becomes the only option for the ambitious bureaucrat.

There are unwritten rules to the art of murder in the
Imperial Service. Doing the deed oneself is considered
tacky and boorish and costs the murderer status in the eyes
of others. After all, it is perfectly acceptable to eliminate
a rival, but to allow oneself to be linked directly to the
crime is to demonstrate that one is lacking in the necessary
finesse to assist in running the imperial government.

Poisoning is likewise regarded déclassé, though a
sufficiently clever application— on the skin of the victim’s
concubine’s breasts, for example, or on the pen he intended to use to commission one’s own murder —- often
wins admiration. In fact, assassination has become something of a spectator sport within the Imperial Service, and
particularly clever, subtle and vicious murders win admiration for the ones who authored them. Indeed, it is best,
if one plans on eliminating a rival or superior, to plan the
murder so as to allow the slightest hint of suspicion — but
no more than that, and no proof —tosettle upon one’s self,
so that one may plausibly deny involvement to imperial
investigators, yet still reap the benefits of having one’s
peers know exactly how dangerous one is.


### The Simple Art of Conspiring


Murder among the Thousand Scales is a serious business. Members of the Imperial Bureaucracy pride themselves
on the sheer purposefulness of their assassinations. Let the
scions of the Great Houses fight duels over matters of
honor or temper; the Thousand Scales kill only with
deliberation and malice well aforethought.

_ It is extremely rare for a member of the Imperial
Service to kill in the heat of the moment. Instead, assassi-

nations within the bureaucracy are usually planned months,
oa if not years, inadvance. Rare, too, is the assassination that

### Failure


Not every attempt to remove arival ends in
success. Many fail to do more than annoy their
targets, and some simply amuse. Even the cleverest attempt can go awry, assuming the target
is sufficiently wary — and anyone in the Impetial Service who is worth assassinating has long
since learned how to be wary.

Once an attempted assassination is foiled,
the would-be target has a myriad of choices as
to how to respond. Some tend toward instant
retribution, serving notice that they are not
to be trifled with. Such approaches tend to
discourage repeated assassination attempts,
at least for a while.

Others prefer to wait days, months or even
years before extracting their vengeance. A steady
stream of reminders to the would-be assassin
during that time lets him know that he has not,
in fact, escaped and will pay for his crimes...
eventually. Some elder bureaucrats enjoy the
torment they can inflict with the waiting far
more than the actual moment of revenge.

If an attempt is particularly poor, the intended target may not even acknowledge it ever
existed. This is a supreme insult, and it often
drives rash, young would-be killers to perform
foolish deeds. Said deeds, including challenging detractors to duels, attempting more hasty
assassinations and so on, generally get their
perpetrators killed sooner rather than later, but
the blood is not on the original target’s hands.

And finally, on rare occasions, an intended target is so impressed by her would-be
killer’s cunning and ambition that she seeks
out the putative assassin as a protégé. After
all, young talent like that should never be
allowed to go to waste.

is usually accomplished by the bestowal of lavish or clever

On the other hand, there are times when an assassin

### The Thirty That Survive


The Empress once commanded as many as 81 full
legions, each with 5,000 front-line soldiers and half that
many skirmishers and auxiliaries, all of whom would kill or
die at her whispered command. In these fallen days,
however, there are but 30 legions remaining, plus the
infamous — and not entirely beloved — Vermilion Legion. Further, few of those legions currently fight at full
strength, as the decline in the legions’ prestige and the lack
of proper financial support have combined to render a
career in ranks a less-than-appealing prospect:

THOSE THAT Dip Not

In the wake of the Empress’ disappearance,
seven full legions were disbanded, though technically one rebelled before it could be dissolved.
Soldiers of the disbanded legions were mostly
absorbed into the standing legions to bring
them up to fighting strength, and their officers
were either transferred or forcibly retired. Some
few officers took their troops with them and
became mercenary commanders, working in
the Scavenger Lands and anywhere else bloody
constraint reared its head. Others simply vanished, leading more paranoid members of the
Deliberative to conclude that they are still out
there, biding their time and waiting to extract
vengeance on the Realm that abandoned them.
The usual response to this theory is disdain and
mockery of its author as a weak-livered worrywart. Still, there are a surprising number of
soldiers from those vanished legions that no one
~~ not even the magistrates — can account for.

Afterthe Empress’ disappearance, imperial funding to

support the legions dried up. More importantly, the cen- ‘
tral intelligence that directed them~as “harnessed :

components of a single force was gone as well, and. there

mand'left in place. Unprotected by the Empress’ guiding
intelligence and cut off by the lack of a formal-di
continued financial support, the legions:

was-no contingency plan, no emergency chain of com- ;

from the Blessed Isle and difficult to infiltrate. And the
Great Houses simply ‘disliked the fact that there was a
military force beholden to a power greater than themselves, that could bring any one of them to heel as easily as
a child might snap his fingers. And so, between the three,
a plan was devised to subjugate the legions as soon as the
lead conspirators overcame their fear of the Empress’
imminent return long enough to act.

At the Celebration of the Seven Shattered Helms,
when the elite troops of each legion traditionally paraded
past the throne of the Empress for her approval and
largesse, the trap was sprung. The Esteemed Minister of
the Imperial Treasury sorrowfully informed the generals
that no funds had been allocated for their continued
support by the Deliberative and that unless an alternate
source of funding could be found, the legions themselves
would have to be disbanded. After all, it was not like they
were truly necessary any more — surely the mere memoties of the Realm’s wrath would be enough to deter any
foolish barbarians. And in case the generals had entertained any foolish thoughts about perhaps using the
troops at hand to remonstrate against the decision, there
were several thousand private troops and dozens of sorcerers — all loyal to the Great Houses and not under any
general’s command — positioned outside the Great Square
wherein the legions’ elite units were currently assembled.
However, if the generals might consent to listen toa plan
proposed by the masters of those Great Houses, perhaps
some accommodation could be reached?

And so, the legions were brought under the control of
their “sponsors,” with each Great House taking one or
more legions under its wing. The purges that followed were
swift and deliberate, as the legions’ officer corps were
tested for loyalty to their new masters, and those who were
found wanting (or unwilling to put an individual house’s
needs before those of the Realm entire) were either dismissed or dealt with. In their place were raised scions of the
Great Houses and their allies, dependable women and men
whose loyalty was assured and whose ambitions were for
house and self, not the Realm

And so, a few short years ‘after the disappearance of

the Empress, the legions have become the. tools of the

<!--PAGE 56-->
Rirecgs

alarming rate, and there are not enough talented
political appointees to take their places. With inferior
command in the field, casualties are rising, and even
the dullest peasant is starting to learn that a long, dull
life roiling in the fields is preferable to a short, terrifying one in the legions.


### Structure and Organization


When ar full strength, each legion consists of 5,000
infantry, with both women and men serving in ranks.
The legions employ no cavalry, as the logistics of tending
the steeds are an insurmountable obstacle in legion
deployment. Instead, when a legion goes into the field,
the local potentate or satrap’s house cavalry is co-opted,
or less frequently, irregulars are recruited from the local
population. Similarly, the legions do not travel with siege
engines, though there are anywhere from a half-dozen to
30 fangs’ worth of engineers attached to each legion to
construct such engines on-site.

Legions also have associated talons of archers or
slingers, usually amounting to half the legion’s strength,
and working at half-pay. Such soldiers are lightly armored and are not considered to be a true part of the
legion itself. These troops are generally considered
expendable by their commanding officers, and the soldiers themselves are well aware of it.

Each legion also travels with a baggage and supply
train, tended by slaves. Commoners are discouraged from
traveling in a legion’s wake, though many merchants have
grown rich outside of Guild strictures by paying visits to
legion encampments. The baggage train is guarded by a
single wing under the command of the quartermaster,
whose rank is second only to the legion’s commander.
Ironically, slaves in military service are often treated better
than those in domestic service, as no general wishes to
return from battle to find that her baggage handlers have
tun off or, worse yet, overwhelmed their guards and looted
the baggage train. Officers commanding at least a talon
generally have at least one personal slave as well, and a
general is liable to have a small household (including a
scribe/secretary, a dresser and.a majordomo) in tow.

With the baggage train also comes the legion’s
support: smiths, armorers, fletchers (if there are archers
present), coopers, cooks and so forth. Usually, the
slaves and laborers can equal up to a quarter of a legion’s
strength in additional manpower, though it is considered a sign of considerable disgrace if a general is forced
to arm his support.

Each legion is commanded by a single commanding
general. The 10 dragons under her command are led by
colonels, with the chain of command (should the commanding general be incapacitated or the dragons

<!--PAGE 57-->
operating independently of the legion) determined by
seniority. Commanding generals tend to attract subordinates of similar aspect, and an Air-aspected Exalt on
an Earth-aspected general’s staff has little hope of advancement. Generally, four dragons are composed of
heavy infantry, and six of medium infantry, and there
are between four and six loosely organized dragons of
skirmishers and missile troops. .

This unwieldy number of subunits is made manageable by the use of flights, ad hoc formations of dragons put
together to accomplish a given task. A legion in the field
normally uses three dragons of heavy infantry irone flight,
two medium infantry dragons and a dragon’s worth of
skirmishers. The last heavy infantry dragon and the remaining skirmishers are kept under the control of the
legion commander for use as a reserve. The most senior
dragon commander in the flight leads it.

Beneath each dragon’s colonel are a pair of wings,
each commanded by a major. A wing comprises two talons,
and each talon answers to a captain. Talons are broken up
into five scales, each the responsibility ofa lieutenant, and
at the bottom of the command structure are individual
fangs, under the authority of non-commissioned sergeants.
Furthermore, each officer ranked major or higher has at
least one subaltern attached to her, so that he might learn
the proper conduct of an officer before being entrusted
with so much as a single fang.


### Names and Standards


Each unit has a numerical designation, i.e. “10th
Legion, Second Dragon, First Wing, First Talon, Fourth
Scale, Second Fang.” In the field, however, this is cut
down to the smallest unique designation possible; odds
are that when an officer shouts out “Third and Fourth
Fangs, advance!” everyone concerned will know whom
she’s talking about. Most units also have nicknames.
These are related to incidents in the unit's history. Often,
they're named after famous battles the unit took part in,
legendary commanders or spectacular incidents. Shivash's
Talon, for example, is named after a-lieutenant who
carved ‘his way through hundreds of barbarians to extricate his soldiers from a trap he had led them into; while ©
i Gorges Talon is named after a forced march that the
nituridertook to relieve a besieged watchtowér. No unit

“ever names itself; the name must be -bestowed from

### Rank


‘The Dragon-Blooded, except under spectacular circumstances, do not enlist in the ranks. On certain
occasions, a scion of one of the Great Houses has been
known to join up as a common soldier specifically to
thumb her nose at a hated parent, cousin or rival, but
such gestures are regarded as exactly that — gestures, not
to be taken seriously.

Instead, the Dragon-Blooded comprise most of the
legions’ senior officer corps. It is possible for an unExalted
soldier to achieve an officer’s rank, whether by being a
patrician or unExalted Dynast or else through spectacular
battlefield performance, but these individuals rarely rise
above the rank of major. The vast majority of the Realm’s
commanders are Terrestrial Exalted and are quick to
remind any challengers or naysayers of that fact. In fact,
many officers are Exalted not of a Dynastic family, sponsored into the legions by the Empress (now the Regent)
and using them as a way to gain social respectability.


### The Soldiers


The soldiers of the legions are of mixed race, mixed
gender and uniform competence. A legion’s history of
postings is written in its troops, as most recruit wherever
they go to fill the ranks of the fallen. However, once a
soldier enters the ranks, he leaves his previous nationality,
city allegiance and so forth behind. Once you are of the
legions, nothing more need be said. Most troops enter the
ranks as slingers or archers, are trained there and graduate
into the actual legion as other troops die or retire.

OrFicers’ Duties

In the field, the responsibilities of an officer of one of

‘the legions are straightforward, if not simple: Drill the
troops, command the troops, and obey orders from one’s
superiors. In theory, at least, chain of command is strict,
though there are always instances of experienced superior
“officers handling well-connected juniors with kid gloves
“for fear of political repercussions. No general, no matter
“howswell respected, has the time or inclination to explain
“toa senior mber. of the. Dynasty precisely why her

upon the legion, an attempt has been made to instill this
respect for the chain of command in the newer officers,
though many of the recent political appointees seem to

However, no army spends all of its time in the field,
and an officer's duties extend far beyond the campground
and battlefield. Officers are also expected to recruit —
with permission of the local magnate, of course — as well
as making something of an appearance in local society
when not actively campaigning. Despite the legions’ recent decline in prestige, an upwardly mobile officer is still
quite a catch for marriage or assignation. During months
when no campaigning takes place, an eligible officer is
liable to be inundated with invitations to tea, concerts,
demonstrations of gardening techniques and so forth, all in
the interest of producing a sufficiently honeyed lure.

Even when not on the prowl for — or avoiding —
conjugal alliances, an officer is expected to represent the
Realm in social situations. Dignity, professionalism and a
steadfast refusal to be drawn into discussions of internal
politics are the hallmarks of legion service under those
circumstances, whether it be a state dinner with a local
governor or a hastily arranged tour of a base camp for a
magistrate who “just happened to be in the area.” The
legions’ mandate, after all, is to deal with threats to the
Realm romoutside its borders, and an officer who is drawn

ceive ¢ the better

"TACTICS AND REGIMEN

The Thousand Correct Actions of the Upright Soldier
details within its pages the proper response to any situation, from making camp to facing a vastly superior foe with
no archers. Each officer carries with her a version —
abridged in the case of junior officers, complete in the case
of senior — and is expected to study it so that proper
responses to all tactical situations can be learned, memotized and prepared for use.

If a commander follows the Thousand Correct Actions, he stops his troops on the march well before
nightfall so that they can properly entrench and ensure
supplies of clean water before sleep. He sets pickets for
when the soldiers under his command are not advancing,
and sends out skirmishers when they are. He sees to their
supply, their morale, their training and their obedience,
all with the implicit understanding that he will have no
qualms about ordering them to their deaths should it be
in the best interests of the legion as a whole. Furthermore, the Thousand Correct Actions also contains a
wealth of small-unit tactics, ranging from espionage to
infiltration to ambush to house-to-house fighting. Officers who heed the tenets of the Thousand Correct Actions
generally have healthy, well-trained and loyal troops. In
part, this is because one of the first rules laid down within
the book’s pages is the incontrovertible fact that, while
soldiers may be sacrificed for the good of the legion as a
whole, they should never simply be thrown away. Doing
so, the Thousand Correct Actions teaches, weakens the
legion as a whole and undermines the resolve of those
soldiers who have not yet been discarded. Respect for this
concise manual of all things military has declined of late,
as the political appointees promoted into command
follow their own often flawed instincts and not the tried
and true lessons of this ancient field manual.


### The Great Mission


The legions’ orders are simple: to obey the commands of the Empress or, in her absence, those of the
Regent (and through him, the Deliberative). The rest is
just details. In theory, if tomorrow the Regent called for
the legions to raze Arjuf. to. the ground, they would dos so
or die, to the man, trying. : ;

that their loyalty to the legion is demonstrated and vouched
for. Skill and experience are valued in the Red-Piss Legion,
notone’spriorservice or political history. As the saying goes,
“Vermilion blood washes away the past.”

Vermilion officers are generally more solicitous of
their troops’ lives than their counterparts in other legions,

in part, perhaps, because they spend so much time
training those troops to be soldiers. Then again, it may
well be because the officers of the Vermilion Legion are
well aware that their men are the only real support they
have and that squandering them is foolhardy.

Holding a commission in the Vermilion Legion is ©
a double-edged sword politically. While one is attached
to the Red-Piss Legion, one is considered disgraced, a
failure — untouchable in polite company and likely to
break the china andsteal the silverat fancy dress parties.
It is socially acceptable for officers of other legions, not
to mention any other Dragon-Blooded with the merest
pretensions of military prowess, to sneer at the work of
the Vermilion Legion and its officers and dismiss it as
“not real soldiering.” However, on those rare occasions
when a former officer of the Red-Piss Legion reappears’ -in polite society, her service suddenly becomes a benefit, a badge of honor marking her as worthy of awe.
After all, if she could endure service in the Vermilion
Legion, who knows what else she might be capable of?

<!--PAGE 60-->
fist of the legions to smash the impudent rebels who have
dared to set foot on the soil of imperial tributaries, the
likelihood of the legions going on the offensive are
minimal. Instead, they are deployed to support tottering
allies, to curn back barbarian incursions that strike too
deep into valued territory and, most importantly, to
maneuver around the Blessed Isle seeking advantage over
the troops of other houses.

With each passing day, the Great Houses of the
Dynasty have more and more say in where the legions
are deployed. They pay for the posting, and so, the
legions go where the Great Houses want them. Those
members of the Deliberative who stand outside the
Great Houses see and are alarmed by this trend and are
frantically scrambling.to prevent the legions from becoming the Dynasty’s private armies. However, even
with the help of many of the commanders in the field,
their efforts may be too little and far too late.

THe ALL-SEEING EYE

The All-Seeing Eye is, at once, the secret service and
the last line of defense of the Realm, and it is crumbling.
A collection of spies, assassins, observers and informants,
it has long been the invisible whip thar the Empress held
over her closest subjects. So skilled were — and are — the
women and men of the All-Seeing Eye that the merest
whisper of their presence was often enough to dissolve
conspiracy or drive corrupt officials to confess their
iniquities. There are innumerable tavern tales of the
assassins in the Eye’s service, whose touch is death and
whose gaze is a curse. Fear of the Eye’s attention was an
effective rein on would-be conspirators, rebels and other
riffraff, and ensured that the other branches of the Thousand Scales were able to operate smoothly. Toiling in
anonymity, the operatives of the All-Seeing Eye were
feared, envied and richly rewarded.

Success, however, brings with it enemies, enemies
who were all too happy to see the All-Seeing Eye reined
in once the Empress’ protection vanished. While the
magistrates and the Great Houses of the Dynasty were
quite happy. to enlist the support of the visible aspects of
hamstring the legions, they were quick to
immediately thereafter. Known Eye agents
were discredited, exposed and, in some instances, killed.

### Recruitment: Coming Under the Eye


Unlike the other two branches of the Thousand
Scales, the All-Seeing Eye chooses its members. Sometimes, they are soldiers or officers in the legions. Other
times, they are talented clerks hoping for a ministerial
position someday. Still others are drawn from the ranks
of the patricians or even the Dynasty, and the Eye does
not hesitate to use peasants, slaves and even Immaculate monks in its efforts.

In certain ways, the Eye is the most egalitarian
organization in the Realm, as in its ranks, Sidereal
Exalted assassins toil side by side with guttersnipes and
trustworthy slaves, each chosen and rewarded according to skill and usefulness. Perhaps the largest segment
of the Eye’s agents, however, are Dragon-Blooded,
though a surprisingly high number are taken from the
ranks of non-Dynast s or Iselsi fugitives. This

<!--PAGE 61-->
"THE OPERATIONS OF THE EYE

The All-Seeing Eye's function, if it can be said to
have a single one, is to ensure the continued existence
of the Realm. Beneath that rubric, multiple other
duties crowd for space, resources and attention. However, when resources are scarce, certain duties take

“precedence over others.


### Finances


Verification of revenues and suchlike is perhaps the
least exciting of the Eye’s duties, but on a day-to-day basis,
it is the most important. Without an eye on the tax
revenues collected from the tributaries and the prefectures, the Realm would quickly find itself robbed blind.

from the return of the missing funds to public humiliation
of the offender to execution. Long ago, the Empress gave
her blessing to the Eye’s ability to serve as judge, jury and
executioner, and even now, the magistrates are reluctant
to tamper with that right.

THe Wy_p Hunt

Mixed in with its more scurrilous and fearsome agents,
the All-Seeing Eye also employs any number of augurers,
astronomers and diviners dedicated to locating the Anathema, wherever they may appear. Working hand in hand
with the diviners of the Immaculate Order (in many cases
without the Immaculates’ knowledge of their true allegiance), these are the souls who call down the Wyld Hunt.

The duties of the diviners are simple: Watch the skies
and other signs for indications thatan Anathema has been
reborn into the world. When such signs appear, pinpoint
the location of the new problem and sic the Wyld Hunt on
it. Finding the exact location ofa new Anathema’s appearance, however, is more difficult than discovering its
existence, and so, often, this results in an odd partnership
of augerers and spies, diviners and informants. Once the
Eye is convinced that a new Anathema is to arise, the
diviners are asked to give their best estimate as to where
the new threat will be located, and the area is flooded with
agents, observers and informants. After all, stories of a
local child suddenly being bathed in light and emerging
unharmed tend to make the rounds, and sooner or later, an
agent of the Eye will hear them.

Once the Anathema has been located, the Wyld
Huntis called forth. Officially, the Hunt is the provenance
of the Immaculate Order, and the Eye prefers it that way.
However, it is usual for at least one agent of the Eye to ride
with the Hunt when it goes out, to ensure that the hunters
perform their duties with the suitable efficiency. Hunts
also provide an excellent cover for agents engaged in other
business. Often, multiple agents will be attached toa single

THe DELIBERATIVE

The Deliberative is the lawmaking body of the Realm.
It represents the entire population of the empire — or at
least those segments of it that matter —~ and it has the
power to defy the Empress herself. In the halls of the Palace
of the Deliberative Senate, the laws of the Realm are
crafted, debated upon and polished, and the great orators
thunder-forth their arguments.

Such, at least, is the theory. The seality-ts is some-

=. what. different.


### E Chambers


wield ‘considerable power.
are called Senators, or E é


### The Offices


There are several offices within the ranks of the
Deliberative that Senators can aspire to. The most
powerful is the Master of the Deliberative, who oversees
the Greater Chamber's debates and plays an extensive
role in shaping the debate. Also attached to the Greater
Chamber is the Incorruptible Secretary, whose primary
responsibility is to tally any and all votes taken. These
two roles have their echoes in the Lesser Chamber, with
the Master of the Lesser Chamber and the Scrupulous
Monitor of the Will of the People serving essentially the
same functions. Each chamber also has a Guardian of
the Enlightened Discourse (called the Protector of the
Debate in the Lesser Chamber), who commands the
Guardians of the Deliberative, an elite force of guards
on hand to protect the Senators from all threats — and

‘from one another. Beyond that, all Senators are consid-

ered equal — at least in theory. There is no doubt that
a Senator of the Greater Chamber outranks one of the
Lesser, and within each chamber is an ornate hierarchy
of alliances, allegiances and blackmail that ensures a
rigid, yet mostly invisible, pecking order.

All offices are voted on at the first session of the
new year, though a no-confidence vote can be called at
any time. If the no-confidence vote succeeds, all officers
are removed from their posts and no new business can be
brought to the attention of the Deliberative until anew
Master of the Deliberative is in place. The chambers
cast their no-confidence votes separately, though a
change in the Greater Chamber almost always signals
one in the Lesser.

New officers are elected by a two-thirds majority in
the Greater Chamber and a simple majority in the lesser.
In both chambers, no-confidence votes require a twothirds majority.

"THE SENATORS

The Senators of the Greater Chamber are allowed to
be.attended by up to three servants and three slaves while

they. are in debate. One of the-servants is inevitably a

scribe; to record the pertinent details of the debate for the

<!--PAGE 64-->
Lesser Chamber Senators are permitted two servants and one slave. This is as much a practical
consideration as it is a reminder of rank; the Lesser
Chamber has vastly greater membership than the
Greater, and were each Senator allowed to bring in an
entourage equal to her sense of self-worth, a coliseum
would not be able to hold the throng.


### The Halls of Debate


The Deliberative is housed in the Palace of the
Deliberative Senate of Exceedingly Judicious Nobles
(the second building of that name, the first having been
burned to the ground four centuries ago). The building
itself is a massive structure of polished white stone set
amidst immaculately manicured grounds in the Imperial
City. The palace itself and its attendant gardens are
patrolled by the Guardians of the Deliberative, and by
custom, all who enter must leave their weapons and
accouterments of sorcery once they enter the palace
proper. This gesture is widely regarded as a symbolic one,
yet the ceremonial surrender of one’s arms to the home of
the laws of the Realm does emphasize the putative nature
of the work performed here.

The interior of the Palace of the Deliberative Senate is laid out to facilitate contemplation of the weighty
matters of state, and as such, it features innumerable
small gardens, pools, private chambers, baths, steam
rooms, residential quarters, libraries containing every
debate the Deliberative has ever held and kitchens. All
of this is constructed around the two largest rooms in
the complex: the Hall of Contemplative and Prudent
Debate, where the Lesser Chamber is seated, and the
Great Hall of Most Inspired and Noble Lawmaking,
where the Greater Chamber performs its labors.

The Hall of Debate is furnished entirely in wood,
with the benches of the Senators surrounding the debate floor on three sides. To the north are the seats of
the officers of the Lesser Chamber. The benches themselves.are plain wood and extremely uncomfortable.
Officially; this discomfort is to remind the Senators that
they-ar €: here to perform a difficule labor and not to

& process isas uncomfortable as possible for the
sand that their workdays are short and their

expected to enter and exit through the appropriate door
for their aspect. The Hall of Debate has nine doors, for a
reason no one has yet been able to adequately explain, and
its walls are bare of all decoration.


### Entering the Deliberative


The members of the Greater Chamber are drawn
exclusively from the Great Houses. Having a house
member selected to the Deliberative is something of a
double-edged sword. While it is an honor and it keeps
the family’s interest from being trampled in debate, it
also incurs the expense of supporting a Senator during
the legislative sessions, and it takes the newly minted
Senator out of other, potentially more useful labors.
However, an appointment to the Greater Chamber is
an honor and must be treated as such and is generally
celebrated with banquets, hunts and, on some occasions, auspicious marriages. An optimist would say that
it means that the merits of the new Senator are recognized; a pessimist that she was finally troublesome
enough to merit keeping a close eye on.

Once selected, a Senator of the Greater Chamber
journeys to the Imperial City for her formal investiture, a
process that takes place at the opening of each meeting of
the Deliberative. This ceremony is a command performance, and no dawdling is allowed. A Senator who arrives
late for her investiture is already three steps behind in the
great political game of the Deliberative.

Lesser Chamber Senators, on the other hand, are
selected not by the Empress, but by the members of the
Greater Chamber. Drawn from the ranks of the patricians, the wealthiest merchants and the tributary
queens and princes, the Senators of the Lesser Chamber are picked for their pliability, their wealth and
their potential to be useful to their betters. Often, a
Senatorial position is a bribe to a particularly important tributary prince or a reward for a scion of a
patrician family that performed sterling service to a
Dynast. There is a great deal of horse-trading that goes
on between Greater Chamber Senators when it comes
time to appoint new faces.to the Lesser, as Senators
jockey to appoint t avorites — or upstarts they
want to keep an eye*o! mans :

### The Art of Debate


When she first founded the Deliberative, the Empress
laid down a strict set of rules as to each and every one of
that august body’s behaviors, ranging from how new Senators are appointed to the rules of debate. Over the years,
the latter have acquired a near-religious authority, and the
process of initiating debate on the Deliberative’s floor has
become ritualized to the point of ponderousness. This was
quite pleasing to the Empress herself, though perhaps for
reasons other than those imagined by the Senators, and on
occasion, she would sit, veiled, in the Greater or Lesser
Chamber to hear the debate. The Regent, however, never
addresses or observes the Deliberative at all.

"THE GREATER CHAMBER

If there is any power in the Deliberative ac all, it rests
in the Greater Chamber. An appointment to the Greater
Chamber carries with it more prestige and, indeed, more
authority than one to the Lesser, and Senators of the
Greater Chamber are sure to let their so-called peers know
about it. Greater Chamber Senators have right of way in
processions, are served first at formal occasions and have

the right to displace a Lesser Chamber. Senator from her:

seat at any § state function.

Deliberative wear capes ‘of 8


### Introduction of Amotion


Only the Senators ofthe Greater Chamber can introduce a motion into debate. Each motion must have a
sponsor and a pair of seconds, who together compose a

‘| Declaration of Harmonious Intent to present to the Mas-

ter of the Deliberative. The Declaration isascroll consisting
of the content of the proposed motion, as well as testimonies from its sponsor and his seconds that the motion will,
if approved, blend harmoniously with the existing laws of
the Realm. While initially a simple text, the Declaration
has gotten more complex over the years, and now, any
Declaration that is delivered to the Master without suitable ornamentation (often by artists or poets kept on staff
for just such an occasion) is regarded as déclassé and not
worth the Master’s time. The Master cannot officially
refuse to present a Declaration, but he can find faults in
one, real or otherwise, that can render it “unsuitable,”
accomplishing the same thing.

The Declaration must be hand-delivered by the
sponsor to the Master of the Deliberative at the beginning of a day’s session, and there is great honor associated
with being the first to present to the Master that day.
Those who find themselves at the tail end of the line are
subject to ridicule and abuse from the seated Senators,
who feel that the stragglers are delaying the opening of
the day’s work and, by virtue of their worthless petitions, will extend it well into the night. They may have
a point — after all, if the sponsor of the Declaration
could not be bothered to arrive sufficiently early to
present it in a timely fashion, its content surely cannot
be that important. Jockeying for position within the
line of petitioners has become something of an art form
unto itself; allowing a senior and respected Senator to
advance past one in the line is the sort of favor that can
reap huge political dividends down the line.

As each Declaration is received, it is read in its
entirety to the Greater Chamber as a whole. The Master
of the Deliberative then calls for a formal vote to determine whether the Chamber is willing to debate the
Declaration. If the majority accedes to the notion, the
© Declaration.is burned i in a brazier kept to the deft of the

<!--PAGE 66-->
debate on each petition, starting with any left over from
the previous day’s discussion. If debate is extensive, days
and days of undebated declarations can pile up. It is the
Master of the Deliberative who decides the order in
which the proposals reach the floor, and this is the real
power associated with the position. By simply delaying a
proposed piece of legislation until the end of the day's
debate, the Master can effectively kill it or buy its
opponents (or allies) enough time to see their viewpoint
carry the day in debate. And if the session ends by
acclamation, then the petition is put off to the next day
after the recitation of Declarations. Ar the last full
month of the scheduled meeting of the Deliberative, a
ban is placed on further Declarations except by express
order of the Empress. This ensures that, sooner or later,
the debate catches up to the backlog of Declarations.

When debate opens on a particular petition, the
sponsoring Senator is given the chance to orate in favor of
it. He may take as much time as he feels necessary, and
some notorious filibustering sessions have stretched over
days of continuous blather. Most Senators prefer to speak
froma lectern at the center of the hall in which the Greater
Chamber meets, but others walk the floor, and on one
occasion, a Senator from House Cynis gave her entire
oration laying on her back between the benches.

Once the sponsor is finished, the rest of the Deliberative may join in the debate. The Master of the Deliberative,
aided by her secretaries, chooses the order of the debate,
and again, this grants the office tremendous power. While
it’s rare for a Master to be so unsubtle as to simply ignore
an eloquent speaker in a proposal’s behalf, she can certainly select a clumsier proponent to go first and, thus,
discredit the cause through sheer incompetence.

When debate on a petition finally dies down — and
there is no guarantee that it will do so in timely fashion —

a vote is called for and tallied by the Secretary. If a simple °

majority approves, it moves on to the Lesser Chamber, to
dare their disapproval. If a majority disapproves, then the
petition is considered denied, and debate moves on.


### Ending a Session

Greater Chamber’s labors can end. The first is that the

All results of the day’s work are tallied, petitions that have
been passed are sent on to the Lesser Chamber, and within

WHERE THE REAL Work GETS DONE

Ascribing particular effectiveness to any aspect of
the Greater Chamber is a tricky task because, by
design, the Greater Chamber is not supposed to ‘be
effective. However, what work there is that does get
done is generally the result of as much work off the
floor as on it. The endless maze of cloakrooms, meditative gardens, corridors and chapels that surround the
Deliberative’s meeting halls is the perfect place to cut
deals. Indeed, it is not uncommon to see Senators
summoned from the floor during debate to urgent
meetings down the hall.

Ultimately, the work of the Greater Chamber
boils down to a series of quid pro quos. A vote here
equals a voice raised in support there, a Declaration
allowed onto the floor today buys one a year later.
Most of the scheming and politicking within the
Greater Chamber is about the long term, as junior
appointees — particularly those with a penchant for
rabble-rousing — find‘to: their sorrow, as they almost
inevitably incur mass r

WHEN CiviuiTy BREAKS DOWN

On occasion, tempers flare during the
Deliberative’s debates. Usually, the Senators can
restrain themselves from flinging more than
words at one another, but every so often, actual
fisticuffs break out. While this is not so much of
a problem in the Lesser Chamber, which boasts
few Dragon-Blooded, in the Greater Chamber,
it can lead to impressive chaos, not to mention
loss of life and limb. The Guardians of the
Deliberative are supposed to interrupt such
brawls, but even these selfless souls have more
sense than to insert themselves into a brawling
mass of Dragon-Blooded. Instead, they generally
wait until one side has been pounded senseless
(or worse) and then move in to “mop up” any
small pockets of squabbling still remaining. By
ancient custom, combatants in the Deliberative
chambers are to use neither weapon nor Charm
nor sorcery, but that still leaves a great deal of
leeway for breaking furnishings... and people.

The last instance of a scrum in the Greater
Chamber occurred during the “packing” of the

Chamber just after the Regent’s accession. Several older Senators of House Tepet decried the
process as a blatant ploy by the younger houses to
seize power unlawfully. A representative from
House V'’Neef took offense to this accusation, and
in the resultant melee, two of the most vocal
hecklers of the appointments — including the
venerable and well-respected Tepet Salas — were
killed. Several of the younger V’Neef Senators
and their allies took leaves of absence from the
Deliberative immediately afterward, and the Senator who'd initiated the braw! was assigned to a
20-year diplomatic mission to the smaller archipelagos of the Western Ocean.

Chamber, ail votes are public — and tallies the results. If
any less than a two-thirds majority votes to the negative,
the motion passes. It achieves the status of decree and goes
to the Empress (now the Regent) for consideration. If,
however, more than two-thirds are mustered in opposition, then the motion is defeated. In such instances, a
scroll bearing the text of the motion is returned to the
Greater Chamber, bound in twined black and red ribbons.

A SuRPRISING DEGREE OF RESISTANCE

The Lesser Chamber would seem, at first glance, to be
_ nothing more than a rubber stamp for the machinations of
the Greater. For two equally compelling reasons, however,
this is not the case. For one, many of the Senators of the
Lesser Chamber are so desperate to assert their statesmanship that they feel compelled to comment on each and
every piece of legislation proposed, regardless of whether
they have anything of value to say. This tends to wear
down even the staunchest advocates of a petition.

Second and more importantly, almost to a man, the
Senators of the Lesser Chamber hate those of the Greater
and take great pleasure in confounding their designs.
While, on occasion, either bribes or a sense of responsibility to the Realm as a whole can sweep the Lesser
Chamber along with a piece of particularly pertinent
legislation, given their choice, the patricians prefer to
stick it to the Greater Chamber.

‘THE EMPRESS... AND THE REGENT

Written into the laws surrounding the Deliberative is
a sizable role for the Empress herself. She could, at any

“totally. Instead, bi

time, propose a petition without going through the process
of Declaration, and she could veto any decision of the two
chambers. Only a near-unanimous vote of both chambers
could overturn her veto —~ one dissenting vote was allowed in each chamber.

More importantly, the Empress had the ability to
appoint Senators to the Greater Chamber at will, and she
used this tool masterfully. An appointment to the Greater
Chamber cannot be refused save through suicide or
joining the Immaculates, and as such, the “gift” of sitting
in the Deliberative was often used to defuse young firebrands and defang rebels. Elevation to the Deliberative
carried with it an immediate audience with the Empress,
which made it useful as a method for disrupting activities
that the Empress looked upon unkindly. Once named to
the Deliberative, even the most hardened dissident generally assimilated into polite society, seduced by power.
Even those rare few who didn’t were rendered harmless,
pinned to the palace by their duties and overwhelmed by
their self-seeking peers.

Of late, however, the policy of adopting would-be
reformers into the Deliberative is having some unexpected
consequences. Operating under the formerly mistaken
notion that the Deliberative possesses real authority, these
firebrands — many of them from outlying estates or even
the Threshold — are pushing for real alterations in the
structure of the Realm. Accordingly, debate in the Deliberative has taken on a more serious and explosive air.
Votes on Declarations over such issues as redistribution of
taxes, greater autonomy for the governors of prefectures
and Threshold states, increased civil liberties and taxes on
the Immaculate Order have gotten uncomfortably close
for the Deliberative’s conservative old guard. Several
members of the so-called Yellow Banner faction of Young
Turks have been ambushed by thugs on their way to the -
Deliberative in recent weeks, and threats of retaliation
have been issued on the floor of the Deliberative itself.

### Attendance


Attendance at a session of the Deliberative is, contrary to popular belief, not mandatory. A Senator is
perfectly within her rights to skip a day’s session — or
even an entire month’s worth. However, doing so was
frowned upon emphatically by the Empress, who liked
knowing precisely where all of her Senators were at any
given time. Even with the Empress gone, however, the
Senators rarely, ifever, miss more than a single day, as the
fear of falling behind in the great game of politics outweighs any desire they might have to be elsewhere.

<!--PAGE 70-->

### The Immaculate Order


To say that the Order of the Immaculate Dragons is
the official faith of the Realm is to be, on a certain level,
ingenuous. After all, the Immaculate monks do not
mandate attendance at religious services or demand
tithing. They do not denounce those who are not sufficiently pious as being damned to everlasting torment,
and they do not feel the need to organize pogroms or
religious crusades against less enlightened neighbors.

After all, they know better.

And that, ultimately, is the key to the Immaculate
faith. They know. They know that there is a celestially
ordained order to the universe and to the beings within
it, and they know precisely where they —~ and everyone
else in the Realm — fits in that order. Theirs is the
supreme confidence that comes from having all of the
answers, a perfect understanding of the way the universe
actually works. With the Immaculate Texts asa guide, an
Immaculate monk is confident in his role in the greater
scheme of things — and satisfied with it.

This is, at the same time, the great blessing and the
great mystery of the Immaculates. Their serene confidence
— and their obvious prowess — serve to reassure the
populace as a whole of the rightness of their beliefs.

THe CENTRAL TENET

The core belief at the center of the Immaculate faith
is a simple one. There is a structure to the universe, a
celestially inspired blueprint in which every thing and
every living being has its place. Every being must show
respect to those below it in this great chain of being and
has a duty of obedience to those who are higher. Thus, it
is right and proper for a farmer to kill and eat a chicken
for sustenance, and it is right for the chicken to give up
its life so that the farmer might live. By the same token,
it is right and proper for that farmer to give part of the
fruits of his labors on his land as taxes to the Dragon-
Bloaded noble who owns the land he toils on. So long as
all provide their proper obedience and accept their proper
respect, the cosmos functions smoothly, and all is well.
The chicken has fulfilled its destiny, the peasant is fed,
and the Dragon-Blooded is honored. .

It is when proper obedience to one’s role and one’s
superiors is not given that chaos results, and where
chaos grows, bloodshed follows. It is not proper, for
example, for a fox to devour the chicken, as the chicken
owes its obedience to the farmer. By eating the farmer’s
fowl, the fox disrupts the chain of appropriate behavior.
In the small scale, this creates local chaos. By eating the

<!--PAGE 71-->

hungry, which is inappropriate for a spiritually superior

and leave his true labors behind. The fox reaps death,

Monks dedicate themselves to one Elemental Dragon
or another when they are fully initiated into the Order.
Dragon-Blooded acolytes most often choose the Elemental Dragon whose sign matches their elemental affinity,
though, on rare occasions, a postulant feels that she jis
