other children in their house. Children of the same age
are sometimes tutored together and might also be allowed to take meals together and even sleep in the same
room. It is believed that this helps to encourage house
loyalty. However, they are also encouraged to compete.
They're each pushed separately to do better than the
other, and some households go so far as to punish a child
who falls behind his peer.

EAar.y EDUCATION

Dynastic children aren’t shipped off to boarding school
until their ninth birthdays, and there’s plenty they can
learn before then. The children go through an exercise
regimen each day designed to teach.them the basics of
coordination and strength and to teach them how to
endure discomfort and physical stress.

They're usually taught a second language during these
early years. Which one depends on where they live — it’s
often something useful to their daily lives. They’re also
taught the basics of survival or how to sail if they live in the
islands. If they live in a coastal area of the Realm, they
probably learn both. Most learn to ride horses.

Children are taught enough self-defense to be able to
protect themselves a little; the emphasis is usually on
getting away from their attackers and calling for help.
Houses with a strong military emphasis, such as Cathak,
Ragara, Tepet and Peleps, often teach the basics of the
martial arts and archery during these early years. This isn’t
to say that there are seven year olds running around
capable of beating up adults, but they learn enough that
later lessons come more easily to them.

Children do play games with each other, including
Gateway, but children’s games are always designed to
teach them something. Thatsomething might be as simple
as hand-eye coordination, endurance or strength or as
complex as strategic thinking or philosophical concepts,
but the lesson is always there.

Many Dynastic children are taught to sing or to play
a musical instrument with at least minimal skill — enough
so that their parents can show them off without being
embarrassed. Ragara, Teper, Cynis, Sesus and Cathak in
particular value these skills.

Dynastic children raised outside of the Realm proper

### Education


Schools do a great deal to shape future Dragon-
Blooded and the unExalted family that supports them.
Because of this, much is expected of the schools. They are
expensive and exclusive, and they are always overseen by
the Exalted in some capacity or another.

While it is possible for a Dragon-Blooded child to be
schooled at home, it is almost never done. A few households have tried it and found that their children had an

extremely difficult time being accepted into society. They

rt et ind-their. peers in terms of ability and

<!--PAGE 104-->
these foreign schools, to give the children a comparatively
safe look at the Threshold.

Each school teaches a certain basic curriculum
mandated by the government. This curriculum includes:
archery, bare-fisted fighting and self-defense, swordplay, athletics, history, geography, government and
politics, religion and philosophy and spirit lore. This
isn’t to say that every student will excel in every field.
Students couldn’t possibly take every class offered, and
even if they did they wouldn’t get top marks in each one.
In addition to the basic curriculum, students must
usually complete at least one advanced course on combat skills, one on government and politics and one on
religion.and philosophy, to prepare them for their lives
as Dragon-Bloods.

Students cannot be failed out of a primary school,
although children who cause real and constant disruptions
may be: returned to their families. These children. are eoften

to attract the most skilled professors was to specialize in
particular fields of study; that specialization brought the
most talented scholars in the school’s favored field to that
school. A child who goes to one sort of school, however,
can still easily go on to a career in a different field — the
specialized curriculum is meant to give the child color, not
to preeducate him ina profession. Some secondary schools
prefer to take students with widely varying backgrounds;
they believe the practice makes the educational experience richer for everyone.

Some schools specialize in outdoor activities, such as
hunting, tracking, forestry, mining, farming, riding, sailing, skilled labor, ecology and so on. These are the less
expensive schools and are usually attended by the children
of lesser Dragon-Blooded.: Eagle’s Launch, Traiter and
Bright Obelisk house the only schools of this nature,

Other schools specialize in. politics and: government. They offer entire courses, on: the Thousand Scales -

<!--PAGE 105-->
City, Immaculate Preparatory Institute in Lord’s Crossing and the Chestnut School in Juche.

There are also military-oriented schools. These institutions traditionally teach basic strategy, tactics and military
history, as well as simple physical combat. Their history
courses concentrate on the Realm’s victories and defeats
—and how they were achieved. The best military schools
can be found in Noble, Chanos and Arjuf. Several Ragara
generals have been known to teach semester-long courses
at the Chanos school, which makes it very popular.

Still other schools emphasize lore and.learning.
These academies teach languages from far-off places,
the rules of distant spirit courts, the intricacies of
philosophy, religion and history, the basics of medicine
and first aid and so on. Some parents believe that these
schools prepare Dynastic children for the widest range
of possible professions, and thus, these schools are very
popular. The best of these schools are found in Eagle’s
Launch, Cloaka, Sion and Portee.

The last type of school emphasizes artistic ability
and working with one’s hands. Children who attend
these schools might be taught the basics of architecture along with music, sculpture, painting and glass
blowing. The best such school is the Gournay Academy in Tuchara, and it has a long waiting list. Other
well-known artisan-schools can be found in Blackwater and Juche. Artistic ability is highly valued among
Dynastic children, although few parents want their
children to make a living in the field.

Typically, about one-third of the classes in primary school are “dabbling” courses. This means that
the students spend one or two classes each term on
various sub-topics within a field. For example, an
artisanry course at a military school might cover glass
blowing one week and blacksmithing the next. These
classes aren’t intended to teach the skill to the students. They’re intended to give them a realistic view of
what the skill entails and what concerns the workers
have. This knowledge helps the child make better
decisions later, both regarding his own career path and
that of those who work for him.

### Secondary Schools


By the time they’re ready for their six years of secondaryschool, the Dragon-Blooded have had their Exaltation.
They’re no longer distracted by the worry of whether
they'll disappoint their parents or whether the bully in the
next room over will Exalt first and make their lives even
worse. It’s time to get down to the real work.

Most of the secondary schools have long waiting lists,
so parents typically start the application process long
before their child finishes primary school. Not everyone
gets into secondary school, and some drop out without
finishing. There is a certain stigma attached to the failure
to graduate; such Dragon-Blooded are considered inferior,
less capable and less able to handle the rigors of life.

It’s during secondary school, that many Dragon-
Blooded meet and make their closest friends. These are the
people that they adventure with after they leave school.
Often, these individuals become political allies, lovers and
hunting companions. Even if they don't stay close, they
might do favors for each other now and then.

More information on secondary schooling is included

on page 122 of this chapter, in the section under “Dragon-
Blooded Schooling.”


### Coming of Age


When a Dragon-Blooded graduates from secondary
school, she is considered to have come of age. She is
officially an adult and a member of her house. This recognition brings with it both responsibilities and freedoms
previously unexperienced. It is probably the first time that
the Exalt has been allowed to truly make her own important decisions, and there’s no slow, gradual transition.


### Dropouts


Dropouts from the secondary schools are generally
not offered posts of importance in the Imperial Service. However, if the dropout takes a less important
post or otherwise picks up a profession that stays
within the Dragon-Blooded purview, then the child’s
parents do not ignore him. They might fail to invite
him to parties where there will be important people,
but they do treat him as family.

More dropouts choose to travel and socialize instead,
particularly if they, ve ‘themselves tobe capable peor

‘then their lac

Rib stoked ton
y choose to live with their. par

<!--PAGE 106-->
many more go off to live with other house members.
Dynastic parents promote this to broaden their children’s
experiences and keep them from falling back into childhood habits. The status of the relatives who offer up their
homes depends on how well the child did in school and
how promising her future looks.

While the graduate can return to live with those
relatives at any time, an ambitious young Dynast has little
time for such coddling. She will have a job and often an
apartment (termed a “day home”), which could be in
another city entirely, though rarely one more than a few
days from the home she has chosen.

A Dragon-Blood may choose to take a sabbatical
before the job she has been offered starts. Those Exalts who
do take sabbaticals spend two or more years lazing about
and enjoying high society, learning things not taught at
school, traveling or adventuring. It is expected that most
graduates will do this, and those who offer positions to
graduating Dragon-Blooded take this into account.

Most sabbaticals involve wide travel, visiting distant
lands, meeting influential people and involving oneself
in all the excitement one can handle. Travel is recognized as a very enlightening occupation for a young
Dragon-Blooded and is highly approved of. A young
Exalt is only considered to be wasting her talent and time
if she occupies more than 30 or 40 years on these adven-
‘tures. However, if she noticeably furthers her house’s
interests during her travels, then she will be allowed to
travel as long as she likes without protest.

If the Dragon-Blooded skips her sabbatical to stay
with her house and immediately take a position, then she
involves herself in the intricacies of high society right
away. Young Exalted focus their energies on the hunt, latenight games of Gateway, pleasure-sailing and the
maneuverings of the imperial court. Exalted whoskip their
sabbaticals have more contacts in the Imperial City than
their traveling brethren, and they often have the ear of
various officials. These Dragon-Bloods haven’t had as
much practical experience out in the world, however, and
their views and skills are more narrowly focused. They’ve
a leg up in the political world, but their more worldly
cousins often.consider them stodgy and narrow-minded.

Those Dragon-Blooded who immediately move into
military. careers fall between the two groups described
above. Through maneuvers and campaigning, they experience.some.of the same travels as their more re adventurous

they’re currently posted in. Many officers take frequent
leave to attend to familial matters.

Dragon-Blooded who move into military careers right
away also make more friends and allies among the patricians than doother Dragon-Blooded. They work constantly
and closely with the unExalted; it takes a very arrogant or
disconnected Dragon-Blooded to avoid making any ties or
friendships during this time.


### Dragon-blooded Society


The normal unit of Dragon-Blooded society
is the household. This is a husband and wife or a
single senior individual who acts as the head of
the household. If the household is located in a
Manse (all the better ones are), one of these
individuals will bear the Hearthstone by right,
but it can be loaned out, typically to children or
relatives in need. Also part of this household are
any of the family’s children, cither those too
young to attend primary school or older children
on holiday from school. Finally, a household will
generally include one or more Dragon-Blooded
who have graduated from (or washed out of)
secondary school and are living away from home
but haven’t set up households of their own.
These cousins or nieces and nephews can vary in
age from freshly graduated to quite old, and some
may have their own offspring. These loosely
telated Dragon-Blooded see to household security, help raise the children, hunt and train for
battle and socialize with members of other houses
tofurther their house’s image. How longa Dragon-
Blood remains with the household depends on
what sort of relationship he develops with the
mistress of the household. Add to this the
unExalted family members living on the premises and the assortment of tutors, slaves,
bodyguards, functionaries and servants, and even
minor households can grow quite large.

]NCOME oe

Newly graduated (
Blooded get a “family in

<!--PAGE 107-->
Dragon-Blooded who choose travel and adventure
after graduating often find that this money is enough to
support them in a spare lifestyle. They supplement their
income by taking food and items from others or by performing suitably impressive tasks for payment. No
self-respecting Dragon-Blooded will engage in acts of mere
menial labor to earn money; the Terrestrial Exalted are
willing to help set the piers for a great bridge, but they
would never dig ditches.

Those who go into the military straight out of secondary school are well taken care of. Housing, clothing and
simple meals are paid for by whoever sponsors the legion.
Dynasts use their family income to pay for particularly
good weapons or armor, for gifts for distant family or
potentates they're visiting or for fancy clothing for society
parties. Many military Exalted save up their family income
against a future day when they might choose to move into
a less active position or when they have a family of their
own to provide for.

Dragon-Blooded who immediately go into jobs and
find homes supplement their family income with their
personal salary. The family income allows them to either
live beyond the means of their position or to save up. Most
Dragon-Blooded use it to suitably attire themselves for
parties and gatherings and to throw wild parties for their
friends and peers. Typically, the stipends ofa given generation of Exalts are under the control of anaunt or uncle; one
child of a given generation is given the responsibility of
managing the stipends for all of the next generation.
House leaders see this as a good way to teach the young
how to work with their elders. The uncle or aunt with the
purse strings is often better attuned to the competencies
and politics of that younger generation than any of hisown
siblings and cousins, giving him leverage in dealings with
both elder and younger Dynasts.

Married Dragon-Blooded receive an increased family
income to aid in the support of their spouse and to help pay
for the new social responsibilities that come with marriage.
Exalted with children who haven’t yet come of age receive
afurther stipend. Also, as each Dragon-Blooded completes
another decade of activity that in some way benefits her
house,-she typically receives an increase in her stipend.

most in need of a supplementary income, and a few

large, these Dragon-Blooded must fend for themselves.

Dragon-Blooded do not count on inheritances.
Their parents tend to live so long and beget so many
offspring that inheritances are rare and generally insignificant. When there is an inheritance, half of it goes to
the spouse or eldest Dragon- Blooded child. Another 40
percent is divided between all remaining Dragon-
Blooded children. The final 10 percent is divided up
between all mortal children.

### Society


Sociery with a capital “S” comes in three main parts:
parties and salons, visits and public celebrations. Dragon-
Blooded are expected to participate in all of these events,
and the Dynasts usually make political contacts and wrangle
contracts and votes in these settings.

Large parties (referred to as “galas”) usually involve all
of the Dragon-Blooded of a city or region, and to fail to
invite someone can be seen as a grave insult. Because of
this, ifa host doesn’t wish someone to attend, she'll do her
best to either throw the party when he’ll be traveling or to
arrange something to delay him from arriving. This makes
it almost impossible to snub several nobles at once; it is said
that the Empress preferred this state of affairs, as it kept the
Dragon-Blooded more worried about measuring their social status than interfering with her rule. Galas usually
begin in the early afternoon and continue until the wee
hours of the morning, although some last for several days.
Wedding galas-are a:particularly important type of gala;
they are derailed: on: 13, under “Marriage.”

Smaller parties usually involve anyone related to the
event being commemorated. A birthday. party, for example, would include the Dragon-Blooded whose birthday
is being celebrated, any local house members, any distant
house members who are important or close to the honored
Dragon-Blooded, the Exalt’s betrothed and his family and
any important house allies. “Related to” is often interpreted loosely to include anyone the family is trying to
impress or make an alliance with.

Salons can be brunch or afternoon affairs, or they
can last for several days. It is not uncommon for five or
ten local Dragon-Bloaded of a similar age to spend the
weekend together, usually bringing their lovers as well.
They discuss poetry, talk about the latest measures going
through the Deliberative, take drugs, play romantic and
political games or simply go hunting together and play
Gateway for days on end.

Visits occur when one Dragon-Blooded visits another's

complaint. The visitor never pays for her room or the food
or for any other normal resources she uses during her stay.
In practice, ifa visitor stays for longer than two weeks, she
is expected to present her hosts with goods that more or less
make up for the cost of housing her (a “visitor’s gift”).
Close friends are not expected to provide a visitor’s gift,
but they often bring gifts of lesser value in thanks.

typically organize them. The meals at public celebrations
are less fancy, the dress less elaborate, and most Dragon-
Blooded behave themselves a little better since they're in
the public eye. A public celebration could be as short as a
day-long parade or as long as a two-week festival; most last
around three days.

Parties can range from wild Cynis orgies to weeklong Ragara Gateway tournaments with wines, the best
delicacies and the most popular poets. Nellens likes to
include gladiatorial contests. House V’neef is best known
for its drunken revels and for including patricians of all
sorts in its festivities — this keeps them from having
many Dragon-Blooded attendees, but makes the house
all sorts‘of friends in other places. Tepet has’ long been

Ledaal tends not to throw as many parties as the
other houses, but it isn’t left out entirely. Most of its
parties are smaller, more private affairs and include
hunting, small gladiatorial games and complex contests
of wit. Most Peleps parties take place on the water in one
of the house’s pleasure yachts. Peleps guests hunt for
particularly elusive or dangerous sea-prey. No one really
cares if they succeed — the excitement of the chase is
considered enough. In the meantime, storytellers give
dramatic performances, guests eat delicacies of all sorts,
and some impressive vote collecting occurs; Peleps parties have what some might consider undue influence over
the activities of the Deliberative.

### Sworn Brotherhoods

Dragon- Blooded _often become involved i in sworn

TS of elite ‘troops ‘form
s s they: find themselves

<!--PAGE 110-->
a social bond, joined with oaths of mutual service and
protection. Often, a circle forms for a specific purpose, and
oaths are taken regarding that purpose.

The sworn purpose of a brotherhood can be vague
or specific, idealistic or practical. Those that are vague
can be creatively interpreted to include or exclude
whatever the group wants but also sometimes leave the
members feeling that they have to intervene in matters
that they might otherwise prefer to stay out of. Narrow
purposes are less open to interpretation but also include
a smaller range of responsibilities.

Possible purposes include: the protection of a prefecture or province, the hunting and slaying of Anathema,
the betrerment of the Realm, the discovery of ancient
secrets and lore, the spread of the Immaculate Philosophy,
the destruction of harmful spirits, the protection of the
helpless and so on.

The Empress encouraged the formation of sworn
brotherhoods. Once every five years, she publicly honored
a brotherhood that had done something particularly noteworthy in service to the Realm, and she instructed the
stipend-masters of the houses of those involved to give
small increases to the family income of the brotherhood
members. After all, not only do most brotherhoods wander
around doing good things for the Realm for little reward,
but they usually cross house boundaries. This complicates
house loyalties and results in Dragon-Blooded whose first
loyalty is not necessarily to their house. This helps to keep
any one house from becoming too strong and, thus helped
the Empress to maintain her position.

Depending on acitizen’s particular experiences with
past brotherhoods, he might passionately despise them,
laud them as godlike and noble or guardedly approve of
them. Brotherhoods do noteworthy things, and not everyone loves their actions. Many citizens of the Realm
have strong feelings one way or another regarding the
sworn brotherhoods and their habit of involving themselves in other people’s problems. A village that was
saved from a river dragon by a brotherhood will surely
give free room and board — and anything else it can
possibly afford — to any brotherhood that passes through.
A village whose “heretical” altar was burned and whose
favorite Little God torn asunder might shut its doors to
any brotherhood it sees.

Not all brotherhoods are equal. Some do their
research, make sure they know what's going on and do

they wantiwith a few good frien

A brotherhood can range from a band of heroes to a
group of glorified brigands.

Brotherhoods usually stay together for at least 10
years. A very few have stayed together for several centuries. Brotherhoods disband by mutual agreement — this
often takes place when one or more members of the
brotherhood find themselves elevated to positions of authority from which they cannot travel the Blessed Isle or
when one of their number dies. The first step in the
disbanding of a sworn brotherhood is a mystical sundering
of the oath all swore when the group first formed. Afterward, most brotherhoods individually inform their houses
that they will no longer act as traveling companions; some
hire heralds or messengers to pass that word along to large
cities where they have received acclaim in the past. Even
once they officially disband, most brotherhoods stay in
touch. The sort of closeness engendered by the experiences a brotherhood goes through is not easily dispelled.
These Dragon-Blooded often become political allies, arrange for their children to marry each other, do favors for
one another, attend parties together and just generally
support each other. Some eventually retire together, living
out their last decades together or going out once more as
a brotherhood to die in a last heroic effort.

A few brotherhoods end badly, breaking up with
vicious feelings of betrayal and anger. Ir is said among the
Dragon-Blooded that the best friends make for the best
enemies, and this is just as true among the swom brotherhoods. Not all members of all brotherhoods have the same
ideas of what they want to do with their lives, and all it
takes is one closet idealist among bullies (or vice versa) to
turn everything bad.

ADVENTURE & RESPONSIBILITY

“Adventure” is considered a proper part of a Dragon-
Blooded's life. There is much good that can be done for the
Realm through the impulse for individual heroism. Sworn
brotherhoods are just one official channel for this desire;
many Dynasts choose to adventure without the formality
of a brotherhood behind them. Individually or in small
groups, the Dragon-Blooded travel, experience the world,
meet people and learn where their preferences lie in
matters from swordplay to'sexual position.

The Dragon-Blooded live for a long time. Alchough:._

careers midstream, comple )

influence to back their house’s schemes. It is not unheard
of, however, for a Dynast to juggle adventure with responsibilities. Spouses who don’t love each other, after all,
don’t need to live together all the time. Some spouses
actually adventure together. As Dragon-Blooded spend
very little time with their children, it doesn’t take much
time out of adventuring to have a child now and then.
Adventuring can easily support the Exalt’s house, as long
as the Dragon-Blooded keeps his house's aims and goals in
mind as he chooses his path.

Indeed, some Dragon-Blooded further their own political careers in this fashion, spending decades adventuring
with a brotherhood (be it formal or informal). It is a small
step from adventurous roaming through the Realm and
Threshold to taking the same kind of action on behalf of
the Scarlet Throne. Such groups work as archons or the
agents of other groups and, depending on their prestige,
might outrank local satraps in matters of imperial security.

Even Dynasts who settle down and take on sedentary
political careers often go out for weeks here and there to
adventure. Many use the protection of villages or prefectures under the control of their house as an excuse to get
outand rake ina little excitement. Others take “vacations”
to outlying provinces in the Threshold, traveling to locations of unrest and upheaval. Those with some military
background take temporary positions with brief military
campaigns or exercises, those without it simply cause
trouble. Most satraps and prefects try to keep in mind the
fact that up to 100 Dragon-Blooded of varying power,
interests and influence may turn up independently of one
another in response to local crises.


### Keeping in I Ouch


Dragon-Blooded are encouraged by their families
to keep in touch with their houses after they’ve come of
age. This helps both the young members of the house
and the house itself. It is not uncommon for traveling
Dragon-Blooded to take the time to send regular missives to their families, often via the Imperial Post or
Immaculate monks they meet during their travels. Others use family members, servants of the family, Charms,
spells; Hearthstones, Guild caravans or even friendly
spirits who owe the Dynasts a boon.

_CALIBRATION VENTURING

A tradition has started up over the last
200 years. Each time Calibration rolls around,
bad things seem to happen around the Realm.
Even when monsters don’t mysteriously attack and disasters don’t befall the populace,
the sheer level of superstition surrounding the
time causes everyone to go a little crazy. Because of this fact, various houses indulge in a
tradition called “Calibration Venturing” for
those five days. Ragara first started the tradition, and it has slowly spread from Great
House to Great House since then.

Anyone who can be spared gathers together with friends and family and heads out
into the countryside. In theory, they wander
around keeping people calm, reminding the
populace that everything is under control and
dealing with any true disasters that happen. In
practice, most help to keep the populace under
control, some hole up in their hideaways and
party, and the remaining few take the opportunity to do a little pillaging of their own.

In some Threshold towns bordering on
lands allied to the Realm, people dressed as
demons have descended upon the towns during
Calibration. They burn the buildings, kill and
rape the residents and take whatever they like.
Rumor has it they were seen using Dragon-

‘Blooded Charms, but no one is willing to
substantiate this claim. Those few peasants

who live through the attacks are usually too
frightened to speak of them. A number of
sworn brotherhoods have promised to travel to
these villages during the next Calibration,
though the various Dynastic houses are too
busy trying to outmaneuver one another for the
Throne to worry too much about what is a
relatively minor problem in the hinterlands.

might pass'on a token that will give them safe passage. If
they explain’ abou

<!--PAGE 112-->
Traditionally, such missives are always phrased in
neutral terms of what is happening. Few young Dynasts
stoop to asking for help or whining about their situation.
They're expected to be strong, and they know that if their
family chooses to help, it is usually because the family
expects to get something out of the deal. While Dynasts
carefully phrase their missives so as to subtly evoke the
reaction they want from their family, they know that
asking outright is one of the surest ways to convince their
family not to help.


### Marriage


Marriage isa very important partofa Dragon-Blooded’s
life, Thro gh marriage, alliances are made and broken.

Reasons & DECISIONS

Marriage is seen as a sacred duty by the Dragon-
Blooded, for many reasons. Particularly in these trying
times, the Great Houses need all of the alliances they
can build, and marriage is one of the surest ways to
create an alliance. After all, both sides of the family will
want to assure the future of any Dragon-Blooded children who are born, and they can’t do that if they're
back-stabbing the rest of the immediate family. Martiages also take place.to produce children, as well as to
legitimize Dynasts’ role in society.

Some Dragon-Bloodeéd: Marry to make. allianées:,
for their families.“Others' marry. to get the increased.”
family income that com s from such: ‘an event, Some

<!--PAGE 113-->
those Dragon-Blooded who don’t enjoy living with
their families but don’t wish to travel.

Still others choose to marry simply because that’s
what their families want of them. Parents can have a fair
degree of influence over their children, and they often use
this influence to bully or manipulate them into getting
married. Marriage is almost always a benefit to all parties
involved, so there’s plenty of reason to push for it.

Many houses, particularly Ragara and Mnemon, distinguish between “coming of age” and true adulthood.
Dragon-Blooded can still be considered children, or at
least childish, until they've married or agreed to marry. As
a result, some members of these houses marry to gain
greater respect or to be taken more seriously.

A few Dragon-Blooded do marry for love. Some
simply choose to marry someone they love, consequences
be damned. Others fall in love with the person their family
wants them to marry. After all, courtships and betrathals
can last fora long time, even decades. During this time, the
families try to make it as easy as possible for the betrothed
to get to know and like each other. Most families take care
not to betroth Dragon-Blooded who despise each other, so
there’s at least some good will to parlay into possible love.
Also, some Dragon-Blooded who fall in love while at
school manage to convince their families that a betrothal
to the beloved would be a goad idea.

Patricians who marry into a Dragon-Blooded line are
expected to provide a wedding gift to the Dynastic family
(the patricians often refer to this as “the bribe”). This gift
is expected to be lavish and expensive and often includes
fine silks, livestock, promises of political favors and jade.
When Dragon-Blooded weds Dragon-Blooded, both families provide wedding gifts to each other.


### Marrying Without Permission


Marrying without one's parents’ permission is a difficult thing todoand brings with it unpleasant consequences.

Few imperial judges will consider wedding a Dragon-
Blooded without his parents’ permission, particularly if
those parents are powerful and the young hothead is not.

A marriage to a peasant is not legal unless three
imperial judges sign the paperwork. A few judges are
softhearted; others will perform marriages that lack parental approval for their own reasons. Others are greedy and
will do it for a great deal of money. Such judges had better
acquire protection (political, magical or physical) from
the Exalt’s parents, however, unless they happen to be
suicidal. Ifneither of the families approves and there are no
promises to protect the judges, then the wedding is unlikely to go forward.

No imperial judge would ever wed a Dragon-Blooded
andaslave or one of the disenfranchised, even if the family
did, for some reason, approve. Such a wedding is considered wholly illegal and could not be performed without a
direct dispensation from the Empress and the Deliberative
— permission that has never been given.


### Ceremonies


Marriage ceremonies are usually gala events, overseen by both immediate families, at least afew important
members of both houses and, depending on the importance of the parents, possibly the local governor and

-prefect. Both sets of parents contribute to the preparations. Often, a competition develops — each set of

parents. wants to be in charge of the most important
parts of the’ ceremony or to provide t the most tlavish and

<!--PAGE 114-->
house, and it is traditional for her to stand with the
judge during the ceremony.

The couple getting married speaks brief but solemn vows promising to support their families and the
Realm. They also vow to do their best to produce
Dragon-Blooded children and to raise them to serve
the Realm. The ceremony primarily consists, however,
of a speech by the judge about the duties and responsibilities of marriage, adulthood and serving the empire.
Judges are often chosen based on their speaking skills,
and certain judges are constantly in demand for weddings. The priest then blesses the union, and the
couple is legally wed.

The ceremony is followed by music, pageantry and
poetry. Plays, poems and songs all expound on the subject
of marriage and children, and some families expect the
couple to perform at least one song or piece of music
together. The rest of the day usually passes in a haze of
wine, food, gifts and congratulations.


### Length of Marriage


Dragon-Blooded marriages are typically lifelong affairs. There are only a few situations under which divorce
is allowable. If the couple has tried to have children for
at least two decades and been unable to do so, then the
marriage is considered void. If the couple has gone five
decades without having children, then the marriage is
also voided, whether or not the couple has tried to have
children. Special dispensation may be given by the Empress and the Deliberative to allow such spouses to
remain married, but it is rarely extended — usually only
in cases where both spouses can be proven to be sterile.
It only takes one living child every 50 years to keep any
of this from being a problem.

A Dragon-Blooded can petition an imperial judge
to void his marriage if his spouse is convicted of committing a serious crime such as treason or the murder of
an Exalt. He can also petition for an annulment if he
can prove that his spouse tried to harm or kill him or one
of his underage children. Otherwise, death is the only
end to a marriage.

Re-marriage after widowing is considered completely
acceptable, particularly since notall Dragon-Blooded marry
other Dragon-Blooded. Patricians don’t live nearly as long
as the Exalted, and the Realm sees it as foolish to remove
a Dragon-Blooded from the possibilities of parenthood
simply because he married someone shogt-li

Marriage between Dragon-Blooded and
humans can work out well if the marriage is
not done for love. Such marriages almost in-

evitably end in tragedy if the couple is in love.
The human is incredibly unlikely to live as
long as the Dragon-Blooded, although a few

Blooded in the history of the Realm have

couple, and the couple’s estate is fined heavily.

Failing to uphold this fiction usually results in a
slight loss of status among one's social peers. A Dragon-
Blood who passionately kisses his lover on a public street
of his hometown, for example, would probably find his
friends spending less time with him and doing fewer
favors for him. A Dragon-Blood who simply holds hands
with his lover, isseen having dinner ina public restaurant
with her or kisses her passionately in a foreign land
where, surely, no one knows that she isn’t his wife,
however, is unlikely to receive any disapproval.

A spouse is also not expected to raise a fuss over adultery
unless his partner is particularly public about it. This act is
considered breaking the fiction of faithfulness just as much
as a public kiss on the part of the unfaithful spouse. Most
spouses simply pretend that their husband or wife isn’t
having an affair at all, and find their own lovers to enjoy.
Because of this, wives often pretend that any children that
result from their liaisons are legitimate children from their
marriage, with almost no discussion of the truth.

When Dragon- -Blooded host parties, they often
- invite. their guests’ lovers as well. They go to great
lengths making certain quarters are positioned so that
“Jovers can meet discreetly.

How Often?

Once a couple has had a single child, it no longer has
to worry about its marriage being voided. Some naive
couples take this as license to forget about childbearing
altogether. However, such a couple discovers that, after
a few decades of childlessness, rumors abound that the
child it did have wasn’t the product of a legitimate
coupling. Couples that aren’t particularly interested in
having children average two to three children per century. The average across the Realm is eight per century —
about one every 12 years.

Couples particularly eager to take advantage of the
monetary and political gains to be had through children
might have themas often as every five years. However, just
as the Exalted live longer:than.mortals, certain biological.
processes take longer in.Exalted: than’ mortals. Female’
Terrestrials who have ‘children: more ‘often than: about
once every five years tend to:suffer from occasional weak-

ess and dizziness, .as. well as, a:decreased (

### Baby-swapping


Even though children legally belong to the
social class of their mother, Dynastic families go
to elaborate lengths to fake the birth of the child

of an affair into their family if the mother is a
peasant or low-status patrician. For instance, in
Realm Year 731, Peleps Zatar brought his pregnant lover into his houschold as a servant and
had his wife, Kidra, pretend to be pregnant,
even to the point of isolating herself in her
rooms for the last days before birth. When the
baby was born, it was presented as the child of
Peleps Kidra, and the patrician was allowed to
remain as the baby’s wet nurse for the first year.
The peasant was given money to keep her quict
and promised that her child would be raised
with all of the advantages that a peasant child
would never have.

BLOODLINE & BREEDING

Bloodline and breeding are very important to the
Dragon-Blooded. The more they dilute their bloodlines
with human stock, particularly humans who aren't of
Dragon-Blooded lineage, the less likely they are to give
birth to children who will Exalt. Thus, by breeding with
easaritsor slaves, they reduce the chances that their
children will advance to spiritual harmoriy, This act is
considered foolish at best and cruel at worst

to be seen as impetuous, foolish
seen as not caring for the fami

aren’t actively removing the chance to produce another
Dragon-Blooded child.

Because having children by poorly bred partners is
seen as a mark of bad character, the word of an offending
female Dynast is often distrusted and scorned. Her behavior is seen as an embarrassment, and her friends may shun
her. Some houses reduce the family income of the offending Dragon-Blooded by the same amount that they would
have raised it if she’d had a “proper” child. As a child’s
behavior is often seen as reflecting upon her parents, some
families will go to great lengths to hide their children’s
indiscretions, applying punishment privately. Should
poorly bred children happen to Exalt later in life, this
stigma vanishes as quickly as it came.

By contrast, a male Dragon-Blood who sires many
children out of wedlock receives no stigma to speak of. It
is not as though the children so produced will render him
unable to father again for six years, after all; house elders
tend to see this as a chance for the father to sow his wild
oats. Just as long as he doesn’t make his wife wear the
pillow too often, the worst he’s doing is breeding more
lieutenants for the legions.

Officially, the stigma of mixed breeding does not
apply directly to children produced by such liaisons; it
wasn’t their fault, after all, that their parents did something wrong. In practice, however, it does spill over, simply
because the reputation of a Dynast is seen to affect the
reputation ofhis children and parents. In'ssome ways, alack
of stigma for br ing improperly i is almost worse than the

clothe them. They are expected to do their utmost to
obtain the best education for their children, particularly
as these efforts are seen as investments.

There are no laws preventing child abuse in general,
although killing or crippling a Dynastic child who might
yet Exalt is a severe crime. It’s unusual, however, for
parents to hurt their children — they don’t spend enough
time with them for this to happen, and it would be seen as
endangering their investments. They’re more likely to
harm a slave's child or a peasant’s child if they're in an
abusive mood. lfaservant were to harma Dragon-Blooded’s
child, she would be lucky to escape with adozen lashes, and
the imperial judges invariably see this response as justified.

Parents leave their children in the capable hands of
servants and mortal family members and otherwise leave
them to their own devices within the boundaries set by
those custodians. Most houses frown on public displays of
affection toward children, and Dynast parents are perfectly comfortable with that.

Parents are expected tokeep their children safe. Many
Dynastic children have larger contingents of guards when
leaving home than their parents do. The imperial courts
fine a parent who cannot protect his underage children.

Even after a child comes of age, Dragon-Blooded
parents have certain responsibilities. In the child’s youth,
they have a great deal of influence over the family’s
stipend-master and, through her, determining the size of
the stipend the Exalted receives as a youth. The income
comes ultimately from the house, not the immediate
household, but the parents have a large say in determining
how much the child gets. Parents often use this as a club to
get their children to behave, though the generation’s
stipend-holder is the final arbiter in such matters and
should take the house’s interests into account as well as the
parents.’ It is a mark of maturity to have the power and
influence to derive enough wealth from direct service to
the Scarlet Throne that they can do without a stipend and,
thus, exist independent of the house’s and one’s parents’
direct influence.

If their Exalted children wish to stay with them, the
parents must provide room, food and reasonable amenities
at a’ moment’s notice. They are expected to work to
provide their children with a suitable marriage. They're
also expected to use their contacts to help their children
find work. Children are expected to support their parents
in matters related to the house’s prosperity, and vice versa.

There are two ways of “disowning” a child. The first is
informal and is usually used when a child fails to live up to
his duty to support his house. It may be done at any age after
a child leaves primary school. This is often done to children who drop out of school, continue their travels well
past a reasonable date without getting anything useful to
the family out of them or fail to marry and at least attempt
to produce children within a century. This might also be
done to a child who repeatedly and publicly brings shame
and humiliation upon her parents.

A child who has been informally disowned may not
inherit from her parents, and they are freed of any obligations to her during the period of her disownment. Usually,
the process is not reversed until the child has proven that
she can behave asa responsible adult and contribute to the
well-being of the house. .

There exists a formal, legal process by which parents
may disown their children. It is almost never used. Whichever parent is Exalted must put the process into motion;
both parents must agree if they are both Dragon-Blooded.
The parents file a motion with an imperial court to have
their child declared outcaste. They must have the approval
of a senior member of the house to which the child
officially belongs. The judge must decide whether or not
the case has merit. If it does, then the Empress makes the
final verdict — or, in recent years, the Regent does so. This
is never done lightly, as the process is considered itreversible. It is only done if the child commits a grievous crime
or betrays her house or the Realm in some great way.


### Retirement


Even the Dragon-Blooded age and grow tired. Even
the Dragon-Blooded retire eventually.


### Retirement Age


There is no set retirement age, as the life span of the
Dragon-Blooded varies greatly. lt would be foolish to force
retirement on one of the Dragon-Blooded before his usefulness to his house or the empire had ended.

Retirement is entirely voluntary, but there are incentives to keep Dragon-Blooded working on behalf of their
houses. Once a Dragon-Blooded has retired, his family
income will not be increased again, even ifhe continues to
aid the house. For each decade of retirement in which he
does not continue at least minimal activities to the benefit
of the house, his family income will be reduced. In addition, the retired Dragon-Blooded will, of course, no longer
receive a salary for the job he performed. Thus, Dragon-
Blooded who have grown accustomed to a high lifestyle ~and haven't saved up often push back retirement as far.as °

might be given more and more dangerous jobs, until he
either gets himself killed through his own senility or decides
to back down gracefully. He might be loaded up with more
work in an attempt to push him into giving up. Or he might
be quietly offered a severance bonus, an under-the-table
payment to set him up happily in his retirement. It can be
a gamble to try to push one’s house into offering this
payment, and houses have occasionally chosen to avoid the
expense by hiring assassins.

By and large, however, Dragon-Blooded like to save
up a bit for retirement and retire before they're entirely
worn out. That way they can travel again, spend some time
with old friends, study subjects they never got around to
learning or get to know family members they never spent
time with before. Approximately one-third ofadult Dragon-
Blooded actually live to retire. The number would be lower
due to the dangerous lifestyle they lead, but some Dragon-
Blooded retire young. These are usually Exalted who made
their mark upon the world early and accrued a fortune for
themselves. A decent fraction of these retire into religious
service, joining the Immaculate Order and — in theory —
divorcing themselves from worldly cares.

It is possible for a Dragon-Blooded to come out of
retirement and back into an active life again. Most,
however, stay retired unless they’re offered a job that
they cannot refuse.

WHAT TO Do & WHERE

There is no set agenda for retirement, but there are
certain expectations. Dragon-Blooded are expected to
relax a little and enjoy themselves. They're also expected
to continue using their resources to the benefit of their
house. They aren’t t expected to work constantly at it the

For Dragon-Blooded who absolutely do not want to
deal with their houses in retirement, joining an Immaculate monastery is considered one of the very few acceptable
options. Others who are willing to do work for their houses
from afar have gone traveling, acting as spies or agents, or
gone monster hunting in their retirement. There are rare
cases of entire sworn brotherhoods getting back together
later in life and heading out again to find excitement.
These usually consist of those Dragon-Blooded who retire
early, for obvious reasons.

Some retirees simply slip gracefully back into the
welcoming arms of society life. Their socializing can be
considered helping the house, and they get to sit back, sip
fine wine and enjoy themselves. Fresh, just-out-of-school
Dragon-Blooded refer to these elders as “buzzards.” The
new adults either go out of their way to avoid the retirees
or out of their way to take advantage of them. Some retired
Dragon-Blooded enjoy stringing the inexperienced along.
They play games with them, letting the young Terrestrials
believe for a little while that they're getting what they
want and then either ruining the schemes, taking their
schemes over or otherwise getting the upper hand.

Other Dragon-Bloods retire to homes out in the
country. They relax, breed horses or dogs, explore the
countryside and hold lavish parties for old friends. They'll
often agree to host and wield their influence at parties for
the house in order to be able to keep their lives quiet and
undisturbed otherwise. Some houses use visits to these
relatives as cover for meetings they don’t want discovered.

A few more civic-minded Dragon-Blooded retire in
order to devote more time to helping their house in
non-job-related ways. Some may retire in order to focus
their attention on a promising protégée. This is usually
a mixed blessing for the protégée. Her mentor now has
more time to devote to her teaching and training, but
her mentor also has more time to devote to poking his
nose into her business. Such mentors often insist that
their students devote as much time to their studies as
their mentor devotes to teaching them, and up-andcoming young Dragon-Blooded rarely feel that they
have this much free time.

Still other Dynasts retire in order to devote more time
to secret or personal projects. They don’t want their jobs
toget in the way, or they want to work on things someplace.
where no one can keep an-eye on them. These Dragon..

### Funerary Rites


Funerary rites fall into two categories: the practical
and the ostentatious. Practical funerary rites are those
used when there is no good way to get the Dragon-
Blooded’s body home. These are usually performed when
Dynasts fall in foreign battles. Ostentatious funeral rites
are performed for those whose bodies are returned home
to their houses. Such rites may also be performed without
the body for Dragon-Blooded who died on foreign soil
and.could not be returned home.
~ ‘The practical rites (called “field rites”) involve, at the
very least, saying prayers to the Immaculate Dragons. Ifthe
Dragon-Blooded’s body could not be retrieved, then this,

combined with an all-night vigil, is the entirety of it. If the:
peat was retrieved but returning it home is greatly i imprac- ee

Dragon-Blooded whose bodies are returned to their
homes are given lavish, expensive funeral rites. Many
Dragon-Blooded gather together to mourn, and funeral
rites can last up toa week. Somber pageants depicting great
deeds performed by the deceased are performed. If the
family knows a talented playwright, it will probably pay
him to exaggerate the dead man’s deeds; this is considered
entirely right and proper to the occasion. Great speeches
are given lauding the dead lord’s character. It is believed
that exaggerating the reputation of the deceased might
somehow sway the opinion of the Dragons, causing him to
be reborn into a better station in the next life. Additionally, everyone knows that flattering the animal soul helps
satiate it and keeps it sleeping peacefully.

All-night vigils are held for the deceased, and some
family members make a great show of staying up for days
until they pass out from exhaustion, sometimes fasting as
well. Funerals are considered a time for displays of piety,
accomplishment and wealth. Like all Dragon-Blooded
social occasions, they're also times to make contacts and
manipulate political situations.

The funerary rites are important to sustaining the soul
in its journey to a new life, and so, the Dragon-Blooded
perform them for those they love. On a more selfish level,
those Dragon-Blooded who devoutly maintain days-long
vigils in honor of a deceased relative help to ensure their
own places in the next life, and they help to ensure that
they themselves will receive equally lavish memorials.


### Legends of Death


Officially, the Dragon-Blooded do not believe in
an afterlife for themselves. The Immaculate Philosophy says that their higher souls will be reincarnated
again and again, growing spiritually ever closer to the
Immaculate Dragons.

In practice, there are all sorts of superstitions.
There are little “what if?” questions that people quietly
ask themselves. There are odd “normally we reincarnate, unless this, this and this all happen” cases that
some of the Dragon-Blooded believe in. These superstitions vary from region to region.

Some Dragon-Blooded believe that it is possible for
their spirit to merge with the currents of Essence within
their Manse. Of course, this requires that they die there,

<!--PAGE 122-->

### Rites of the Brotherhoods


Members of sworn brotherhoods make each
other a promise when they join together — that
nothing except death will prevent them from
retrieving cach other’s bodies and making sure
that they receive at least full field rites. At one
time, entire brotherhoods would be lost trying to
retrieve their companions’ bodies, leaving no
one to return for their own. Asa result, a second
tradition developed. If a brotherhood had done
everything in its power to retrieve its comrades’
bodies and it was obvious that it could not
succeed without being destroyed, it could, instead, perform a Passage Rite.

This rite requires that che remaining members of the brotherhood perform the basic field
tites without the body as soon as possible. Then,
they must journey to finda parent, child, spouse,
lover or sibling of each deceased, preferably
someone the deceased cared deeply about or at
least spent a great deal of time with. To that
person, they must present five jade talents, any
personal items belonging to the deceased and
one item of importance to at least one member
of the brotherhood. In her presence, they swear
to always fight in the name of their fallen
comrade and to say a prayer for him at each
temple or shrine they enter.

Dynasts who believe in this recent legend work particularly hard to retrieve their comrades’ bodies in war.
Several monks have heard the Mouth of Peace postulate
that this particular tale was started by allies of the
Deathlords themselves, in an attempt to spread fear.

Almost all Dragon-Blooded know that an unmourned
and uncelebrated soul cannot move on. This is one of the
reasons for the popularity of lavish and elaborate funerals.
The larger the display, the easier it is for the soul to move
on toits proper place. For this reason, some members of the
Deliberative have recently suggested that a funeral should
be held for the Empress. Otherwise, if she’s dead, her soul
will be unable to move on along its path.

DRAGC ine AY

there might come a day when all of their efforts and talent
would be needed. This ensures that there is a constant
stream of well-educated, well-prepared young Dynasts
ready to join the machinery of power — and to assure its
continued smooth functioning.

However, education in the Realm also serves a social
and political function as well. The schools serve simultaneously as badges of station — a graduate of this school in
the Imperial City will lord it over a graduate of that school
in Chanos — and as means of association. Given a choice,
the graduate of the school in the Imperial City will
socialize with classmates and fellow alumni, and the shared
bond of time at a school serves as a means of introduction
within the numerous agencies and organizations that serve
the empire. Indeed, there are vast and ancient webs of
alumni connections scattered throughout the Realm, with
bonds between members nearly as strong as those between
offspring of one of the Great Houses.

Schools also provide a place for the adults to politic as
well. School functions provide an excuse for parents to
meet and discuss the business of the Realm. Schoolyard
friendships can grease the skids for alliances between
parents. Prestige garnered by students reflects well on —
and assists the aspirations of — their parents. The schools
are inextricably woven into the fabric of politics and
business in the Realm, and while everyone maintains a
polite fiction otherwise, the truth is well-known.

Finally, the educational process provides valuable
opportunities for the students themselves, not just to
learn, but to practice the skills and techniques that will be
used throughout their adult lives. Conspiracy, politicking,
manipulation, alliance-forming, matchmaking — these
are the meat and bread of the student experience at the
schools of the Realm, and they are talents that even the
dullest career bureaucrat will find useful.


### Competition at an Early Age


The political struggle of their parents informs the
actions of the Dynasty’s children. Every recitation and
concert is rated on two entirely separate scales: How well
did the child perform, and how weil did she perform
relative to the other children of wealth and privilege in the
district? A near-perfect'reciral of a difficult third- century
flute piece is worth nothi
in the Eye of Creation*
Suddenly, the near-perfe¢
to brag about, no way t

<!--PAGE 123-->
peers don't live exclusively through the deeds of their
offspring, stories of what the little ones have accomplished
lately do help set the social pecking order in uncertain
situations. Woe betide the first proud parent who brings
out the tale of how little Sextes did well at reciting a
memorized passage of the Immaculate Texts. By doing so,
he opens the door for other parents in the group to use their
children’s undoubtedly superior exploits as a means of
establishing dominance.

The more remarkable the child’s deeds, the better it
reflects on the parent, so it’s in each parent’s best interest
to trump the last anecdote. With the devaluation of a
child’s feat, so too does the parent lose standing. An entire
vicious game of social climbing can be played out behind
a bland discussion of precisely how far young Daana’d has
progressed in her lessons. There is an art to knowing when
to bring out the stories of one’s offspring. Parents are
almost comically eager to set forth their children as paragons but leery of doing so too soon and providing an
opening for a story that can trump theirs.

Wiser — and older — parents know better than to
make the first move in this game, and members of allied
families usually refrain from playing at all. However,
there's always a proud new mother who is entirely too eager
to discuss her offspring’s achievements — and who loses
considerable face to her elders and betters as a result.

Children who don’t provide sufficient material for
their parents’ discussion are often harangued and disciplined. “You have shamed the whole house by not doing as
well as...” isa commonly heard refrain, one which almost
inevitably inspires a powerful, lasting hate for the child
one is being compared to. More than one centuries-old
hatred between Dragon-Blooded has grown out of this sort
of unpleasant childhood memory, often with disastrous
consequences for the Realm as a whole.


### Early Childhood: Schooling in the Home


From the time they are old enough to speak until
their ninth birthdays, children of the Dynasty (and
those patrician families with delusions of grandeur or
Exalted Blood) are schooled at home in most exacting
fashion: Primary school begins at age nine, but a child
entering school is already:expected to be well-educated

start the process even earlier. The current vogue is to
start reading lessons to unborn infants in hopes that the
child will be well ahead of her peers from birth; evidence either way is lacking.

Once it is determined that a child is old enough to
begin schooling, the instruction begins at the knee of a
tutor. Most households have a single tutor for all of the
children who are not yet of schooling age, and in some
cases, several branches of a single family or several
families in a remote district will pool their resources and
share a single tutor. The goal from day one is to mold the
children into tiny replicas of their parents, young Dynasts
who will make their parents proud and be suitable additions to any school. Instruction continues year-round
unless the head of the household decrees otherwise, but
the inhabitants of the Realm regard the early years as the
most important for schooling and are loathe to waste a
single day.on frivolous pursuits.

### Going Off to School


Children are bundled off to primary school after their ninth
birthday. The occasion of departure
forschool isa festive one, highlighted
by a performance from the guest of
honor. Guests at these galas are expected to present the child with gifts
related to their schooling, anda child
of a powerful, wealthy or important
family can expect to receive pretty
much everything she needs through
the end of primary school.

Then, once the festivities have
ended, the child is sent off to school,
often accompanied on the journey
by at least one parent and a smattering of supplies, prized possessions and
delicacies. Tutors and slaves are left
behind, as the child is to be given
over completely to the school’s keep-

ing. Besides, there are no doubt
younger siblings or cousins on the
way, and they, too, must be prepared.
During the trip, the child is ex-

mind, but also to give her discipline, a work ethic and
respect for her superiors. A child of this age is expected to
be obedient, respectful, quiet and able to perform on
command. No more is asked of her, save that she prepare
herself for.primary school, so that she might bring honor
and prestige to the family name.

pected to be properly stoic, to look

at leaving home for the first time. Those who show undue

unbecoming and does not bode well for the future.

(usually around the 11th birthday or so, though some have
been known to Exalt as late as 17, and one child of Hause
Ledaal Exalted while his overprotective mother was saying
her goodbyes), she is immediately transferred to the other,
smaller portion of the school.

While most primary schools do not have Dragon-
Blooded on staff (except occasionally as senior
instructors or dominies), those that teach Dynasts are
experienced in dealing with Exaltations and their aftermath. A great many new Exalted are disoriented by the
process and have no wish to leave their friends and peers
for whatever reason, and they will lash out at anyone
who attempts to remove them from familiar surroundings. However, a teenager, even one with the power of
the Five Dragons in her, is no match for the nurses-cumsecurity guards. These handlers gently but firmly keep
the other children at a distance, ensure the safety of the
new Exalt and take her — by force, if necessary — to the
other side of the school compound.

The Exalted compounds at primary schools are
smaller, more compact and more stoutly constructed
than the rest of the school. Most times, they are located
at the center of the school proper, both to protect the
newly Exalted children from prying eyes and to protect
the surrounding area from the children. Some schools
give the Dragon-Blooded children private chambers,
while others prefer barracks-style sleeping arrangements
so that the older children can support the younger ones
as they realize what they have become. Same-aspected
children tend to cluster together at these times, even
though there is rarely a formal policy mandating such.
They simply find themselves quarters near one another
or swap bed assignments in a dormitory so that they can
be near others of their own kind.

After a sufficient cooling-off period, which can last
as long as two months in some cases, the new Dragon-
Blooded are allowed the same run of the school as any
other children. This means, inevitably, interaction between Dragon-Blooded and their former peers, which
can be unpleasant for all concerned. In some instances,
now-Exalted children use their powers to turn the
tables on former tormentors, something facilitated by a
word- of-mouth tradition ere the older children

<!--PAGE 129-->
attacks are also fueled by jealousy —~ jealousy that another
child Exalted and they did not.

In either instance, the school staff does its best to
end the violence as quickly as possible, and if a normal
child gets in a fight with a Dragon-Blooded one, it’s the
normal child who bears the brunt of the discipline. No
dominie wants to be the one to explain to a powerful
V’neef noble why his precious, Exalted child had a halfdozen teeth kicked in, even if said child worked diligently
to bring it on himself. Asa result, the children get quite
clever at finding ways to extract vengeance and inflict
punishment in private, when the adults aren’t around.
Creating a distraction on the exercise yard to draw off
adult attention from the real beating is one favorite; so
is sneaking into a rival’s room after curfew to administer
a silent beating. However, even the victims of these
assaults have one cardinal rule: They do not run to the
dominie or any of the teachers. The question “What
happened to you?” is always met with an answer such as
“T fell down the stairs” or “ | was exercising, and I fell.”
Adults are the enemy and a crutch. To ask for their
assistance is to admit weakness, and to admit weakness
is to concede that one has no business Exalting.


### The Daily Regimen

### Devotion


As the Immaculate faith is the official religion of
the Realm, it is unsurprising that the future leaders of
the empire are indoctrinated in it early. Each day
before breakfast, students are expected to study a
section of the Immaculate Texts. In some of the stricter
schools, students are not served their meal until they
can recite, perfectly, the day’s passage. Those who
cannot do so do not eat.

STupy

After breakfast, students march to their classrooms,
there to be drilled on all the arts and sciences befitting a
future noble of the Realm. Morning study includes mathematics, history, theology and proper etiquette. Such
classes tend to be relatively large and to stay together
throughout an entire morning's work. Generally, they
are grouped by age — all of the first-year students (or as
large a group as a classroom can support; larger schools
have multiple sections) learn the same curriculum, and
there are examinations at the end of each season to
ensure that the lessons have been learned well. At the
end of the examination period, the results are posted
publicly, the better to encourage those who did well and
humiliate those who did not.

The afternoon’s labors, after a break for lunch, cover
broader topics, such as musical instruction, poetry, singing, weapons, riding and public speaking. Afternoon
classes are smaller and, within limitations (i.e., the nineyear-old freshmen are rarely thrown into fencing classes
with the 15 year olds), are broken down into groups by
interest and talent, though there are numerous instances
where a parent insists on his offspring being taught
something for which she has absolutely no natural aptitude. Additional course options are open to students who
have Exalted: rudimentary sorcery, the art of controlling
their powers and so on. While most schools mix in
Exalted and unExalted students in at least the morning
sessions, the afternoons are segregated, and so are the. Seating arrangements in common areas suchas the dining
«hall. The Exalted children are never allowed to forget
what. they are,

and:neither are their peers.

### Cheating


Cheating is grounds for immediate expul-

. sion. The price of expulsion is high, as it severely

hinders one’s chances of acceptance at another

school and stands as a black mark on one’s record
for a very long time.

Asalways, however, the rules for this sort of
thing are somewhat elastic, especially if the
cheater’s parents have in the past made or
might make in the future a large donation to the
school’s endowment. In such cases, creative
approaches to examination-taking are ignored,
if not overtly applauded. A sufficiently clever
and original approach to cheating can actually
win the teachers’ admiration, if carried off with
sufficient panache. After all, the teachers reason, life is about finding answers as much as
necessarily knowing them.

calisthenics and then proceeds to a combination of
exertion (running, rope climbing, martial arts, sword
and archery practice and so forth) and games. Such
games are carefully supervised, especially if the school
allows Dragon-Blooded and normal children to mingle.
Some of the more prestigious and betrer-endowed schools
have two exercise yards, one for the normal children,
the other for their betters.


### Schedule


Six out of every seven days is devoted to the regular
classroom schedule. The seventh operates on a slightly
different timeframe. This is the day when parents are
permitted to visit (and'to:take their children off the
grounds). The morning is devoted to exercises or to. special
instruction in afternoon class material if. teache deem:

<!--PAGE 131-->
and the last official month of the school year is devoted
entirely to final, comprehensive exams. The last week of
any season’s schooling is also devoted to examinations; the
first to welcoming new students and setting class schedules. There are two days in between sessions for celebration,
relaxation and the posting of test scores. Other than that,
instruction proceeds relentlessly and without surcease.

is frowned up), each and every child must take her place

At graduation’s end, the children’s belongings are

often bribes to take good care of younger children who will

‘THE STAFF

It takes a particularly strong bunch of individuals to
deal with the cyclopean task of educating teenagers in the
Realm, letalone teenagers with the powers of the Dragon-
Blooded: The staff at a given school is a tight-knit bunch
of wary professionals, fearful both of and for their charges
but resolute in their determination to carry out their
appoinged task. The very intensity of the task of education


### Those Who Teach


The teachers are, unsurprisingly, the backbone of any
primary school’s staff. Some are Dragon-Blooded, and of
those, many are in “working retirements” from powerful
positions. The role of instructor to these children is a very
important one, and as such, the teachers are very well
compensated. In exchange, they are expected to achieve a
resounding level of professional excellence. Instructors are
Immaculate-trained at the very least, and graduates of a
school often come back to teach there. They are expected
to have encyclopedic knowledge of math, the natural
sciences, the history of the Realm, music, theology —
short, anything that a student might be expected to learn.
Most schools have rigorous examinations for their teachers in place, similar to those for entering the ministries, and
a failure may doom a would-be instructor to a sudden and
permanent career change.

Teachers have absolute control in their classrooms;
this is one of the conditions understood when the child is
accepted. No Dynast’s heir, regardless of their parentage,
is permitted to test the teacher's authority in the classroom. This is good training for later life, or so the theory
goes; knowing one’s precise place and rank is important in
the Realm. In actuality, most teachers are well aware that
someday their students will be among the great and powerful of the empire, and as such, the number of canings and
so forth is kept down, save for the truly incorrigible.

Each teacher is also assigned a number of students
or, in some cases, an entire dormitory hall, to oversee.
Duties range from impromptu counseling to breaking up
dorm fights to organizing group activities — mock
battles against other halls/dormitories/age groups being
a particular favorite.

Often, there is an Immaculate monk assigned to the
school’s shrine, to aid in the children’s spiritual instruction. Notall schools go this far, but many of the monasteries
actually encourage some of their younger members to take
up tasks such as this, as preparation for later days when
they'll be instructing initiates. These monks are provided
only with room and board, and their pay goes to the temple
from which they originate.


### Those Who Administrate

The administrative staff of each school is “ade: ‘up OF

<!--PAGE 133-->
word is law, and even the wealthiest and most spoiled
students are liable to learn this the hard way.

‘THOSE WHO SERVE

Primary schools also have numerous staff members
who are regarded by the students’ parents as part of the site
itself. The most important tend to the children — dressing
and bathing them, grooming them and otherwise tending
to their daily needs so that their young minds are freed for
the demanding task of study. It is unthinkable that a
student would make his own bed, lay out his own clothes
for the morning or otherwise attend to such unimportant
details. Most schools have one body servant for every five
or so students, though in special cases (particularly those
where additional funds have been allotted by doting parents) students get individual attention. Servants’ quarters
are near those of the students, though they are under strict
orders not to enter the student areas after curfew. The staff
is generally broken into those servants who work with
Dragon-Blooded children and those who don’t, and the
social disparity between the two groups of servants reflects
the rift between normal and Exalted students. The body
servants who work with the Exalted children are also those
who “rescue” the newly Exalted and, as such, are either
fondly remembered or despised as a symbol of the time
when everything changed.

Lower in the pecking order are the servants who tend
to the grounds, as well as the cooks in the kitchen. Such are
beneath the students’ notice, as well as their parents,’ but
they are still carefully vetted by the school’s dominie.
Youngas they are, the students at many of these schools are
still players:in the great game of empire, and the murder or
abduction of a student could disgrace the facility.
Groundskeeper, janitor, gardener — all of these provide
perfect cover for a would-be kidnaper or assassin. Some
schools use entire slave staffs, but this does little to alleviate the problem; it’s as easy or easier to use a slave as an
assassin —~ or to disguise a free citizen as one — as it is to
use a free woman.

All schools employ a large staff of trained and armed

guards. This: is as much to protect the school'from the...
children as it is to protect the children themselves, While
ere are not enough guards on hand to deal with’an«.*

DiscIPLINE

hough trained to deal with assassins or evenaréasonably.
