### Earth Dragon — Jt Etsubo


Earth Dragon martial artists specialize in the tetsubo,
a type of huge studded war-club. Use the stats for sledge in
the Exalted main book to represent this weapon. Followers
of the Earth Dragon Path may ignore the Strength penalty
and Accuracy penalty for this weapon, and they may halve
the Speed penalty.

Fire DRAGON — PAIRED SHORT SWORDS

Fire Dragons may use these weapons independently.
They can attack or parry with both swords using their full
pool. Attacks against separate targets must apply all normal penalties for two-weapon fighting. Attacking two or
more separate targets subjects the character toa-1 penalty
for her offhand. Otherwise, there are no penalties for using
a weapon in both hands.


### Water Dragon — Tiger Claws


Water Dragons ignore the normal Speed and Accuracy penalties for these weapons when using them.

Woopd Dracon—— Bow

In the hands of a Wood-aspected Immaculate, any
arrow adds +2 to its damage bonus.


### Switching Paths


Most of the time, Immaculates are trained in the
Dragon Path that fits the element that they are naturally
attuned to. Fire-aspected Dragon-Blooded usually just
naturally gravitate to the path of the Fire Dragon. Water
goes to Water. Like attracts like.

And yet, there are those who want to walk another
path. An Earth-attuned Exalt who, in some deep part of his
soul, hears the murmuring call of the Water Dragon or a
child of Air whose dreams are haunted by visions of the
vernal forests of the Wood Dragon — these Exalted
sometimes choose the road less traveled.

The masters of the Dragon Paths are reluctant to
accept such students. There is a price for following a Path
that does not fit in with a Dragon-Blooded’s elemental
attunement, and it is not a small one. Until they have
mastered the Dragon Path they choose to walk, these
sciples -f ‘pay the 1 mote non-aspect surcharge for all
Charms, even those of the Dragon Path they are re studying
and other Charms of their own element.: =.

THe PATH OF ELEMENTAL MASTERY

using any Charms of that element, even normal ones.
Characters who learn all:the Charms of multiple Dragon
Paths can ignore the penalty for the multiple elements.

### Tornado Offense Technique


Cost: 4 motes per attack
Duration: Instant

Type: Extra Actions
Minimum Martial Arts: 4

Minimum Essence: 2

Prerequisite Charms: Air Dragon Form

The powerful force of tornadoes can be tapped by an
Air Immaculate, turning her into a whirlwind of death ~~
at least for short periods. For every ¥ motes the Immaculate
invests in this Charm, she may make an additional martial
arts or ranged attack without any penalties. The maximum
number of attacks a character may make in a turn is equal
to her Martial Arts rating.


### Avenging Wind Strike


Cost: 3 motes
Duration: Instant

Type: Supplemental
Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Tornado Offense

Technique

By infusing an attack with a tiny bit of the gusting
breath of the Air Dragon, an Immaculate can send a
target flying backward. An attack boosted by Avenging
Wind Strike is rolled as a normal attack, and damage is
figured normally — with one extra effect. After being hit,
the target’s player must immediately make a reflexive
Strength + Athletics roll. The target is blown the Exalted’s
Martial Arts x 10 yards, -5 yards per success on the
Athletics roll. This effect occurs whether or not the
Immaculate’s strike actually does any damage to the
target. Obviously additional damage could occur of the
victim is blown off a cliff, into a lava pit, etc.

This Charm can be used with hand-to-hand or ranged
attacks. Many an opponent has laughed as a seemingly
harmless chakram sped toward her, only to suffer the wrath
of the Avenging Wind Strike.

"THUNDERCLAP KATA
Cost: 5 motes
Duration: Instant
Type: Simple
Minimum Martial Arts: 5
Minimum Essence: 3
Prerequisite Charms: Cloud Treading Method

The realm of the Air Dragon is the realm of the
storm, and its disciples can tap into the force of this to
belabor their opponents. After a moment of centering
and a few deep breaths, the Immaculate brings her hands
together. The resultant clap of thunder can stun and
deafen those who hear it.

Players of those within the Exalt’s Essence x 10
yards of the Thunderclap Kata must make a reflexive
Stamina + Resistance roll with a difficulty equal to the
character's Essence. Characters whose players get even
a single success manage to cover their ears in time —
but they lose any further action for the rest of the turn
as a consequence. Those whose players failed take
bashing damage equal to the Immaculate’s Strength +
Martial Arts, which can be soaked only with Stamina
and other non-armor protection. In addition, they are
also deafened for a number of turns equal to the
Immaculate’s Essence and, during that time, suffer a
penalty to all actions equal to the attacking character's
Essence from disorientation.

Obviously, this Charm does not affect those without
the need to hear — with one very important exception.
While normal beings are susceptible to the Thunderclap
Kata, it has an even greater effect on spirit beings. All this
Charm’s effects are doubled against spirits, whether materialized or not. The Immaculate does not need to be Spirit
Walking to gain this benefit and, in fact, does not even
have to know that a spirit is present.

The Dragon-Blooded is also immune to her own
Thunderclap Kata and may invest 1 extra mote per being
to insulate her compatriots from its effects. Spirits can’t be
insulated in this manner.

### Hurricane Combat Method


Cost: 10 Essence, 1 Willpower,
1 health level per turn

Duration: Martial Arts in turns

Type: Reflexive

Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 4

Prerequisite Charms: Wrathful Winds
Maneuver, Lightning
Strike Style

The Exalt becomes a whirlwind of speed and mayhem
for the duration of the Charm. While using the Hurricane
Combat Method, the Immaculate adds her Martial Arts
total to her initiative and all dodge rolls (use her Martial
Arts alone if she has no other dodge pool), triples her
movement and doubles her possible jumping height. It also
allows her to make at number of extra martial arts or ranged
attacks equal to her permanent Essence rating every turn.

The health level cost of this Charm is not paid until
the Charm has expired. The Willpower point and motes
must be paid up frontas normal. The charactercan end this
Charm prematurely at the end of any turn. Obviously, care
should be taken when invoking this Charm. Used by a
weakened Exalt, the consequences can be deadly.

SHATTERING Fist STRIKE

Cost: 3 motes

Duration: Martial Arts in turns
Type: Simple

Minimum Martial Arts: 4

Minimum Essence: 2

Prerequisite Charms: Earth Dragon Form

The raw power of the elemental Earth is fearsome to
behold. By channeling this power into his body, an Immaculate can create a great deal of destruction. This
Charm doubles the Exalt’s raw damage for the purposes of
destroying objects. This boost lasts for the character's
Martial Arts in turns. The Charm has absolutely no affect
on the amount of damage that a character does to living
things. The effect of Shattering Fist Strike stacks with
other effects, such as a Slayer Khatar’s, that increase a
character's damage against objects.

Great care is taken in training disciples in how to use
this Charm properly, as careless use can be dangerous.
Even though the Charm does not affect living beings,
improper application can cause damage to the character
indirectly. For example, if an Exalt were to use Shattering
Fist Strike on a pillar supporting the roof, he would
probably be crushed. The Storyteller should apply appropriate damage in these situations.

### Stillness of Stone

Cost: 3 motes
Duration: Special
Type: Supplemental
Minimum Martial Arts: 5
Minimum Essence: 3

With a precision martial arts attack, the Immaculate not
only strikes at certain critical nerve junctures, she instill a
small amount of Earth Essence into the target. The target is
paralyzed andcompletely unable toact forone turn perhealth
level of damage suffered in the character’s attack. If an
opponent is reduced below Incapacitated by such an attack,
he isnotonly killed, but turned tostone. The character’s body

ghastly trophy to the Immaculate’s martial skill.

### Avalanche Method

Cost: 5 motes
Duration: Until line of sight is
broken or the character
-is incapacitated
Type: Supplemental
Minimum Martial Arts: 5
Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Stillness of Stone

The Exalted invokes this Charm as he makes anormal
attack, raining a flurry of blows down upon his opponent,
driving her to the ground — or at least into some inferior
defensive posture.

If the character successfully strikes his opponent,
make a reflexive opposed roll pitting the Immaculate’s
Strength + Martial Arts against the target's Stamina +
Athletics. If the target exceeds the character’s number of
successes, she suffers normal damage but no additional
effect. Ifthe character ties or exceeds the target’s successes,
Earth Essence courses from him into his target’s body,
weighing her down as if beneath the crushing weight of an
avalanche. For every success he gets, the target suffers a
one die penalty toall physical tasks so long as the Exalt can
maintain line of sight to the target. These successes are not
cumulative if the target is subjected to the Avalanche

difficulty, reflecting the inconvenience of maintaining
contact with the target of the Avalanche Method.

Earth-aspected Terrestrial Exalted are totally immune to this Charm, although they may suffer normal
physical damage from the blow charged with it. This power
cannot be channeled through weapons.


### Earthshaker Attack

normal. See the rules on knockback and knockdown rules
on page 234 of the Exalted main rulebook.

PERFECTION OF EARTH Bopy

Cost: 10 motes, | Willpower
Duration: One scene

Type: Simple

Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 5

Prerequisite Charms: Ghost-Grounding Blow

This Charm is the pinnacle of the Earth Dragon Path,
representing the Immaculate truly becoming one with the
Earth Dragon — if only for a short time. The martial artist
drops to his hands and knees for a few moments, putting
himself fully in contact with the elemental Earth. The
Immaculate’s body twists and shifts, his skin becoming rough
and craggy, his body hardening and calcifying. The Dynast
becomes a being of living stone for the rest of the scene.

The affects of this are quite dramatic. The character’s
Strength is doubled by the powerful Earth Essence coursing through his body, and blows from the character’s fists
become deadly weapons, with Accuracy, Parry and Damage ratings equal to the character's permanent Essence and
a Speed equal to twice the character’s permanent Essence.
The character’s unarmed attacks do lethal damage. The
Immaculate’s granire-like skin also gives enormous protection, increasing his lethal and bashing soaks by his
permanent Essence score and allows him to soak lethal
damage with his entire Stamina. This protection is incompatible with armor but works with any other defensive
Charms that character may be using. The character also
ignores the penalties of losing health levels. He still rakes
the damage normally but functions as if unwounded until
incapacitated. If the character is wounded already when
invoking Perfect Earth Body, those penalties are eliminated, although the damage is not healed.

Fiery Hanp ATTACK

Cost: 4 motes
Duration: Instant
Type: Supplemental
Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Fire Dragon Form

A successful Firey Hand Attack does normal damage
plus the character’s Martial Arts rating, as the flaming
touch sears his enemy. The attack does lethal damage,
even if it wouldn't normally. Upon striking an opponent,
the Immaculate’s player also reflexively rolls the character's
Strength + Martial Arts versus the target’s Stamina +
Resistance. A being magically attuned to water may add
two dice to her roll when resisting this attack. If the target
wins or ties, the atrack has no additional effect on her
beyond the damage. [fa target loses, burning Fire-aspected
Essence is unleashed upon her, and she bursts into flame.

While it looks, smells and feels just like normal
fire, the flames from a Firey Hand Attack are partially
magical and can’t be doused normally. Only magic or
the expiration of the Charm kills the flames. If not
doused, a target set aflame will continue to burn for the
Exalted’s Essence in turns. Treat the fire as a bonfire as
per the environmental damage rules on page 244 of the

main Exalted rulebook — every turn, the target’s
player must make a reflexive Stamina + Resistance roll
at difficulty 3, the character taking 2L damage if the
roll succeeds or 6L damage if it fails. This damage is
soaked normally.


### Breath of the Fire Dragon

Cost: 1 mote per Essence
lethal damage

Duration: Instant

Type: Simple

Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Fiery Hand Attack

With a momentary centering kata, a Fire Immaculate can spit a gout of fire up to his Essence in yards. To
attack, roll the Immaculate’s Perception + Martial Arts,
with his Essence rating acting as automatic successes.
This fiery breath does the character’s Essence in lethal
damage per mote the Fire Dragon Immaculate invests in
the Charm. A character cannot invest more motes in this
attack than his Martial Arts score.

By expending a point of Willpower, the Dragon-
Blood can empower the Breath of the Fire Dragon to affect
spirits as well as material objects.

### Essence-jgniting Nerve Strike


Cost: 5 Essence, 1 Willpower
Duration: Instant

Type: Simple

Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Breath of the Fire Dragon

With a series of quick nerve touches, the Exalt actually causes the Essence in his opponent’s body to flare,
potentially causing a great deal of damage. The Immaculate makes a martial arts attack as normal, but ifhe hits, the
attack does no damage. Instead, for every mote currently
in a target’s Personal Essence pool, he takes one health
level of lethal damage, up to a maximum of twice the
Immaculate’s permanent Essence. This damage ignores
armor but may be soaked normally with Stamina and other
non-armor defenses. Obviously, this Charm is of limited
use against non-Exalted, but Exalts could potentially suffer
up to 10 health levels of damage. The target’s Essence
remains totally available, it simply serves as a catalyst for
the attack and is not reduced.

Flow REVERSAL STRIKE
Cost: 4 motes
Duration: Instant
Type: Simple
Minimum Martial Arts: 5
Minimum Essence: 3
Prerequisite Charms: Water Dragon Form

If the Dragon-Blood hits his target, for a fraction of
a second, every moving fluid in the target's body reverses itself. Needless to say, living creatures do not
react well to this.

The character makes an unarmed martial arts attack,
which does normal damage. The target’s player should roll
her character's Stamina + Resistance against the
Immaculate’s original attack roll to hit, unreduced by
attempts to dodge or parry it.

If the target does not exceed the Dragon-Blood’s
successes, the effects vary. Normal mortals and animals of
less than twice the Immaculate’s size failing the Resistance roll are killed outright. Exalted and other magical
beings take a single automatic level of unsoakable lethal
damage. Aquatic or amphibious creatures take two levels
of damage. Even if a target’s player succeeds at the
Resistance roll, she must subtract two dice from any
action involving physical activity for the Immaculate’s
Martial Arts in turns.

This Charm only works against living corporeal beings. Spirit, magical constructs and other non-living (or
unliving) beings are. unaffected.

### Bottomless Depths Defense


Cost: 5 motes, 1 health level

Duration: One turn

Type: Reflexive

Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Ghost-Restraining
Whirlpool Stance,
Crashing Wave Style

When this Charm is invoked, the Immaculate immediately takes a single health level of aggravated damage.
All other damage the Immaculate suffers for the rest of the
turn is negated, drawn down into the bottomless abyss of
the Water Dragon. This Charm may be used reflexively in
response to being attacked, but it must be invoked before
the damage for the attack is rolled.

Most walkers on the Water Dragon Path save this
Charm for very extreme circumstances. The benefits can
be high, but so are the consequences.

### Soul-marking Strike


Cost: 3 motes
Duration: One scene
Type: Supplemental
Minimum Martial Arts: 4

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Wood Dragon Vitality

To use this Charm, a Wood Dragon Immaculate need
merely caress her opponent, which results in a sharp pain
in the target’s head and no other obvious effects. If done in
combat, this caress must be part of a successful attack.

For the remainder of the scene in which the Soul
Mark is placed, any attacks the Immaculate makes against
the Soul-Marked target have an automatic success added
to the attack roll before the target’s player makes her
defense roll. Multiple soul marks are cumulative.

Soul Marking is especially painful to spirits — as
painful as a real brand is to mortals. In addition to the
effects listed above, each Soul Mark reduces a spirit’s dice
pools by—1 for the remainder of the scene, representing the
constant burning pain the being endures.

For 24 hours following the use of this Charm, an
Immaculate can sense when a being she has Soul Marked
is within her Essence in yards, and which being it is.

### Unbreakable Fascination Kata


Cost: 4 motes
Duration: Until abandoned
Type: Simple
Minimum Martial Arts: 4

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Wood Dragon Form

By executing a sinuous series of moves and undulations of the body, a Wood-aspected Immaculate can
fascinate and distract opponents. Those characters affected by the Charm are transfixed by the hypnotic
movements, standing rooted to the spot.

As longas the Exalt maintains the series of moves, the
player of anyone wishing to take any action other than to
stare in rapt fascination must win an reflexive opposed
check between her character's Wits and the Immaculate’s
Martial Arts + Charisma. While executing the Unbreakable Fascination Kata, the Exalt may speak, move at half
pace and dodge. Anything else, including an attack, breaks
the Charm. Many Wood Dragons use this technique to
draw close to an opponent before attacking.

### Spirit- Wracking Method


Cost: 4 motes, | Willpower
Duration: Instant

Type: Supplemental
Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Wood Dragon Form

When strikinga spirit, the Wood Dragon Immaculate’s
knowledge of spirit energy allows her to channel her own
Essence into the spirit’s form, disrupting it temporarily.

The Immaculate must make a successful martial arts
attack, either bare handed or with a weapon. This attack
affects spirits as if they were materialized and does normal
damage. If the Dynast actually wounds the spirit, her
player immediately makes a reflexive Essence + Martial
Arts roll with a difficulty equal to the spirit’s Essence
rating. For every success that the martial artist gets on the
roll, the spirit must subtract one die from all its dice pools.
This penalty fades at the rate of one die per turn.


### Spirit-rending Technique


Cost: 8 motes, 1 Willpower
Duration: Instant

Type: Simple

Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Spirit-W racking Method

Much like Spirit-Wracking Method, Spirit-Rending
Technique allows an Immaculate to strike a spirit being.
But the effects are far more deadly than simple disruption.

The martial artist makes a normal martial arts attack, whether armed or unarmed. If she hits, instead of
doing normal damage, make a reflexive Essence + Willpower roll. If the number of successes equals or exceeds
the spirit’s permanent Essence, it takes one automatic
health level of aggravated damage. If the number of
successes exceeds multiples of the spirit’s Essence, then
the spirit will rake multiple health levels of damage. If the
roll does not equal the spirit’s Essence, the Spirit Reng
ing Technique hi has no: effects: |

<!--PAGE 263-->

### Death-pattern Sensing Attitude


Cost: 5 motes, 1 Willpower + 1
mote per attack dodged

Duration: One scene

Type: Simple

Minimum Martial Arts: 5

Minimum Essence: 3

Prerequisite Charms: Wood Dragon Form

Once a Wood Dragon Immaculate is truly attuned to
the patterns of soul energy, she can perceive the patterns
of death as well —at least those directed against her person
by other beings.

For the remainder of the scene after invoking the
Death-Pattern Sensing Attitude, the Immaculate may
make a full dodge against any hand-to-hand attack
launched at her in a turn by a living being — or a being
animated by spiritual energy. This includes spirits and
the undead. This full dodge can even be used against
ranged attacks, so long as the attacker is within the
Immaculate’s Essence in yards. Each dodge roll costs the
Immaculate a single mote. The maximum number of
attacks that can be dodged in this manner per turn is
equal to the Immaculate’s Essence. This dodge does not
cost the Exalted her action for the turn and may even be
made after she has acted in a turn.

The Dynast does not have to be aware of the attack,
but the Patterns of Death do notalert an Immaculate to the

“CHAPTER SEVEN -

-< Storytelling

This book assumes you've already read Exalted and
understand the world of the Second Age and the types of
stories you can tell in that era. Consequently, this chapter
focuses not on the basics of storytelling, but on specifics, in
particular those issues that are unique to stories about the
Dragon-Blooded. While not wholly different from the
Solar Exalted described at length in the Exalted core
rulebook, the Terrestrial Exalted exist in a very different
milieu from the other Chosen; they are not rebels, but the
guardians of the Scarlet Empire. This means not only that
their Charms differ from those of other Exalted, but also
that the mood, themes and action of their stories differ as
well. The purpose of this chapter is to provide the Storyteller with examples and advice, so thathis Dragon-Blooded
series ‘takes full advantage of the qualities that set the
Terrestrial Exalted apart from their mightier brethren.


### The Dragon-blooded


ore-any examination of i iets

<piring, and the

insights he can exploit in the construction of Dragon-
Blooded Storyteller characters. After all, Storyteller
characters are one of the primary means through which
the players experience the world of the Second Age.
Todo that effectively, the Storyteller must understand
the place of the Dragon-Blooded within it.

As you'll see, many of the topics discussed in this section
foreshadow those described later under “Themes” and
“Moods.” That’s intentional. An enjoyable Exalted series
consists of an integrated whole, not just a collection of
individual parts. Therefore, you'll see plenty of connections
between sections. They’ve been put there to show how each
story element interacts with others to create a greater whole
— another valuable tool in the Storytellet’s collection.


### Frailty

One of those most oh

<!--PAGE 267-->
This frailty might seem like a drawback to using the
Dragon-Blooded in an Exalted story — and itis, at least for
the Dragon-Blooded in question. Yet, with that drawback
also come many dramatic opportunities. While heroism is
about excelling one’s peers, drama also involves weakness.
In many ways, weakness is what truly distinguishes — and
ennobles — the efforts of the Dragon-Blooded. The least
powerful of the Exalted, it is nevertheless they who refounded the Realm, who fight the Fair Folk and the
Anathema and who hold back chaos. Without the monumental efforts of these Chosen, civilization might well
have fallen to the forces of destruction. Such an unfortunate turn of events may yet occur, which is why the
Dragon-Blooded fight to overcome their weaknesses and
protect the Scarlet Empire. Should they fail, the consequences would be disastrous for all of Creation.

Asa Storyteller, the relative frailty of the Terrestrial
Exalted is a blessing in disguise. Stories involving the
Dragon-Blooded can take on a desperate, even frantic
quality, a quality very conducive to feats of heroism and
self-sacrifice — the very stuff of great Exalted stories.
Moreover, that frailty encourages the characters to work
together for mutual benefit. Unlike other types of Exalted,
the Dragon-Blooded are keenly aware of their own precarious position and that of the Realm they defend. Thus, the
Terrestrial Exalted have even more reason to form sworn
brotherhoods and fight side by side in the continuing
struggle against their enemies. Indeed, even their powers
encourage this cooperation.

Furthermore, the Dragon-Blooded are easier for players to identify with — another important blessing. Since
the Second Age is a time of wonders and horrors, it can
seem distant, even unreal, to players of the game. The
success of any roleplaying game depends on the players
being able to suspend their disbelief and immerse themselves in the fictional world of their characters. If the
players can’t do that effectively, the series will fail.

Because the Dragon-Blooded are less like demigods
than other types of Exalted, some players may find them
easier to play as characters. The struggles of the Terrestrial
Exalted are indeed heroic, but their weakness brings them
down. to earth. Throughout human history, there have
en many similar heroic undertakings. Consequently,
players “might use these similarities to b idge the gap

courage and dedication to duty. When the Celestial Exalted became corrupt and threatened the stability of
Creation, the Dragon-Blooded rose up and overthrew
them. They did so not merely out of self-interest or greed,
but because they also believed it had to be done. All that
followed from that decision — the Solar Purge, the foundation of the Realm, the creation of the Wyld Hunt — had
its foundation not in ambition, but in the belief that they
had no choice. Their culture is a military one, focused on
shame, on duty to one’s nation and family and on respect.

From a Storyteller’s perspective, this provides nearly
endless dramatic possibilities. On the simplest level, the
Dragon-Blooded’s concern with standing in the eyes of
others creates ready-made hooks for stories. Asa Storyteller,
youshould never be afraid to appeal to the Dragon-Blooded’s
concer with face to initiate a story. This intangible and
elusive sense of personal worth is at the heart of Terrestrial
Exalted psychology and society. Unlike obviously mercenary and self-interested character types, the Storyteller can
easily manipulate the Terrestrial Exalted’s concern with
face. If a satrapy is threatened by the Anathema, for example, the Chosen governing the satrapy must respond or
lose standing in the eyes of her house and peers.

Face is the quality by which the Dragon-Blooded view
their place in Creation; it is the moral compass that guides
them in their actions. Face compels many Terrestrial
Exalted to defend Creation whatever the cost — that is
their raison d’étre. However, this doesn’t mean they'll act
foolishly in its defense. A proper concern for face demands
a careful weighing of the consequences of one’s actions,
both to oneself and those one wishes to save. As soldiers,
the Dragon-Blooded understand that needless self-sacrifice is a waste of assets. While concern for face isn’t an
excuse for the Storyteller to railroad the characters on
some fool’s errand that no thinking person would undertake, it is nevertheless a powerful dramatic tool. For
example, an appeal to a bonded circle’s innate sense of
personal worth might be one of the few reasons to expect
it to enter the shadowlands and fight the forces of the
Deathlords without giving thought to its survival.

- Despite the ferocity they exhibit in protecting their

Teputations, the fact is that many of the Dragon-Blooded
- fall short of the ideals of the Realm. It has been hundreds

<!--PAGE 268-->
easily be used to justify feathering one’s own nest as it can
be used to defend high ideals.

The lack of nobility in some Dragon-Blooded can thus
serve equally well as the basis for stories. Characters who
hold true to the highest ideals of the Terrestrial Exalted must
eventually come to the realization that not all of their kind
shares them. What do they do? They might also havea rude
awakening when they enter the Threshold or Scavenger
Lands, where they may find humans who view the Dragon-
Blooded as contemptuous hypocrites who use their powers
for petty self-aggrandizement, rather than the defense of
Creation. How do the characters react? This constant clash
between cold hard reality and the demands of honor set the
Dragon-Blooded apart from other Chosen and can be the
source of innumerable stories.


### Decadence and Sophistication


Two obvious characteristic of the Dragon-Blooded are
their decadence and sophistication, both of which lend
themselves well to drama. Although the Second Age is a
time of wonders and potentsorceries, italso one of decadence
and decay. The mighty achievements of the golden First Age
have long since passed away, lost to the mists of time. What
lictle of those glories remains rests in the hands of the Dragon-
Blooded and theirallies. Thissituation presents the Storyteller
with many possible avenues of adventure.

The Terrestrial Exalted maintain the Realm in part due
to their understanding of its First Age wonders, its defense
systems, rain machines and warstriders. In many cases, their
understanding is incomplete, even fragmentary. Yet, that
understanding surpasses that of almost every other group in
Creation, giving these Chosen a unique advantage over
their foes — one that compensates them excellently for
their physical weaknesses. The Dragon-Blooded see themselves as the guardians of the First Age; some even believe
they're ushering in a newer and better age.

The Dragon-Blooded’s greater understanding of First
Age wonders makes it imperative that they become involved when these artifacts make an appearance. Even in
cases where the possessor of these wonders wishes nothing
ill toward the Realm and its inhabitants, his ignorance of
the artifact’s workings alone could cause untold damage. It
does not‘require a First Age weather machine in the hands
of thé ‘Anathema to devastate the Realm, only such a
machine in the hands of someone who has no idea how to

<!--PAGE 269-->
much a part of the Dragon-Blooded life as battle drills —
aremarkable contrast to the hand-to-mouth squalor of the
world outside the Realm.

An astute Storyteller should have no problem using
this aspect of the Dragon-Blooded as a source of great
stories. Like face, the Terrestrial Exalted’s sophistication
is a double-edged sword. There is little question that the
Dragon-Blooded perspective is indeed correct. They are
the most cultured, sophisticated and elegant beings under the heavens. Yet, that culture, sophistication and
elegance masks the rot that is at the core of the Realm.
Without the guidance of the Scarlet Empress, the Great
Houses and their Dynasts see only opportunities for selfagerandizement, even at the expense of the Realm’s
security. Whereas the Dragon-Blooded war against the
Realm’s external enemies with sorcery and the sword,
they war against one another under the cloak of refinement. This gives the Storyteller many opportunities to
involve the characters in bids for personal power, whether
as instigators, targets, allies or hapless dupes caught in the
middle of Dynastic politics.


### Authority


The final element that sets the Dragon-Blooded apart
from their Celestial counterparts is the authority they
wield within the Realm. The Terrestrial Exalted rule the
world, and no one should forget that — including the
Storyteller. The Dynasty is the Realm; the Dragon-Blooded
are its face, its mind and its hands. Thus, what a sworn
brotherhood of Terrestrial Exalted does reflects on the
Realm, for good or for ill. That alone provides plenty of
story possibilities. At its most basic, this authority means
that Dragon-Blooded stories can and should involve more
delicate politics and conspiratorial plots than stories involving the Solar Exalted.

The Dragon-Blooded justify their actions by claiming
they’re only doing what must be done. Even when what
must be done isn’t morally dubious or otherwise questionable, it can still be a bloody business. Keep in mind that
most Terrestrial Exalted are almost totally guiltless about
this. They ruthlessly exploit Creation because it is their
au They: are its overlords and need i its strength todefend

which doing so makes perfect sense. The T:

Blooded would probably just kill such a mouthpiece

fashion, since the Chosen’s claim to authority is an -

axiom to them. Show instead the long-term negative
effects of high-handed behavior. If the resentment, political instability and economic dislocation of toppling
the government aren't enough to persuade the Dragon-
Blooded not to, giving them a lecture isn’t going to
convince the players, it’s just going to sound like the
Storyteller lecturing them through a mouthpiece.

Atthe same time, this isn’t the only approach one can
take toa Dragon-Blooded series. Indeed, it may not be the
best approach in all cases. Bear in mind that part of the fun
of playing the Terrestrial Exalted is in being in charge.
Unlike the Solar Exalted, who must hide themselves lest
they be destroyed by their foes, the Dragon-Blooded proudly
stride across the earth, their power manifest for all to see.
Most of their kind truly sees the Realm as Creation’s only
hope for order and security. They look upon their role in
protecting and upholding that hope as a trust given to
them by the Elemental Dragons. Since they take their role
seriously, they expect others to do the same.

In story terms, this means the Dragon-Blooded expect to be treated with deference by those socially beneath
them. For many players, this is incredibly enjoyable and
as Storyteller, you should try to use it to good effect. Many
games place the characters at the bottom of the social
hierarchy, cruelly battered by Fate and their social betters. A Dragon-Blooded series operates ona very different
principle. Even beginning Terrestrial Exalted are important people. True, they are not generals or the leaders of
the Great Houses, but they are nevertheless representatives and agents of the Realm. That alone makes them
more important in the empire’s social hierarchy than the
mightiest ordinary human — never mind the Anathema
who cower at its edges.

A good Dragon-Blooded series takes full advantage of
these Chosen's unique status as agents of the Realm. As
noted in the previous sections, the types of adventures the
Terrestrial Exalted undertake differ greatly from their
Celestial counterparts. Instead of undertaking furtive ex-

ploits on the fringes of Creation, the Dragon-Blooded are
“merchant..princes, diplomats, adventurers and_ military

officers, an heir: exploits are totally out, in nthe open.

<!--PAGE 270-->

### Dealing with Unpleasantries


The Second Age is a brutal time. Slavery, Dynastic intrigue and the systematic oppression
of “lesser” peoples — never mind the purge of the Celestial Exalted — are all standard
operating procedure for the Realm. Consequently, it’s pretty easy to make the Realm out to be
the bad guys of Exalted. After all, the Dragon-Blooded routinely accept a variety of practices
we in the 21st century believe to be evil and immoral. So, how is it possible for anyone to play
in a Dragon-Blooded series without being repulsed by these practices? How can you paint the
Terrestrial Exalted as honorable, upstanding men and women when they accept slavery and
oppression as facts of life?

There are two approaches to this problem, neither one of which is strictly superior to the
other. At the same time, it’s your game, so feel free to pick and choose from among the options
offered here. The first approach is the simplest: let the players’ own moral sense “intrude” into
the game setting. In this approach, the Storyteller doesn’t ask the players (or their characters)
to accept slavery, for example, as just another facet of Second Age culture, like the Immaculate
Order or sorcery. While this isn’t the most true to the setting, there’s nothing wrong with that.
In fact, use the players’ sense of moral outrage as the basis for telling thrilling stories. Our ways
are better and more enlightened, and if you implemented them in the Second Age, life would
improve. It’s just an unquestioned fact, and nobody has to contradict their most basic
presuppositions to portray a sensible character.

If possible, though, you should try to keep the game and reality separate. Simply because
what works in our world and time works in the world of Exalted isn’t an excuse for a Storyteller
or player with strongly held political views to bring those views into the game. If the player
wishes to do so — and is capable of exploring the issues surrounding them without rancor —
it can be the basis for roleplaying of the highest caliber. The characters, for example, could be
crusaders against the practice of slavery, defying tradition and their own superiors in an effort
to fund an “underground railroad” to freedom in the Threshold. Alternately, they could
attempt to change the Realm’s treatment of outsiders, seeking détente with the satrapies, the
Scavenger Lands or even the Anathema. On the other hand, if this is going to cause conflicts
within the player group as personal assumptions clash, then it’s probably best to just avoid
political issues altogether. While this first approach softens the hard reality of the Realm, it’s
one that may be more to the liking of players uncomfortable with the harsher elements of
Exalted.

The second approach is the realistic approach, in which the Storyteller presents the setting
and all of its elements in full. In this way, the Realm can be seen for what it is: a brilliant,
glorious empire built on the backs of the oppressed. This game doesn’t really make any
assumptions about right and wrong at all. In this approach, the Realm is no different than
ancient Athens or the former British Empire or modern America, a well-meaning but flawed
state riddled with contradictions and falling far short of its highest ideals but still achieving
something unique and wonderful. Though it has flaws, the flaws don’t subtract from the
heroism and sacrifice displayed by its finest sons and daughters.

The realistic approach isn’t about whitewashing the Realm’s atrocities so much as it is
about accepting that “that’s the way things work here.” The Second Age isn’t our own; it’s only
natural that it possesses many customs and traditions we find repulsive and morally objectionable. At the same time, these customs allow for the creation of a society that defends the whole
of Creation against the depredations of foul beings who would bring the world crashing down
around them. Does this justify the Realm’s actions? Do the Terrestrial Exalted even need
justification to do whatever they must to keep the world as they know it from ending? This all
depends on the perspective of the individual character, and how each assesses the strengths and
weaknesses of his society. Obviously, the downside is that many people aren’t going to be
comfortable playing characters who blithely live in a world whose foundations are economic
exploitation, power-politics and religion as a social control.

<!--PAGE 271-->
"THE PRELUDE

Like any other kind of Exalted, the Dragon-Blooded
begin their lives as ordinary mortals, lacking the godlike
abilities that distinguish their kind. However, these Chosen begin life knowing that there is a good chance they will
one day be Exalted like their parents, grandparents and the
many generations that preceded them. The offspring of
Terrestrial Exalted are more likely to experience the
Second Breath than any other humans, for the blood of the
Terrestrials breeds true.

The basic principles behind the prelude are described
in Chapter Three of the Exalted core rulebook, so there’s
no need to go over them here. The Dragon-Blooded are
raised in an environment where there is every expectation
that they will be Exalted. Unlike the Solars, for whom
Exaltation is always a surprising, even frightening occurrence, the Terrestrial Exalted spend much of their childhood
anticipating it. Indeed, they spend much of that time
preparing for it. The educational system of the Realm is
structured to give young Dragon-Bloods the skills and
knowledge they need to prepare for the Second Breath.

The self-confidence of the Terrestrial Exalted stems
from the near-certainty of their following in their parents’
footsteps as Exalted. How would that affect play in the
prelude? Was the Dragon-Blood a studious, dutiful sort
who believed he needed to do everything within his power
to prepare himself for Exaltation? Or did the near-cer-

ainty of it leave the Exalt without ambition or the need to
prove himself? By playing out the prelude, the Storyteller
gains insight into the characters who'll be the protagonists
in her series. This is vital knowledge that will undoubtedly
pay off later in the form of more meaningful stories. When
it comes to effective Storytelling, the more you know
about the characters, the better the stories you can tell.
Preludes go a long way toward providing that knowledge.

Equally important is the character's reaction to the
moment of Exaltation. How does he deal with this sudden
tush of power? How does it change him? Was the Second
Breath everything he’d expected, or was it somehow different? By understanding what the character expected of his

Exaltation — and what he experienced — you're ina better

ition tocreate stories that matter to him. For example, a

Exaltation finds himself suddenly in possession of human
abilities that he is expected to wield in

Ifhe accepts it, does this involve a sudden te realizat
is his duty t to do so, or does he serve : the Hed

ragon- -Blood who'd never given much serious thought'to =,

é > defense of the:

Alternately, a newly Exalted Terrestrial may be well
prepared for the physical consequences of the Second Breath.
Dragon-Bloods do spend much of their youth awaiting this
occurrence. What may catch anew Dragon-Blood by surprise
are the social consequences that result. This doesn’t include the
envy and even fear unExalted Dynasts show toward their
Exalted brethren — Terrestrials expect that and even relish
it. It’s affirmation they’re the Chosen of the Elemental
Dragons, a superior breed. The social consequences are that
the character becomes the focus of his society. The Dragon-
Blooded really do rule the Realm, and the rest of their lives
will be spent politicking about how best to apply their power
to further their house and their Realm. Politics can banish a
Dragon-Blooded to places he would never choose to go, and
necessity can likewise summon him back. This gives the
Storyteller excuses to send a new Dragon-Blooded character
wherever she wishes, provided she can explain it within the
context of the setting. How a character deals with the
isolation and demanding schedule of an Exalt, especially ata
young age, will have an important impact on the character's
overall personality. Does he spurn responsibility and wander
the Threshold as an adventurer in a sworn brotherhood, or
does he embrace it and work inside the Imperial Service?

More so than any other kind of Exalted, the Dragon-
Blooded focus on the past and family. Theirs is a world of
service and sacrifice for the good of the Realm — and the
Terrestrial monopoly on power. It’s appropriate to focus
heavily on the prelude. Compared to the sudden reappearance of the Solar Exalted, the Exaltation of the
Dragon-Blooded is not an event that ends one life and begins
another, it’s an accepted — and expected — part of life. How
they were before that event and how it changed the Dragon-
Bloods’ relationships with people around them are critically
important to understanding the characters’ motivations.

‘THEMES

Themes are the underlying ideas or concepts that
drive the action of any story. This section describes several
of the most important themes for Dragon-Blooded stories,
as well as ways to portray each theme in a positive or
negative way, depending on how the Storyteller wishes to
use them in his series.

. Adventures The: ‘Dragon- Blooded are heroes — at

<!--PAGE 272-->
Most “adventurers” are profiteers, would-be founders of a
Great House or insensitive cultural imperialists.

Power: The Dragon-Blooded are leaders of men.
They control the most powerful empire in all Creation and
defend it with all their might. Yet, that power comes with
aresponsibility on both a personal and public level. A good
theme for stories is an examination of how far the Chosen
will go to maintain power — for both themselves and the
Realm they represent.

Positive: Characters undertake their missions using
whatever resources are necessary to succeed, but they
remember that a misuse of those same resources could
ultimately undermine both the Realm and themselves.
They are sensible, industrious and probably environmentally conscious.

Negative: Characters do whatever they want they want
to ensure their success, never worrying about the long term
of their actions. They are Princes of the Earth.and do

faithful to the Immaculate Philosophy, or have centuries of
power inured them to theirown excesses? The responsibility
the Dragon-Blooded owe to the Realm’s people is an excellent framing device for stories in the Second Age.

Positive: Characters know their place in society and act
with that knowledge, giving proper respect to their superiors
and remembering to protect those under their care.

Negative: Characters are more concerned with the
respect others owe them than with their own duties toward
those beneath them. All power and profit in the society flow
upward, without reciprocity. Characters see the social hierarchy as inviolable and will not bend it even for a just cause.

Time: The Second Age differs greatly from the First,
and there are signs that the world could change even more.
This turning of ages is an‘excellent theme for stories. °

Positive: Great change’ is:
understand and channé
tebuild the Second Age

<!--PAGE 273-->
thinking or be swept aside as the Celestials were before
them. The tension between tradition and innovation is
another excellent theme for stories.

Positive: Respect for the past ensures that true wisdom
is not lost with those who discover it, but it does not mean
a neglect of the present or the abandonment of progress.

Negative: All that is worth knowing has been discovered and passed on to the present. It is best to stick to the
old ways because their surety is certain, while new ways
bring only chaos and disruption.

Triumph: Success is a friend to the Terrestrial Exalted. The Realm has known nothing but triumph for
centuries. With the disappearance of the Scarlet Empress,
that’s starting to change. How do the Dragon-Bloaded
deal with this, and what effect does it have on their notions
of face and authority?

Positive: Victory is the goal of characters, the crown of their
achievements, but sometimes, it is the striving for it that offers
the richest rewards, and even partial victory is worthwhile.

Negative: Total victory is the only worthwhile goal.
Anything else is worthless, it is not partial victory but
partial failure, an acknowledgement that a character has
somehow slighted himself and those who depend on him.

Moops

Whereas themes provide the underlying structure of
the series, moods set the tone and style for individual
stories. By varying the mood, you can make two stories
ostensibly about the same subject (war, for example) feel
very different. This is very important in games that focus
on the same themes for many sessions. Varying the mood
can help maintain a feeling of variety in the story. A few
of the most common Dragon-Blooded moods are discussed
briefly below, along with examples of plot elements that
bring these moods to life.

Excitement: The world of the Second Age is brimming with danger and tension. That makes it a compelling
place, filled with opportunities to prove oneself. Emphasize the heady rush of doing great deeds in the name of the
Realm. The Dragon-Blooded are heroes, after all, and
players. enjoy feeling the same heart-pounding thrills as
their: characters.

» Examples: A footrace through the streets of the Impe-
|:City, an avalanche falling on the chatacters.as they
climb a mountain, the doorknob turnin ascharacter
rifles through the minister’s desk, a barbari
ing a hill. 2

Exoticism: The Second Age i is also a tim

should bring that fact home. Hi
toms and dress of the Threshold

Examples: The sight of the Lap as the sun sets, the
increasingly large trees near the Elemental Pole of Wood,
the elaborate welcome rituals of Rubylak.

Mystery: The glories of the First Age have been lost.
Even among the Dragon-Blooded, who retain much knowledge, they are poorly understood. Finding a First Age
artifact is an occasion for mystery. The world outside the
Realm remains unknown. In its shadows lurk the Solar
Anathema, the Deathlords and their allies. The characters
should feel that there’s always something they don’t know,
something that needs to be discovered.

Examples: A disembodied voice providing valuable
information, the disappearance of an important Dynast,
the discovery of a tomb beneath a forgotten ruin.

Romanticism: The world of Exalted is a romantic
one, meaning that common events are often meaningful or
melodramatic. With the Celestial Exalted, their powers
often make the characters extremely primal, with little or
no concern for mortals and the mortal world. Dragon-
Blooded are better-suited to have one foot in the prosaic
world, The juxtaposition of day-to-day life in an oftenharsh world with the dramatic concerns of melodrama can
make the game sweeping instead of just weird. It’s a much
more meaningful event to marry a spirit or fight a
shapeshifter when nobody else you know has done it.

Examples: Peasants revere the Terrestrials as divine
heroes, fighting a duel for the bed of a queen on the
battlements of a Threshold fortress, a flight over stunningly beautiful, incredibly rugged mountains to escape a
conspiracy of one’s political foes.

Violence: The Second Age is a brutal world in which
the power of the sword is often the final answer to any
disagreement. Beneath its more genteel veneer, the Realm
is a military dictatorship in which the Dragon-Blooded
hold the top spot. Violence — or the threat of it — should
hang overhead in most stories.

Examples: Brutal pitfights and gladiatorial combat or
war against a pack of Lunars intent on ravaging a military
outpost, a defeated general decimating his legions as punishment for their cowardice, a victorious army putting
prisoners to the sword.


### Conflicts:


The ‘clash. of id als and. factions is the engine that
drives adventu nd‘Age. This:section deant conflicts that) may crop

<!--PAGE 274-->
in house disputes may find themselves tugged in one direction by familial ties and in another by duty to the Realm.
Terrestrial versus Celestial Exalted: The battle between the Dragon-Blooded and their Celestial counterparts
is an old and vicious one. The Terrestrial Exalted have
never forgotten how the Solars abused their power and
brought the Realm to the brink of extinction. They see the
Contagion as divine punishment for the Solars’ sins beneath Heaven. The Solars see the Dragon-Blooded as
usurpers, who coveted the Solars’ power and did whatever
they could to seize the Solars’ place. Now that the Solars
have begun to return, the old wounds have reopened,
bringing back centuries-old pains. This conflict has the
potential to ruin the Realm along with the potential to put
ancient feuds to rest —a great source of drama in any game.
The Realm versus the Scavenger Lands: The Realm
may be the most powerful empire in history, but its position
is no longer as secure as it once was. Rivals and upstarts —
some of them aided by the Anathema — have arisen,
upsetting the balance of power. In the days before the
disappearance of the Scarlet Empress, military action alone
would have been sufficient to end their threat to the Realm.
Now, diplomacy is required as well. The Dragon-Blooded
must accept that there are new rules and new realities. The
Scavenger Lands present a dynamic, chaotic counterpoint
to the stolid decadence of the Realm. Storytellers shouldn’t
hesitate to play this up to dramatic advantage.
Dragon-Bloods versus Deathlords: More sinister are
the Deathlords, agents of the Malfeans imbued with powers
equal to that of the greatest Exalted. At present, they exist
only on the edges of Creation, but that is changing. In the
years to come, the Deathlords will challenge the Realm as no
enemy has before. Without the unity formerly enforced by the
Empress, the Dragon-Blooded may not be up to the task.
Exalted versus the Fair Folk: These beings from
outside Creation are threats not just to the Realm, but to
all living things. Their ways are wholly unlike other
creatures and their glamour makes them difficult to negotiate with. Defeating the Fair Folk requires coming to grips
with their strange ways and character, which provides the
Storyteller: with another chaotic counterpoint to the ordered society and culture of the Realm.


### Types of Stories

A Dragon-Blooded series can be as wide ranging or as
he players and the Storyteller desire. In this

While none of these stories is unique to the Dragon-Blooded,
they nevertheless epitomize the qualities that set these Chosen apart from other Exalted. More importantly, they provide
excellent starting points for players and Storytellers who may
still wonder how to construct a Dragon-Blooded series.


### Intrigue


If the Dragon-Blooded are the heart of the Realm,
then intrigue is its lifeblood. The Terrestrial Exalted’s
current position as the rulers of Creation derives from their
cunning overthrow of their Celestial brethren. Likewise,
the Realm’s success over the centuries is the result of the
Scarlet Empress’ many schemes, playing one faction off
another for the benefit of the whole. The Dynasts, generals
and bureaucrats of the Realm have emulated their liege for
centuries, creating a society in which duplicity and deception have become a way of life.

Stories involving secret plots and hidden schemes are
well suited to the Terrestrial Exalted. Almost from birth,
Dragon-Blooded leam how to work behind the scenes,
manipulating friend and foe alike to attain advantage for
themselves and the Realm they serve. With the disappearance of the Scarlet Empress, these skills have been put to
the ultimate test. Nearly every faction and leader within
the Realm is making a grab for power, hoping to come out
on top. This situation can make for exciting stories of
politics, self-interest and high ideals.

How do these currents and plots play out in the course
of a Dragon-Blooded story? Before this question can be
answered, the Storyteller must answera few more basic ones.

WHAT Kinb OF INTRIGUE?

Intrigue covers a lot of ground. It’s about secrets and
schemes hidden from prying eyes. But what sorts of secrets?
Who wants to hide them — and why? The Dragon-
Blooded function as the Realm’s politicians, soldiers and
priests. All of these factions have plenty of reason to
become involved in intrigues, if only to defend their own
interests. Yet, a general's plot to instigate a border incident
in the Threshold to increase his own position is not the
same as a Dynast seeking to forge an alliance between her
house and another. As Storyteller, you first need to think
about the kind of i intrigue. you'd like to explore within your

deal with overt
achinations, it’s:

<!--PAGE 275-->
where the plotter wishes to achieve a goal that is only
slightly at variance with what he appears to be seeking.

Secrecy has two levels, the “exoteric” and the “esoteric.” Exotericsecrecy involves covering up public activities
and motives, such as when a murderer attempts to throw
investigators off the trail or when a politician claims his
support fora particular course of action is not self-interested,
but in the public's interest. Exoteric secrecy offers the easiest
entry for the characters, since they're aware of at least some
of the basic facts (a murder has been committed, a senator
counsels war), even they don’t know the whole story.

Esoteric secrecy involves covering up Hidden activities and motives, such as destroying evidence of smuggling
weapons to an enemy or embezzling from an illegal slush
fund. Esoteric secrecy is more difficult for characters to
enter because they have no public component whatsoever.
They operate completely behind the scenes. In these cases
(commonly called “conspiracies”), the plotter’s goal is
either so at variance with his public actions or completely
removed from them that he can only initiate it from
behind the scenes. No other option is available because
the plotter’s goal is so pernicious or obviously self-interested as to be unattainable any other way.

Military and religious intrigues follow patterns similar
to those of political intrigues, but they differ somewhat.
Because both military and religious matters are more practical than politics, there is usually an added level of real-world
impact to these intrigues. Attempting to gain power through
the manipulation of a prophecy from the Immaculate Texts
requires the Storyteller to explain the nature of that prophecy and how it could be used to serve a certain end — and
engender a mass movement. Likewise, a game of
brinkmanship on the borders of the Scavenger Lands will
have an impact on the Realm’s military situation. Thus,
these types of intrigue stories require a bit more effort at
scene setting if they’re to have any meaning for the players.

While most types of intrigue can ultimately be reduced
to power games, this isn’t true of all of them. Take romantic
or sexual intrigue for example. In this type of intrigue, the
object is generally not power, although it certainly can be.
Instead,: “personal fulfillment, whether: physical or emotional, is the goal. The character seeks out a love interest or

this end through secrecy and deception.

WHO ARE THE CHARACTERS®

of things. This includes theS
patrons, contacts and antagonists 16!

conquest and work methodically toward that end. Self-mo vated stories are sigple enough to. handle. They

Intrigue stories can vary a great deal, depending on the
role the characters play within the story, and some thought
needs to be given to this aspect of the plot. For example, if the
characters are agents of a Dynast seeking to enhance the
position of one of his clients in the Deliberative, a given story
will come together very differently than if they accidentally
stumbled upon the plot in Nexus. Both stories would involve
thesame elements, but the plotting and development of each
differs. In one, the characters can draw upon the resources of
a powerful patron. In the other, they're left to their own
devices, possibly flailing about in the dark as they try tocome
to grips with what's really going on. As Storyteller, you need
to consider how the characters fit into the intrigue story, as it
will have many consequences for the unfolding drama.

In a similar vein, Storyteller characters are another
vital element of this type of story. Patrons are a common
feature in intrigues. They’re the link between the characters and the secret goings-on of the world. Patrons can be
official government representatives, wealthy nobles or
meddling wise men. Whatever their circumstances, they
possess more knowledge of what is really going on but, for
some reason, are unable to act themselves. Thus, they need
agents — the players’ characters — to do what they cannot
or will not. The most important things the Storyteller
needs to know are how much a patron knows, how he
knows it and what he wants out of his involvement.

When devising patrons, it is important to avoid the
“glued to his chair" syndrome. That is, the unfortunate
tendency for powerful and knowledgeable patrons to turn
to.a bunch of no-consequence ne’er-do-wells to undertake
aquest whose ultimate conclusion he could have achieved
in five minutes had he put his mind to it. Why doesn’t the
patron act on his own behalf? Why does he need the
characters? If you don’t come up with a good explanation
for this type of question, the players will — often with
disastrous results. In intrigue games, players are prone to
see conspiracies and double-dealing everywhere. That’s
acceptable, but too much of it can derail the game. Always
make sure the patron’s actions make sense — to you, as
well as the players — before undertaking this type of story.

Ifthe characters have no patron, their involvement in
an intrigue story is either self-motivated or accidental.

<!--PAGE 276-->
Accidental intrigues require more work on the
Storyteller’s part than ones motivated by the characters.
Attheir simplest, the Storyteller must understand the true
nature of the intrigue and what went wrong so as to allow
the characters entry into its mysteries. Suppose, for example, there's a plot to kill a Dragon-Blooded general
who's visiting a city in the Threshold. An assassin has been
provided with the general’s itinerary and details about his
visit. If all went well, the assassin would simply lay in wait
and kill the general when he arrives. Unfortunately, a
random attack by a local cutpurse kills the assassin instead!
The players’ characters stumble upon his body and information linking him to the general’s visit. With that simple
act, they're drawn into an intrigue, as the assassin’s employers try to stop the characters from revealing their plan
or preventing its execution.

The example nicely illustrates the nature of accidental intrigues. The Storyteller must understand who has
hired the assassin (a rival general) and why he wants to
initiate this plot (to ascend toa higher position within the
legions). He also has tocome up with a plausible reason for
the intrigue to go awry (a random attack by a thief). These
things are easily done, but they do require extensive
preparation to succeed. For intrigue stories to work properly, the Storyteller needs to know as much as possible
about the characters and their actions. Armed with that
knowledge, he can tell some truly dramatic tales.

Another type of self-motivated story is one in which
the characters themselves are the patrons. Terrestrial
Exalted regularly employ minions to do their dirty work.
The characters are probably no different, especially if they
have Backgrounds such as Henchmen, Connections or
Allies. Stories where the characters are patrons can proceed much like other self-motivated stories, except that
the execution of every detail isn’t in their hands. Certainly, they'll plan the details, but it’s their allies and
followers who do the dirty work. That means it’s up to the
Storyteller to decide whether the minions succeed or fail
—and what the consequencesare for the characters’ plans.

Determining success or failure derives from two important considerations. The first is the plausibility of the
plan the characters have created. If it’s obviously a fool’s
errand without any chance of success, you don’t have
anything to worry about. If it’s intricately detailed with
every contingency mapped out in advance, it’s also pretty

ingat the £ ackgrouinds that§
y, for example; is"

<!--PAGE 277-->
Abilities) than a sketchily detailed one. It might be wise,
to avoid hard feelings, to let the players portray the plotters
as they go about their missions.

THE ViEW FROM THE GROUND

So how does this all play out in the end? The answer
to that depends greatly on the kind of game the Storyteller
wants to run. If intrigue is the bread and butter of the series,
intrigue stories should add depth and texture to this
hidden world driven by the secret wheels of vice, greed and
conspiracy. They should highlight the corruption of the
Realm. Every character has ulterior motives, yet few do
anything for reasons other than self-interest. In such a
world, betrayal is commonplace, even expected.

The Dragon-Blooded are certainly a scheming and
wily group of beings — they don’t rule Creation’s greatest
empire by accident! Yet, many Terrestrial Exalted also
believe themselves to possess high ideals and the best of
intentions. Don’t expect the characters to start off totally
cynical. Introduce elements of intrigue slowly, even in a
series wholly devoted to this type of story. Rather than
throw the characters immediately into all manner of
Byzantine machinations, a better way to begin a series is to
use a “standard” plot, such as exploration or a rescue, and
distort the motives of those involved.

The Dragon-Blooded don’t use intrigue when a more
straightforward approach would suffice. At the same time,
the Realm is a corrupt and decadent empire, so conspiracies abound. And when they do occur, the Storyteller
needs to come up with a good reason for their existence.
Why would someone, or some faction, go about achieving
his agenda through hidden means, often of a drastic and
immoral nature? Why couldn't he achieve them in the
open? You must construct plausible answers to these questions in order to create an enjoyable intrigue story.

An effective storytelling tool is to make the stakes
matter to the characters. As members of the Dynasty, the
characters have more than a passing attachment to the
Realm and its well-being. Thus, anything that concerns
the safety of the empire — and their place within its
hierarchy — is a valid starting point for this type of story.
Beyond that; though, a story needs to appeal to more than

<!--PAGE 278-->
More so than so than any of the other story types
described in this section, family ties touch on every character and every type of series. While they certainly won't be
the focus of most of them, the characters all must have them.
Every Terrestrial Exalted is a member of a Great House.
They and their kind are all intimately related to the regime
the Scarlet Empress founded. Even if they reject their role in
maintaining and defending that regime, they are still bound
to it by ties of blood and tradition. Relatives and family
obligations can intrude into almost any kind of series.


### Thicker Than Water


Many players will have some ideas about their characters’ families, and you should do your best to accommodate
them. Unless you have a strong reason for disallowing a
player from deciding that her father was a famous general
or that her cousin is a member of the Immaculate Order, let
her do so. Part of the enjoyment of any type of Exalted
series is in letting the players immerse themselves in
another world. By allowing them to make determinations
about their family, you’re giving them a degree of control,
which helps them to establish a connection to an otherwise unfamiliar setting. Besides, they're taking a little bit
of the work out of your hands, which is always a good thing.

The other factor to bear in mind is that Dragon-Blooded
families are large. Even after allowing the players to describe
some of their relatives (assuming they even wish to do so —
some players don’t), there are still many more to detail. Thus,
there's always room for the insertion of your own pet creations, the relatives that'll drive the plots of your
Dynasty-centered stories. Bear in mind that the Dragon-
Blooded, especially those directly descended from the Scarlet
Empress, are very long-lived. The characters, as young scions
of their house, are but the latest generation of their families.
Even their parents and the people running the household
they live in are probably in the middle of the house’s social
hierarchy. This means the truly powerful, influential and
ancient members of the house will likely remain undetailed
—~ the perfect fodder for the Storyteller as he plans the series.

With that in mind, you should create only as much of
the house’s social hierarchy as you need for your stories.
Since many Storytellers have no idea what they'll need
laterin their séries, here are a few guidelines for creating an
appropriate genealogy:

© Decide the house with which the family is affiliaced.
Unless Ou ntend the series to revolve around high-

® Determine how many generations exist between
the leaders of the house and the characters’ parents. If you
expect to kill off, disgrace or otherwise eliminate many
family members in the course of the series, choose a larger
number, since you don’t want to defoliate the family tree
completely. Remember, too, that the Dragon-Blooded
live 250 years on average when creating generations (which
should be between 30 to 50 years in length).

® Only name and provide details for family members
you expect to use immediately. The rest can be detailed on
a story-by-story basis as they become important. If the
players have ideas, use them ~~ either directly or through
the Family Background described in the sidebar.

Consider a plot involving the efforts of House Peleps
to forge marriage alliances between itself and House Sesus
as an example of these guidelines in action. If you've
already established the names and details of marriageableage Exalted in the family, you can use them. If you'd rather
introduce new characters, look for appropriate spaces on
the family tree and create new characters. That’s why it’s
best to leave blank spots here and there. It’s impossible to
know fully when and where you'll need a certain type of
family member, so erring on the side of too little initial
detail is better than having to create a “long-lost cousin"
at a moment's notice.


### Family Matters


Dynastic stories are outside the personal experiences
of most players and Storytellers. After all, few of us live in
large and influential families whose decisions have consequences not just for ourselves bur for the world at large.
This means we can’t just draw on our own experiences to
come up with ideas for plots and stories. Instead, we have
to turn to history, literature and even soap operas. Soap
operas provide plenty of inspiration for family-based
roleplaying games, with their large cast of characters and
the sometimes-incredible relationships they have with
one another.

The Great Houses are, of course, like the great noble
houses of Europe or Asia, huge clans of people, many of
whom are mutually antagonistic toward one another.
Strife and discord are commonplace, as is the presence of
factions within the clan.:But despite this, the house’s

Family-centered stories come packed with reasons for
the characters to become involved. When it comes to their

<!--PAGE 281-->
unique rewards, not the least of which is the ability tosee the
Second Age at earlier points in its existence. Instead of
being ancient history, the rise and decline of the Realm is
partof the plot of the series. It’sa difficult type of story to pull
off and requires planning to succeed.

Before beginning, the Storyteller needs todecide when
he will set his series. There are over 700 years from which to
choose. Each era in the Realm’s history offers different
challenges and rewards. A game set shortly after the Contagion, for instance, would likely revolve around the rebuilding
instigated by the Scarlet Empress. Meanwhile, a game set
during the 300s to the 500s would probably be about
governing the Realm’s far-flung territories. Thus, the Storyteller should first determine which themes and conflicts he
wishes to highlight and choose the era appropriately.

The real work of planning a multigenerational series
is in the plotting. All Exalted have extremely long lifespans.
Although the Dragon-Blooded are the shortest-lived of
the Exalted, their lives still last several hundred years. In
rare cases (typically sorcerers), a Terrestrial Exalted can
live much longer. From a Storytelling perspective, these
longlives lend continuity to the series, but they aren’t with
difficulties. How long will a Dragon-Blooded character
live? How much experience does he gain over the course
of a century or more of life? When you add the complication of children, the problems continue to increase. How
do you determine the nature of these children? How
powerful will they be? How long will they live?

The multigenerational game is an exception to the
way we assume Exalted will be played. Nevertheless, we
understand that Storytellers will want some guidance. The
following are some of the most important questions, along
with suggested answers.

How Lone Do Tuey Live?

Few Terrestrial Exalted live more than three centuries.
Unless there’s good reason to assume otherwise (special
Charms and martial arts disciplines, sorcery, wondrous items,
etc.), no Dragon-Blood character should live more than 300
years. Most live about 250 years. With 10,000 Terrestrials and

11 houses, that’s around 1,000 members of each house at any.

given time. Some houses, such as V'neef and Iselsi are small,

thing that could vsasonabe Galise d

explores a dangerous region, fete. )

This can be decided through roleplaying or by Storyteller
fiat. As Storyteller, try to strike a balance between arbitrary tragedy and lameness. It should be a worry that a character might die from mischance or strife but not a
given, and the Storyteller shouldn’t use it to eliminate
major characters without good reason.

The third way is through agreement between the
Storyteller and the player. Ifa character has led a long and
productive life, many players are happy to let them shuffle
off their mortal coil — especially when they know it’s all
part of the theme of the series.

How Do] Hanote Experience?

Characters typically gain 4 or 5 experience points per
session of play. That allows them to grow and develop
quite quickly over the weeks and months of active play. A
multigenerational game can last much longer. However,
no such game will actually last as long as the game time it
represents. Thus, characters only gain large amounts of
experience points while used in play. The Storyteller
should decide how long he wants each period of “downtime” to represent. Five to 10 years is probably best for most
series. During each period of downtime, characters should
gain one dot in Attributes (or Willpower), three dots in
Abilities (or Virtues) and two dots in Backgrounds. Essence should only increase one dot per three time periods
at most. Characters should gain one or two Charms per
time period and may develop one Combo per period. The
Storyteller is free to increase these amounts, ifhe wants the
characters to advance more quickly, or slow the rate of
increase, if his time units are shorter in length.

Wuat About CHILDREN?

For a multigenerational game to work, the original
characters must either die or be replaced by their offspring.
Whether a character actually has children could be determined by a random roll or through mutual agreement
between player and Storyteller. Random rolls, while arbitrary, do come close to simulating the biological crapshoot
that is reproduction. On the other hand, the Dynasts are
a superior breed and may well not encounter the same

### Warfare


The Realm maintains its authority through force of
arms. That’s always been the case. Indeed, the Scarlet
Empress gained her position through her superior strategic
acumen — a fact not lost on the Realm’s many enemies.
War is difficult to roleplay effectively. Real wars are often
long and painful marching campaigns, punctuated by
short bursts of conflict in which single individuals often
have little importance. The secret is to focus in on those
interesting moments and leave the boring bits for exposition and background. That’s not to say that you should
ignore sieges and supply lines and other important elements of warfare. The Storyteller must portray them in a
compelling fashion, such as when an invasion's success is
put at risk by weak supply lines or when decisive action is
required to breach a city’s walls.

At its most basic, this means understanding the war:

### Eras and J Hemes


The history of the Realm can be divided into
several distinct eras, each one of which suggests
a theme or themes for multigenerational series.

The Great Contagion: The early days of the
Realm see the deaths of many and the end of the
First Age. Series set during this dark time focus
on the fight against the Realm’s enemies in its
time of greatest weakness and on protecting the
posterity of the First Age.

The Rebuilding: Immediately after the Contagion, the Scarlet Empress fights against pirates
and barbarians and expands the Realm’s borders.
Series during this time focus on reconquest and
the growth of the empire. The Realm’s institutions and Great Houses wax powerful during this
time, making it perfect for political series as well.
There are several major wars —the rise of Bagrash
Kél, the insurrection of House Manosque and the
attempted coup by House Iselsi, to name a few.
These caused the Empress to refine her view of the
Realm and to establish the modern, stable system.

The Height: The Realm rules almost all of
Creation and is unchallenged by its enemies.
During this time, history comes to a standstill,
with the Blessed Isle being at the center of an
imperturbable universe. Series set during the
empire’s height focus on internal affairs and
intrigues, as the Realm becomes introspective
and inward looking.

Are the insurgents minions of the Deathlords? Is the
