the South — or anywhere in-between. Terrain has a
profound effect on the course ofa war. Wars fought far from
major supply centers become difficult to prosecute effectively. Wars fought in territories where the Realm has few
trustworthy allies are likewise troublesome.

WHO ARE THE CHARACTERS, AND WHO Do Tuey SERVE?

The Dragon-Blooded are the elite of the Realm. They
are its generals and its leaders. While the characters will
never serve as foot soldiers, they may be sent to a far-off
satrapy to act as advisers for the local troops. In such a
situation, they'd be involved in the war more peripherally
than if they were sent in as commandos to raid a rebel
stronghold. If they were the general staff of an army, the
nature of the series would differ as well. Consider carefully the
role the characters will play in the story. Are they war leaders
sent by the Realm’s central government, or do they answer
to the local satrap or advise a local tributary lord? Are they an
elite unit of hardened warriors or masters of the legions?

Warfare in the Second Age is both similar to and
different from the wars of our own world. Although the
Dragon-Blooded have ready access to Charms and other
magical effects, these things do not give them the command over the battlefield that modern technology does.
The “fog of war” is still a very real factor in the Realm’s
conflicts. Consequently, intelligence gathering is vital —
and what information does reach the government is often
incorrect. Troop movements and the position of fortificationsaren'talways readily available, even tothe Terrestrials.
They must fight without the benefit of exact knowledge,
relying on skill and discipline as well as planning to
achieve their goals. In such a situation, there is great
opportunity for the characters to become personally involved, as either scouts or the advance party of their army.

Logistics is another factor to consider. Moving an army
from one part of the Realm to another takes time and effort.
Along the way, the characters must secure food and water
and protect their soldiers from disease. Hostile local spirits
and harsh environmental conditions can also give the army
serious difficulties if they're not careful. Even after an army
has managed to trek across hundreds of miles, it still has to
keep itself provisioned forthe months this conflict will
probably last. That means:forging relationships with the
locals, using them to supply and outfit the troops. If the ant
is lucky, the locals do thiswillingly. After all ;thesoldiers
here to protect them, right er; in-many: ft

<!--PAGE 285-->
understand why war really is hell. Characters with high
Charisma and Manipulation will find their Abilities put
sorely to the test. Having superb Presence or Bureaucracy
might come in handy as well, especially if you've spent your
budget and the governor is a small-minded man who resents
the idea that he can’t handle alittle insurrection on his own.
Even then, the characters may find the locals taking advantage of their need for supplies, all the while laughing at the
characters’ unfortunate situation.

Stories about warfare needn’t be just about fighting.
Wars are about politics and ideology and resources. As
exciting as kicking butt may be, it'll grow stale without
other options. The key to making warfare dramatically
engaging is context. What else must the characters contend with in order to achieve their goal? The history of war
is replete with examples of victories and defeats that
hinged on things other than skill at arms. Perhaps the All-
Seeing Eye is undermining morale looking for an alleged
traitor. Maybe some of the troops have gone looting or
raping in friendly territory and caused an uproar. An
ambitious young officer might be making a play for command by spreading false rumors about his superiors.
Problems like these crop up in every war; the same should
be true in a Dragon-Blooded series.


### Exploration


Asnoted at the beginning of this chapter, the Dragon-
Blooded see themselves as Princes of the Earth and its due
defenders. They do what is necessary to preserve the safety
of the Realm and its inhabitants. In some cases, that means
participating in wars, but that’s not the only way they
demonstrate their authority. The Terrestrial Exalted also
travel about Creation, seeking out threats and expanding
the Realm’s knowledge of the First Age. In addition,
renewed aggression by the Fair Folk and the reappearance
of the Celestial Exalted have slowed the reclamation of
past knowledge. The Scarlet Empress (and now the Regent) didn’t like being kept in the dark. Thus, exploration
can form the basis for many Dragon-Bloaded stories.

In the context of the Dragon-Blooded, exploration is
generally of two sorts. The first and most straightforward is

in seeking out ruins and lore of the First Age for the benefit...
of the ‘Realm. From their perspective, the Terrestrial Ex--.. ~

Looking for ancient ruins with their caches of First Age
wonders is a lot like an Indiana Jones movie: one part
exploration and two parts adventure story. Enemies abound
who would love to obtain ancient magical weapons to use
against the Realm. Even so, you should never lose sight of
the exploratory nature of these stories. That means developing plausible ruined complexes with reasons to exist —~ as
well as reasons for their death traps and guardians. Create
ruins like you would a character, with “motivations” and
“goals.” What was the purpose of the ruin before it fell into
disrepair? How did it survive? More importantly, why does
it still contain all its magical goodies after millennia? Answering these questions makes for a fun locale in which to
place your adventure story. Without answers, exploration
becomesan exercise in the absurd, which isexactly what you
want to avoid in such high-stakes stories.

The second sort of exploration primarily involves investigation the unknown. The Terrestrial Exalted serve the
Realm ina variety of ways. Because the empire has few overt
rivals, its enemies must work in the shadows, hiding behind
the appearance of peace tostrike against it. Even the greatest
Charms don’t allow the Dragon-Blooded to keep an eye on
every corner of the Realm. Reporting to the All-Seeing Eye
and the magistrates, roving brotherhoods of the Chosen
keep order in its far-flung satrapies. Part special-ops teams,
part grand inquisitors, these sworn brotherhoods visit cities,
villages and monasteries for signs of dissension and collusion
with the enemy. Investigating the unknown isn’t simply
entering musty tombs and ancient ruins.

Stories about this sort of exploration hinge on the
interplay between the security the Dragon-Blooded provide and the price of that security. The Terrestrial Exalted
are forces of unbridled imperialism. They brook no challengers to the Realm’s authority and mete out harsh
justice upon those who dare to do so. Even their most
ardent defenders find them a grim necessity rather than
a desirable option. Their exploration will run afoul of
many of the same problems encountered by imperial
troops in the field: local suspicion, official antagonism
and harsh environments. Play up how the characters do
an.essential job — uncovering hidden enemies — that
many, people just don’t like to think about.


### Board Ng School Dramas


The idea of using boarding school dramas as the basis
i gseries seems bizarre. Of course, it’ 5 'sprecisely

attend one of the many schools that exist on the Blessed
Isle. Here, they spend their time in rigorous physical and
mental studies, preparing themselves for the possibility of
their Exaltation. While there, they make friends and
enemies that will last them a lifetime. They also learn the
rudiments of the sophisticated society they'll one day
inhabit. A boarding school is its own little world, a
microcosm of the society that gave birth to it. Within its
confines, you can easily run almost any type of story you
wish — provided its nature is adjusted to the realities of the
school. A war story could involve a conflict between
dormitories (though not necessarily a violent one), while
exploration could take the form ofa search for information
about the stern headmastet’s past.

The primary problem with boarding school dramas is
that they assume the characters to be children. Of course,
many players enjoy the chance to try new things. Given
the central role of eee schools in Dragon: Blood’

addition to many Dragon-Blooded series.

Itcan be challenging to set an entire series set within
a boarding school. The character creation rules included

Many — if not most — children at a boarding school won't

the basis of the teams involved. Teachers at the school are
usually associated with a particular dormitory (often the one
in which they enrolled when they were in school), creating
the possibility for rivalries among the faculty. These rivalries
also spill over into Dragon-Blooded politics, as alumni of the
“House of Resplendent Wisdom” may retain a lifelong
opposition to those of the “House of Lambent Flame.”
Stories within the boarding school series will center
on extraordinary events during the course of the school
term. No one finds the idea of roleplaying geography class
interesting, so there’s no need to pay too much attention
to these details, unless they have some greater significance. Major events, such as house competitions, mysterious
visitors, family problems and the acquisition of forbidden
knowledge are fitting subjects for stories. You should gloss
over the dull and dreary elements of school. In most cases,
this means focusing on the social world of the boarding
school —the budding friendships, rivalries and mentorships
— thar allow the players to flesh out their characters.
While examinations can be a source of personal tension,
it’s best to avoid making them the focus of dramatic
tension. Mundane school events can be handled through
simple exposition or even a well-chosen die roll.
In the end, the boarding school drama can serveasa “dry
n” for players just entering the larger world of the Dragon-
Blooded. Intrigue, social interaction and ideals all mingle
ie Halls of the school. The Storyreller can more easily

control with easy answers for every difficult question. Even
so, there are several perspectives within the religion, depending on the Dragon one has chosen to emulate. There
are, thus, at least five major schools of the Immaculate
Philosophy, as well as numerous sub-schools. This means
there’s plenty of room for individual interpretation and
debate — the perfect opportunity for exciting stories.

The Immaculate Order isn’t the only religion — just
the most powerful. The Hundred Gods Heresy flourishes
in Threshold communities and even in the countryside of
the Blessed Isle — places where contact with the spirit
world is more common. The cults of the Celestial Gods
likewise survive, hiding from the All-Seeing Eye and other
agents of the Realm. Beyond that, the Cult of the Illuminated contends with the Immaculate Order in an effort to
insinuate itself within the fabric of the Scarlet Empire.

The Second Age is a time ripe with religious fervor,
both cynical and sincere. The same can be true of stories that
concern religious faith. The former is represented by the
Realm’s use of the Immaculate Order to suppress dissent
against its rule and to nip support for the returned Anathema in the bud. Characters who adopt a similar view might
become agents of the Order, fighting against atavistic beliefs
that threaten the order the Dragon-Blooded have established. Stories of this sort become basically an extension of
intrigue- or exploration-based tales. It’s the motivations
behind the characters’ actions that differ — in this case, to
establish the ascendancy of the Realm’s state religion.

<!--PAGE 289-->
A moresincere approach treats religion as more than just
another component inan effort to keep public order. Religion
inspires human beings to a wide variety of actions, many of
them contradictory. Everything from pacifism to extreme
violence has its origin in faith, often in the same faith. Many
Terrestrials who encounter beliefs that run against the Immaculate Order's easy answers and ironclad morality may
react in a hostile fashion. A few might call their own beliefs
into question, especially the new religion offers genuine
insights they'd never considered. Even more interesting
might bea story where a religious teaching or prophecy seems
to place one of the characters in the role of a prophet or
messiah. That’s bound to cause consternation and confusion,
even among the normally unflappable Dragon-Blooded.


### Bringing It All Together


There’s no reason your Dragon-Blooded series needs
to be about any single kind of story. In fact, most series
probably switch back and forth, based on the whims and
interests of the players and the Storyteller. Begin by asking
yourself a few simple questions:

® Who are the characters in your series? What are
their goals and ideals?

® Who are the players in your series? What are
their interests?

® What themes and moods do you want to evoke
and why?

Answering these questions will take you a long way
toward understanding how to compose the various elements of a Dragon-Blooded series.

"THE CHARACTERS

Youcan leam alotaboutacharacter even from raw game
mechanical information. Does she have high Physical Attributes? [s there an emphasis on Mental Abilities? Does she
have lots of Backgrounds that tie her to the Threshold? What
kinds of Charms did she select? Simply looking over a
character sheet can give a Storyteller the basic insights he
needs in planning his series. A character with lots of Abilities,
forexample, will probably do best in stories that stress combat
and physical confrontations over problem solving; while one

with.a’ five: dot Connection to the Thousand Scales is -

Beyond raw numbers, characters possess: personalities

making them an integral part of the story. All the best
storytelling does thar, and so should yours.

"THE PLAYERS

Equally important to consider are your players. What
do they want out of the series? What are their interests? In
some respects, this is even more important to consider,
since players sometimes create characters that don’t accurately reflect what they really want out of a game. For
example, a player might create a socially adept character
with lots of Contacts and Influence, but you know that he’s
not very comfortable with that sort of game. In fact, his
favorite films are action movies, and he hates the Conan
novels because “there’s too much talking.” If that’s the
case, it’s your duty as Storyteller to help bridge the gap in
this seeming conflict. How do you create a series that’s
both true to the character and enjoyable to the player?

Another option is even more radical: Discuss the type
of game the players would like before they create characters.
The benefits of this are twofold. One, the Storyteller can
better ensure that the stories he creates are to the players’
liking. Two, the players can better ensure that their characters suit the stories they'd like to play. If, for example, the
players want to play a Second Age version of The Seven
Samurai in the Threshold, with a sworn brotherhood of
Terrestrials defending a village against the depredations of
a bandit gang, it'd be best if the characters possess the
appropriate combinations of Abilities to succeed. Any
characters in that game better be able to fight. It’s a
straightforward approach, but many players and Storytellers
overlook it and just make up a “generic” game and try to run
with whatever characters the players made up at home.
Sometimes, it works, but mostly, it just seems lame.

EVOKING THEMES AND Moops

Dragon-Blooded stories can run the gamut from wild
melees in exotic lands to brooding plots in the halls of
power to introspective meditation on the meaning of
existence. None of these types of stories necessarily excludes another. It’s perfectly possible to use each and every
one of them in your games. Which ones you do use
ultimately depends on what your series is about and why.

‘Before you.can‘choose from the many elements available,

you need to know what you hope to get out: of Exalted and
why. What kinds ries interest you and your players.

<!--PAGE 290-->

You don’t have to have all the details worked out in
advance. After all, good series are interplays between
Storytellers and players, and there’s no way to plan for
every eventuality. That's not an excuse to give little or no
thought to your stories. If you at least know what aspects
and themes of the Second Age you want to explore, you
can improvise and still maintain feel and mood.


### Alternate Uses for This Material


While the focus of this chapter is on series revolving
around Terrestrial Exalted in the service of the Realm,
that’s not the only option. The Dragon-Blooded are not
confined to the Blessed Isle. Colonies and bloodlines of
these Chosen exist elsewhere. Players and Storytellers
alike may see potential in games using the Dragon-Blooded
outside the confines of the Realm. The following is a brief
overview ofsome of these options and how they differ from
the “standard” approach offered in this chapter.


### Lookshy


In some respects, the military republic of Lookshy is
the Realm in miniature. Founded by a breakaway unit of
Old Realm soldiers, it is now ruled by Dragon-Blooded
nobles and their families, much like the Realm itself.
Lookshy holds to many of the same virtues as the Realm —
discipline, integrity, honesty and bravery — while remaining politically independent from it. Stories set within this

locale are not all that different from those within the
Realm. Intrigue, family matters and warfare are all common elements of Lookshy life.

Unlike the Realm, however, Lookshy is not doctrinaire
in its politics. Neither does it have an extremist Immaculate
cult. [ts leaders have long been willing to deal with outcastes,
sometimes intermarrying with them. Consequently, it’s a
good locale for the reborn Solar Exalted, which adds an
interesting plot element absent from the Realm. As the
Realm becomes more militant in its efforts to crush the
Solars, Lookshy will be a source of contention. Stories here
will concern maintaining Lookshy’s independence and
forging alliances with other states of the Scavenger Lands.
It’s a good place for players who want all the glory and honor
of the Realm without the stifling hierarchy that goes with it.
Lookshy is “the Realm lite” with a dash of rebellion thrown
in. It is ancient Sparta to the Realm’s Byzantine Empire. ~


### Northern Colonies


There are Dragon- Blooded in small numbers throughout the Northern reaches of Creation. Settlements along
the coastal strip are home to displaced Terrestrial Exalted,
either as rulers or advisors. It's possible to set Dragon-
Blooded stories here as well, although they'll differ greatly
from those in more civilized locales.

The Northern colonies are good for series in which
the players and the Storyteller want a gritty, on-the-edge

<!--PAGE 291-->
feel without having to reduce the characters’ power. As
Exalted, Northern Dragon-Bloods are still head and shoulders above mere mortals, but they lack the resources and
equipment of their more sophisticated brethren. Stories of
this sort will probably focus on physical struggles against
the elements and enemies or on simply surviving without
the sorcerous aids common to most Terrestrial Exalted.


### Outcastes


The Realm doesn’t like to admit it, but there are outcast
Dragon-Blooded almost everywhere. Whether they were
exiled or left of their own accord, Terrestrial Exalted have
traveled to every corner of Creation. Frequently, they interbreed with the local population. Eventually, their blood will
run true and one of their descendants will receive the Second
Breath, becoming one of the Exalted. In other cases, small
numbers of Dragon-Blooded were separated from the Realm
centuries ago and must now fend for themselves amid the
poverty and ignorance of the world beyond.

Stories with outcastes have a lot in common with those
set in the North. Unlike them, the themes will differ greatly.
Dragon-Blooded who experience Exaltation in the Threshold may find themselves the objects of fear and revulsion.
Everything they once knew is called into question, and
everyone looks at them differently. These new Chosen may
become alienated from their former lives, cast out by the
suspicions of the simple. Outcaste stories can be about coming
to grips with Exaltation, reclaiming lost heritage and finding
a place for oneself. These stories dovetail nicely with family
matters and religious stories, although the wandering of
outcastes could easily throw them into almost any kind of
story -— their rootlessness being an advantage in this case.

Mixep GAMES

The Exalted core rulebook assumes that the characters are Solar Exalted, reborn after centuries away from
Creation. In a Dragon-Blooded game, the Solars are the
Anathema, the enemy the Terrestrial Exalted overthrew
to save the Realm. Inevitably, these two perspectives will
clash. How do you handle that clash, especially if one or
more; re players ‘has the desire to play Exalted from different

thisideological divide? Is it possible'to run a game «
mixed groups of Solar and Terrestrial Exalted? Let's:

neither perspective is whole the truth, there is room for
mixed Circles. In places such as Lookshy, for example, the
Dragon-Blooded might cooperate with the Solars for mutual advantage. Within the Realm itself, this would be rarer
~~ very probably nonexistent. The Storyteller can certainly
create unusuat circumstances that would allow for it (such
as a secret mission sanctioned by a Dynast or high-level
general), but these are the exception rather than the mule.


### Balancing Act


Mixed Circles will likely be rare for another reason:
The Solars outstrip the Terrestrial Exalted in terms of
power. If the concern about the power differential is overcome, how do you deal with this imbalance? There’s no easy
game mechanical way to correct this. Instead, you should
rely on in-game solutions. For example, a Solar Exalted
cannot operate freely within the Realm, while a Dragon-
Blood can. This limits the Solar’s ability to use his greater
powers without invalidatingthem. Thus, the Dragon-Blood’s
weakness seems less so because of the context in which the
story takes place. Another approach is to ensure that each
character gets equal “on screen” time. A Solar Exalted may
be more powerful in game terms, but is she more important
in story terms? There's no reason this need be the case,
especially if your stories involve problems not easily reducible to questions of power. So long as you work to keep each
character type in spotlight, you're doing fine.


### Storyteller Characters


As Storyteller characters, the Solar Exalted are more
flexible than the Terrestrials. How you approach them
depends on the themes and stories you wish to emphasize.
There’s no question the Solars are considered enemies of the
Realm, but how accurate is that position? Are the Solars
simply demons in human form without any redeeming
qualities? If so, Dragon-Blooded characters should feel no
remorse about cutting them down in the name of the Realm.
What if they are evil bucnot through any fault of their own?
What if the Exaltation they experience is a curse that
inexorably leads them down the path of madness? Dragon-

“Bloods may still be expected to destroy them, but such
“battles will be tinged with sadness— the realization that the

Solats-are as much. victims as villains. It’s also possible the
Solarsa are re aye nging heroes, retumed to wreak divine justice

<!--PAGE 292-->
depth in their villains. Choose a portrayal that suits your
story and your players.

Another valid approach is to ignore the Solar Exalted
entirely. In many Dragon-Blooded stories, they're unnecessary. The Realm isfilled with many opportunities foradventure
that have nothing to do with the Solars at all. Interecine
conflict, religious debates and border clashes happen all the
time — usually without the involvement of the Solars. You
should never feel compelled to include any element of the
setting that doesn’t serve your purpose. If the addition of the
Solars would upset the balance of your carefully laid series,
don’t include them. It’s just as reasonable to exclude them as
it is to leave out the Guild or the Threshold or any other
element for which you have no use. Players usually introduce
enough complications into stories on their own without the
Storyteller’s helping them. If the Solar Exalted would be
another such complication, leave them be.


### Character Sheet Pruning


Using Solar Exalted as Storyteller characters
can be frustrating and time consuming. Compared to the Dragon-Blooded, they have many
more abilities and Charms. To avoid turning your
story into a bookkeeping nightmare, it’s recommended youconcentrate on the basics: Attributes,
highest Abilities and important Charms. Doing
anything more than this — except for important
Storyteller characters — is probably unnecessary.
In some cases, you might even want to reduce
Attributes or Abilities down to three categories
each (Physical, Social and Mental Attributes, for
example) rather than complete tallies. That's
only recommended for very powerful Solars who
have many skills and powers. For Charms, a
similar approach is encouraged. Ignore the full
listings for any but the most important Charms.
Since you're the Storyteller, you don’t have to
worry about whether the character has all the
prerequisites and so forth. If he’s gonna use Flow
Like Blood, don’t worry about if he has Shadow
Over Water. A character sheet should be a shorthand device to jog your memory, not a maze of
numbers to confuse you.

## Provenance

- **Source:** `sources/Exalt Books/Exalted - The Dragon-Blooded.pdf`, 297 pdf pages.
  A pure scan — `Acrobat 4.05 Scan Plug-in`, no text layer at all, so `pdftotext`
  returns nothing on every page.
- **Raw extraction:** `images/_extracted/DragonBlooded.md`, produced by
  `tools/ocr_scan_book.py` at 300 dpi, offset `-1` (printed = pdf - 1). That file is
  authoritative and unmodified.
- **Offset verified** against a known citation: Air Dragon Style prints 243
  (`martial_arts_styles.json`) and OCRs on pdf page 244.
- **Transforms applied here** (readability only, no numeral altered): letterspaced
  small-caps headings rejoined; line-break hyphens closed; jammed stat keys
  (`MinimumEssence` → `Minimum Essence`) spaced; stray apostrophe spacing closed.
- **No tables have been hand-recovered on this book.** A boxed table that OCR
  scrambled is still scrambled here; render the page and read it.

⚠ **Charm-tree diagrams are image-only** and this extraction cannot see them as
structure. Treat any Charm prerequisite graph here as incomplete.
